Middlesex Cases, before Mr. Justice Gaselee.
1443. DANIEL HOLLAND, ANN EDWARDS, MARTHA HILL, and SARAH SMITH, were indicted for feloniously assaulting Alexander Randall, on the 17th of August, on the King's highway, putting him in fear, and taking from his person and against his will, twenty-five sovereigns, his monies.
ALEXANDER RANDALL. I am a lighterman and live in London-street, Limehouse. On the 17th of August, I received sixty sovereigns; I paid away thirty-four or thirty-five, and had the rest in a Scotch plaid bag, in my pocket; I was a little fresh in liquor, but perfectly sensible. On leaving the East India-house I went along Whitechapel, and bought two neats' feet and a calf's foot, which I put into a bundle handkerchief, and carried in my hand; I walked down Church-lane, and in turning the corner into Cable-street, my hat was taken from my head by a woman - it was about two o'clock in the afternoon; I called out "You hussy, bring me my hat;" I had never seen her before - she said nothing - I followed her up Cable-court - she went into a house after turning a corner - I went to the door and demanded my hat at the door, which was open - three women seized me by the collar instantly, and forced me into the house - I held the side of the door-post with my left-hand, having my bundle in my right, but was overpowered; they dragged me in with all their force, pulled me into a room, and there was Holland; he instantly started off his chair and joined them - the bundle was torn in pieces before I gave it up - they began to pull me about in a violent manner - I made to the door and tried to force myself out, but could not - I was knocked down on the floor by Holland and the three women (whom were the prisoners) - all knocked me down and stripped my arm out of my left sleeve, by my resistance to get from them; I begged of them to let me out, saying, "I am robbed of my money;" I had felt my bag go out of my pocket - they were all round me at the time; I begged for my life, saying, I had a large family depending on me; I was knocked down again, and thought my head was totally split - I was completely speechless; after that they dragged me into a place behind the house, called the waste ground, and left me there speechless and senseless - I felt myself being dragged out; I was left there, on a ruinous place of bricks and tiles, till I was conveyed to the watch-house - when I found myself there. I think they said it was between three and four o'clock, when I came to my senses. I am sure my bag and money were safe when I was dragged into the house. I had made a purchase just before - I saw my bundle at the watch-house, with the handkerchief, and neat's feet in it. I am certain of the prisoners' persons - all of them. Holland had a hairy cap on, but in the course of the scuffle he appeared in a red cap, which he had on under the hairy one - I cannot swear which of the women took my hat.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Who had you drank with? A. A man named Farrier, in Little-Bell-alley, about half-past eleven o'clock. I drank part of two bottles of ale with him and another - I had a glass of gin and water; that is all I had that day; it was called Scotch ale - it made me a little fresh. I did not see three women till I got to the house - I was dragged in instantly,and was no sooner in than down - I resisted being dragged in at the door.
Q. Why not call for assistance? A. It is nothing but a den of thieves - I have discovered that since; it is about thirty yards from the main street - I could not have been heard - I was not half a minute at the door, before I was pulled in. I was terrified, but not so much as some people might be - I held the door-post as long as I could; I had no opportunity of seeing either of the prisoners, after I was pulled down; I am positive to all their persons.
COURT. Q. Had you an opportunity of seeing them, while they were pulling you? A. I had - several times.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. After drinking with the person, did you call elsewhere? A. Yes, at three places - but had nothing to drink.
Prisoner HOLLAND. When the handkerchief was at the office, it was not torn. Witness. I produced it at the office torn in this state.
CATHERINE KAIL. I live in Cable-place, Cable-street; this place is called Cable-tier, or Long-alley - it is six or seven yards from the alley, to the top of Cable-court. On the 17th of August, near four o'clock in the afternoon, I saw Holland, and three girls going down the court - I knew two of them - they came out of Cable-tier - I saw no more of them after they went out to the main street. I saw Smith, who goes by the name of Kit Lips and Hall, who goes by the name of Davis, and another young woman, whose face I did not see; I heard Holland say to them, "Get on, or go on," he used an improper word, and said, "Get on, let the **** lay;" or something of that sort. Mrs. M'Ardell, came round the corner in a quarter of an hour, or ten minutes - and in consequence of what she said, I opened my yard-door, and went out into the waste ground, and there saw Randall lying on his back - a bundle lay at the back of his head in a belcher handkerchief quite torn, with cowheels, or neat's-feet, and cucumbers - his hat was nearly off his head - he was a few yards from the door of No. 21. I have frequently seen Smith, Holland and Hill, going in and out there. I always knew Hill, by the name of Davis. Randall lay there sometimes. A man of colour fetched Jackson from the watch-house; he appeared quite insensible, there was no movement in him. I know nothing of Edwards.
Cross-examined. Q. There were cucumbers in the bundle? A. Yes. I did not open it, but saw them.
ANN KELLY. I live in Cable-court, and am married. I have seen all the prisoners, at the house where this happened - I do not know who lives in it. On the 17th of August, between two and three o'clock, I saw Edwards take Randall's hat off - she was at the top of the court. Randall was just at the end of the court, ten or twelve yards from the street; he followed her, and I saw him go into the house after his hat. I saw the three female prisoners go in at the door before him - and I saw him walk in. I stood at my door, which is three or four houses off, for more than half an hour, before I saw the three women go out of the court. I saw nobody with them - I then went by the door, and saw the prosecutor laying on his back on the ground in the room; I looked in, and saw Holland trying to lift him up - I said, "Dan, can't you lift him up?" He said, "Hold your tongue, he will get up by and by, let him be," or something of that sort. I went home, and afterwards saw Randall laying on the waste, at the back of my house; he was five or six yards from their house - and while he lay there, I saw Holland moving his things out of his own into another house. I am certain of the prisoners' persons.
Q. Did you not tell the Magistrate, you could not tell whether Hill or Smith, were two of the women? A. I do not know the names they go by - but they are the three women. I did not hear any noise in the house.
Cross-examined. Q. Randall was ten or twelve yards inside the court when his hat was taken off; not in the main street? A. No, not in the street, there was no man with the three women when they passed me - they had time to go into the main street before Holland joined them.
COURT. Q. How many rooms does the house consist of? A. I do not know - the room and street doors were open when I passed.
MARY HILL. I am married, and live in Cable-court, Cable-street. Holland lives at No. 21, Cable-court. On the 17th of August, between three and four o'clock, I went to the back of my house to take an apron in, and saw Randall laying at the bottom of the ground, near No. 21. I went to Mrs. M'Ardell, she came, and looked at him - he seemed dead. I saw Holland come out of the back door of No. 21, and drop a bundle near Randall's head - he went into one of the back doors again. I did not know Randall, and did not see further into it.
Q. Did you not take the pains to see whether he was alive or dead? A. No.
Cross-examined. Q. You live near the house, and do not know the women? A. No; No. 21 has a first floor to it, and stairs in the passage.
COURT. Q. Is the door in the middle of the house? Q. No, at the side; there is but one room down stairs, and one above.
MARY M'ARDELL. I keep a mangle in Cable-place. I and Hill went into the waste behind the house, and saw the prosecutor laying on his back, with one arm out of his coat. Holland came and put a bundle down about a yard from Randall, who was insensible. We made an alarm after Holland was gone, and the people came. I have known Holland three or four years, and I believe he lives in the court, but I do not know at what number - I have seen him lately at No. 21 - he has no family, but a parcel of women live there - the female prisoners live in the court, in different places I believe - I saw them up and down the court - I do not remember seeing Holland that day, except at that time.
Cross-examined. Q. On your making an alarm were you instantly attended to? A. Yes, persons came out of their back doors; the court is long, and goes sideways; I do not think a person calling out would be heard in the main street.
JOHN KAIL. I am eleven years old, and live with my parents in Cable-street. I know Smith; on the day this happened I saw her in the court; I was going down to the bottom of the court, and saw one of the girls take off the man's hat; he said "Give me my hat;" she said
"Come for it." I heard Smith say to the girl who hadthe hat, "Get him up." She then asked him to come for his hat, and he went up to the top of the court; the girl ran up another alley, and told him to come for it; he said he should not; she asked him again, he went up to the door - I saw some of their hands lay hold of his collar, and then he walked in.
WILLIAM SUMMERS. I am headborough of St. George's. I apprehended Hill on the 1st of September; I think in Hyam's-buildings, Blue-gate-fields; and told her I wanted her concerning a robbery committed in Cable-tier; she begged of me to take her as private as possible; I called her by the name of Davis, which she usually goes by; she said she would not go by that name, but by her right name, Hill. Next morning, about three o'clock, I apprehended Edwards, and asked her how long she had left Cable-court; she said she had left it two or three months; I took her in Blue-gate-fields; I asked the landlady, in her presence, how long she had been there; she said not more than a fortnight, if so long.
FRANCIS JACKSON. I am beadle of St. George's. On the 17th of August I went to No. 21, Cable-tier, about four o'clock in the afternoon. I found the house locked up, and shutters fastened. I went through the next house, and found Randall on the waste ground on his back, with a bundle laying by his side - he appeared quite insensible - I got assistance, and conveyed him to the watch-house, where he recovered - his coat was on then - I cannot say whether he had been beaten - he might have had some liquor - when he came to himself he complained of being very stiff - a bundle laid by him, containing neats' and calves' feet, and some cucumbers - I broke open the house, and found all the goods, except the bedstead, moved from the lower floor - I know that Hill and Holland lived there - I have often seen them in bed there - a person passing the door can see into the room if the door is open - I should think this house is about forty yards from the main street - there are noises so frequently there, that if the man had hallooed it would not be noticed - I know the other two women by their living in the court.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see Randall leave the watch-house? A. I saw him in the morning - he had recovered so as to speak; I saw no blood or bruises; he overhauled his pockets, and said my book has been changed from one pocket to the other, but there was nothing for them there; but they had taken his bag with twenty-five sovereigns.
RICHARD CARTER. I apprehended Holland in Wells-street. I saw him run through the lane, followed him into a public house, called him out, and told him I took him on suspicion of a robbery in Cable-place; he said he knew nothing of it, but he supposed it was in consequence of his former life; he had a brown short coat on, and a hairy cap.
HOLLAND'S Defence. I know nothing about it.
HILL'S Defence. Jackson says the things were taken from No. 21 - I had left there on the Monday.
HOLLAND - GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Life.
EDWARDS - GUILTY. Aged 19.
HILL - GUILTY. Aged 21.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Of stealing from the person only.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-258]
Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1482. CHARLES SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of August, a coat, value 12 s.; a waistcoat, value 5 s.; a pair of trousers, value 5 s.; a pair of boots, value 14 s.; four handkerchiefs, value 16 s.; a cap, value 1 s., and nine shillings, the property of John Rice, in his dwelling-house.
JOHN RICE. I live in Compton-place, Brunswick-square; I only lodge there, and am a shoemaker. On the 2d of August the prisoner came to inquire for work; he worked at my own room two days. On the 4th my wife and child died; I gave him sixpence, and told him to get work till after the funeral; he slept in my house on Sunday, the 7th, in the same room as me; the clothes which I wore on Sunday were in the room; next morning about five o'clock I got up; he was gone, and the clothes also; he had left his own behind; I did not see him again till the 3d of September, when he was taken at the fair with my coat and trousers on.
WILLIAM JOHNSON. I am an officer, and took the prisoner on the 5th of September, at Bartholomew Fair; Rice's son was with me; he had this coat and trousers on.
PEARCE RICE. I saw the prisoner at my father's house, and slept in the same room with him. On the 5th of September I saw him at the Fair; I and my cousin went up to him, and had him taken.
(Coat and trousers sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Of stealing only. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-259]
London Cases - First Jury.
Before Mr. Recorder.
1483. GEORGE TATE and JAMES SUMMERS were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of July, a handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of Isaiah Lukens, from his person.
ISAIAH LUKENS. On the 25th of July I was lodging at Wright's Hotel, Adelphi. I was in Fleet-street, between Chancery-lane and Temple-bar, about twelve o'clock, going home. I am sure my handkerchief was in my outside coat pocket. I did not feel it taken, but a person pulled me on the arm; I turned round and saw a gentleman, who had hold of Tate, right behind me; he produced a handkerchief, and asked if it was mine; I looked, and found my name in full upon it; we took him to Guildhall, in company with Summers, whom somebody had secured; I had not observed them near me before; Tate denied it, and said the person who took him had knocked him down, and that the handkerchief fell from my pocket.
WILLIAM HAWKINS. I am a file-cutter, and live at the Axe Inn, Aldermanbury. On the 25th of July, between twelve and one o'clock, I was coming out of the Court of Chancery, Lincoln's Inn, and coming down Chancery-lane towards Fleet-street, a gentleman pointed the prisoners out to me; they were close behind Mr. Lukens, and were in company; in Chancery-lane Tate put his hand into Mr. Lukens' pocket, and Summers came up close to him to conceal him; he did not succeed at the first, second, or third attempt; Mr. Lukens got within a fewyards of Temple-bar; I kept close behind them; Tate then drew the handkerchief out; Summers was close by his side; a gentleman; who was in my company collared Tate, and I seized Summers; the moment my friend collared Tate he dropped the handkerchief; the officer picked it up; my friend immediately told Mr. Lukens, who claimed the handkerchief; I never lost sight of them.
WILLIAM KISS. I am a constable; and received the prisoners in charge.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
TATE'S Defence. I live in Oxford-market. I was walking down Holborn and Chancery-lane; I turned towards Temple-bar; this gentleman knocked me down, and said I had picked a gentleman's pocket; I had never seen the handkerchief; and in ten minutes he brought this young man up, and said he was with me.
TATE - GUILTY. Aged 17.
SUMMERS - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18250915-260]
1484. JOHN JENKINS was indicted for a rape.
NOT GUILTY.

1485. THOMAS FURLONGER was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of July, a watch, value 20 s., and a chain, value 1 s., the goods of Margaret Furlonger, widow.
MARGARET FURLONGER. I am a widow. The prisoner is my nephew, and visited me. On the 14th of July, a little girl came to me with a note, and I gave her this watch to deliver to my daughter; I found it in possession of a pawnbroker; the chain was not on it then. I live in Great New-street. I believe the letter to be the prisoner's writing.
Cross-examined. Q. When you read it you thought it your daughter's writing? A. Yes; I have received letters from the prisoner; I did not suspect it when I read it, but it is spelt the same as letters he has written to me. I apprehended him next day, and he confessed writing it.
ANN FURLONGER. I am the prosecutrix's daughter. This letter was never written by my authority. I never received the watch or chain, and did not expect it.
ELIZABETH CAPPER (aged nine years, appeared perfectly to understand the obligation of on oath). I know the prisoner; my father used to bring him to our house - he gave me this letter, and told me to say it had come from Mrs. Furlonger, at the White Swan; and told me to take it to Mrs. Furlonger, of Great New-street - which I did; she gave me the watch - he stopped at a public-house, near the house, and I gave him the watch - he gave me nothing - next day I was asked about it, and told how I got the note.
(Letter read.)
"DEAR MOTHER, - I should be obliged if you will send Furlonger's watch; he has sent to me to get it repaired against he comes home; there is something the matter with yours - it don't go, but he will have it repaired before he brings it to you. He is very well, and sends his love - hopes this will find you well, as it leaves us at present. Please to send the watch by bearer.
ANN FURLONGER.
MRS. FURLONGER. The watch was my son's - he had mine, and I had his - he was gone to France, and we had changed watches. I thought by this letter that he wanted it back.
JAMES CLIFFORD. I apprehended the prisoner on the 14th or 15th of July - a duplicate of the watch was left at my house.
JAMES WALKER. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Aldersgate-street. I have a watch pawned on the 14th of July, in the name of Mary Furlonger, by Mary Copping, who is not here - she is ill.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Confined Six Months and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18250915-262]
NEW COURT. (6th DAY.)
Middlesex Cases, Fourth Jury, Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1486. MICHAEL CLANCY was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of July, a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of John Tilley, from his person.
JOHN TILLEY. On the 11th of July, between four and five o'clock, I was in Arlington-street - I felt a pulling behind me, turned round, and saw the prisoner, and two others, near me; but the prisoner was nearest. I took hold of him, and found my handkerchief in his hand - he was in the act of giving it to another boy.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I saw two young men following this gentleman - his handkerchief was half out of his pocket; I saw them in the act of picking his pocket; I called out to the gentleman, and they threw the handkerchief down - the gentleman turned round and took me.
JOHN TILLEY re-examined. Q. Did he say any thing of this at the time? A. No, but when I took him to the watch-house, he begged forgiveness, it being his first offence.
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Fourteen Years.

1488. WILLIAM CRICK was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of June, a whip, value 3 s. 6 d., the goods of James Tweedy.
JAMES TWEEDY. I am coachman to Mr. Brodie. The prisoner was helper in the stable, in New Burlington-mews - he was discharged just before the 27th of June; he called about that time - I went with the carriage, and left him there, about half-past eight o'clock in the evening - there was another helper in the stable; when I came back the prisoner was just gone, and I missed a whip and a hand piece. I found the whip about a week ago.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN BATHS. I live with Mr. Keats, in Albemarle-street. The prisoner lived with me as groom about four months - he sold me this whip; he said he bought it in Mary-le-bone, for 7 s.; I said it was not worth 2 s. 6 d.; I gave him 3 s. 6 d. for it.
GEORGE AVIS. I took the prisoner into custody; he said he had sold the whip, and hand pieces to Bates.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18250915-265]
1489. WILLIAM ARCHER was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of July, a pair of breeches, value 3 s., the goods James Henry Williamson.
WILLIAM JOHN LONGER. I am shopman to James Henry Williamson, a pawnbroker, of Brick-lane. These breeches are his property; I had hung them up between eight and nine o'clock in the morning of the 26th of July, and missed them when the goods were taken down. I had attended the shop that day - they were not sold.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM HAYES. I am an apprentice to Mr. Attenborough, a pawnbroker, of Crown-street, Finsbury. - About half-past four or five o'clock on the evening of the 26th of July, the prisoner brought these breeches to pawn, which he said belonged to his brother, in Long-lane - I had my suspicions, and sent for an officer; he then said he saw a man running with them, and drop them.
JAMES FITZPATRICK. I am an officer. I was sent for to take the prisoner - he said he saw a man running, who dropped these breeches; he picked them up, and thought no harm in it.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.

1490. JAMES REES was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of September, a reticule, value 2 s. 6 d.; a bunch of keys, with a ring, value 4 s.; a handkerchief, value 1 s., and a pair of gloves, value 2 d., the goods of George Shepherd, from the person of Ann, his wife.
ANN SHEPHERD. I am the wife of George Shepherd. On the 15th of September, about half-past seven o'clock in the evening, I was in the New-road - the prisoner came suddenly by me, and snatched my reticule out of my hand, which contained a bunch of keys, a handkerchief, and a pair of gloves; he left the chain in my hand: I did not see his face till he was brought back by a man, but by his figure I knew him to be the same.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me running? A. I saw the man who took it - he took off his shoe, and threw it at me, which I gave him again.
CHARLES COUSENS. The prisoner and these articles were brought to me at the watch-house, by a man, who said he saw him throw them away; the prisoner said he had done it, and he was sorry. He appeared to be in a great deal of want.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-267]
1491. HENRY PAPE was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, a handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Barnard William Moreland, from his person.
BARNARD WILLIAM MORELAND. I am a wax-chandler, and live in Oxford-street. On the 14th of August I was in Nassau-street - a person named Woodward came and asked if I had lost my handkerchief; I felt, and found it was gone - he then shewed it to me; I had had it not ten minutes before.
Prisoner. The gentleman said at the office that it was not his property. Witness. No - I expressed no doubt about it.
JOHN WOODWARD. I was in Mortimer-street - I saw the prisoner and another lad following this gentleman, for about one hundred and fifty yards - I then saw them separate suddenly; I took the prisoner, and found the handkerchief between his trousers and his jacket. When I said I would take him he threw himself down, and began to kick; I took him up, and carried him to Nassau-street, where I found the prosecutor.
Prisoner. I had found it half an hour before.
JOHN WOODWARD. I followed him all the way up Mortimer-street, and am quite sure he did not pick it up.
GUILTY. Aged 12.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-268]
1492. SAMUEL COWDEN and JOHN GRIFFIN were indicted for stealing, on the 2d July, a handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of Charles Munns, from his person.
CHARLES MUNNS. On the 28th of July, I was in Sydney's-alley. The officer came and asked me if I had had any thing taken from my pocket. I felt, and missed my handkerchief from my right-hand coat pocket. I had it about three minutes before - here it is; they had the prisoners in custody with it.
WILLIAM COLTON. About half-past eight o'clock on the evening of the 28th of July, I saw the prisoners in the courts leading from Leicester-fields to St. Martin's-lane, following a gentleman; the prosecutor came by, and they pressed up against him. I saw Cowden do something, and then put something into his breeches and go away - they then took to the left hand, and the prosecutor the right. I sent Reardon to tell the prosecutor, and took the prisoners into a public-house. I then took off Griffin's hat and found the handkerchief.
Prisoner GRIFFIN. Q. I was not in company with Cowden? A. Yes, you was for a quarter of an hour backwards and forwards through the courts. I had seen them in Long-acre before they saw the prosecutor.
DANIEL REARDON. I was with Colton. I saw the prisoners first at the end of Long-acre; they went to the end of St. Martin's-court. I then saw the prosecutor, and the two prisoners close behind him. I did not see them do any thing; but I saw them leave him at the end of the alley, and they turned off to Bear-street. I then went and told the prosecutor.
COWDEN'S Defence. I was coming from work. I saw something on the ground, and kicked it before me. I then took it up, and saw it was a handkerchief. I met this young man at the corner of Bear-street, talking to a woman - I shewed it to him, and he said he would give me a pot of beer for it.
GRIFFIN'S Defence. I live in Stephen-street, Tottenham-court-road. I met a young woman, and stood talking to her at the corner of Bear-street; the prisoner came to me, and said he had found this handkerchief, which he would sell. I said I would give him a pot of beer for it, and we were going to have it, when the officers came up and took us.
COWDEN - GUILTY. Aged 19.
GRIFFIN - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-269]
1493. JOHN ANDERSON, JOHN BENSON, and WILLIAM KEITH, were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, and a handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of James William French, from his person.
JAMES WILLIAM FRENCH. On the 12th of July, about a quarter past ten o'clock at night, I was walking in Wardour-street, a person accosted me, and said I had lost my handkerchief - the officer afterwards produced it.
ANGELIOUS BERTRAM. I saw the three prisoners in St. Martin's-court - they followed the prosecutor through Leicester-square, Princes-street, and into Wardour street. I then saw Keith take the handkerchief - he and Anderson ran into Tyler's-court. I ran and took Benson at the corner of the court - Reardon took the other two in the court. I went into the public-house, and saw the handkerchief in Reardon's hand.
DANIEL REARDON. I was with Bertram. I saw the three prisoners together for about ten minutes, or a quarter of an hour; they followed the prosecutor a little distance, and when they got near Tyler's-court, I saw Keith and Anderson rush into the court. I took Keith, and then Anderson, and the moment I took Anderson, I saw this handkerchief drop - Benson was at the end of the court.
Prisoner BENSON. Q. How long was I with the other prisoners? A. About ten minutes.
ANDERSON'S Defence. I was going to Mr. Jones, in Wardour-street for work. I met Keith, and a man came and took us.
BENSON'S Defence. I was going along Wardour-street, and met Keith. I stood and spoke to him a minute or two, and was going away, when I was taken, on the contrary side of the way to the other two.
KEITH'S Defence. I was walking down Wardour-street - the man took me and threw me on the ground.
ANDERSON - GUILTY. Aged 15.
KEITH - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
BENSON - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-270]
1494. ELIZABETH CARTER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of September, a necklace, value 3 l., the goods of Susan Allen, from the person of Harriet Low .
SUSAN ALLEN. I sent my niece, Harriet Low, (who is five years of age) out on last Friday morning, about nine o'clock, with a necklace on her neck; she came back in about half an hour without it - here it is - but it has been broken in pieces.
WILLIAM JONES. I am an officer. I took a part of the necklace from the prisoner's bosom, in Mr. Sewel's shop, at Kingsland, about half-past nine o'clock; the other part was brought by a witness. The prisoner said, they were given her by another girl, who had picked them up in the road.
JAMES WARBOYS. I am a gardener, and live at Dalston. Last Friday morning I saw the little girl crying - I went up to her; she said the prisoner, and another, had ran away with her beads. I pursued them towards London-field - they got out of my sight for about five minutes; I then got sight of them again - they were still running - I came up with the prisoner, and took her to Mr. Sewel's shop; the moment I touched her, she dropped part of the beads on the ground.
Prisoner's Defence. I do not know the girl who picked them up - she said she had found them; and gave them to me.
The prisoner received an excellent character.
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1 s., and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18250915-271]
1495. NOEL CHAPMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of September, three scent bottles, value 25 s., the goods of Thomas James Rear; and JAMES CARTER was indicted, for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
THOMAS JAMES REAR. I am a chemist and druggist, and live in Sloane-street. I was in my shop, about nine o'clock in the evening, on the ninth of September; the prisoners and several other persons were there; they left the shop, and I missed some bottles; Gilbert, and my apprentice, were the only persons there then - I had been shewing them to a lady about eight o'clock.
GEORGE VAUGHAN. I am apprentice to Mr. Rear. On the 9th of September, I saw Chapman in my master's shop; he asked for a penny-worth of liquorice, with which I served him, and he went away. I did not miss any thing till Mr. Rear told me of it.
Cross-examined. Q. Were not several other persons there? A. Yes; the prisoners were the last persons who left the shop, and five minutes after they were gone, I heard of the loss.
JAMES GILBERT. I am a musician in the Blues. On the night of the 9th of September, I was in the shop, and saw the prisoner Chapman come in.
EDWARD PERKINS. I am assistant to Mr. Perkins, a pawnbroker. On the morning of the 10th of September, Carter came to the shop, and offered these two bottles in pawn; he said, he brought them from a man. I detained him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not go out to look for the person? A. Yes; he soon returned, and said, he was gone.
RICHARD MAYBANK. I am the officer - I went to Perkins' shop, and took Carter. He said they were given to him by a short man, and he was waiting for him - he did not know his name; I afterwards took Chapman from information. He said, he had not been at Mr. Rear's shop - but the apprentice and Gilbert, identified him.
CARTER'S Defence. As I was coming out of the Coach and Horses public-house, I met a man at the corner of George-street, who asked me to take them to pawn, and he would give me a pint of beer.
JOHN LONG. I am a labourer, and live at Chelsea. I have known Carter seven or eight years. I was at the pawnbroker's shop and saw him there - he asked for his ticket - they sent him to find the man; he returned in about a minute, and could not find him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-272]

1497. THOMAS GALLOWAY was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of July, four loaves of bread, value 1 s. 9 d., the goods of James Tibby.
WILLIAM GOMM. I am in the employ of Mr. James Tibby, a baker, who lives in Robert-street. On the 20th of July I left a basket of bread in Doughty-street,while I went into Henry-street - as I returned, I met the prisoner with another about the end of Doughty-street, with four loaves under his arm - I took the prisoner, the other ran away - I am sure the loaves were my master's.
JOSEPH CADBY. I received charge of the prisoner and bread - he gave no account of it then, but before the Magistrate, he said, he was very hungry.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a man with these loaves, who asked me to carry them for him - he ran away and the man took me.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18250915-274]
1498. MARY ANTHONY was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, a coat, value 8 s., the goods of Patrick Carmoady.
PATRICK CARMOADY. I lost my coat on Monday, the 12th of September, from the tester of my bed - it was safe about ten o'clock, when I went to the Session House; I came back and said, I would take the prisoner to Bow-street - she said, "Do not take me, and I will tell."
MARY CARMOADY. I am the prosecutor's wife. I saw the coat at twelve o'clock that day, when the prisoner, who lives in the kitchen, took it to brush - I was in bed at the time, and thought she was going to take it to my husband - when he came home from his beat in the morning, she said she had pawned it at the corner of Blackmore-street, and torn the ticket.
WILLIAM MAINWARING FENNER. I am shopman to a pawnbroker at the corner of Blackmore-street. I never saw the prisoner in our shop, nor is this article entered in our book.
GUILTY . Aged 17.
Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18250915-275]
1499. STEPHEN HARMER and WILLIAM CARTER was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of June, five quarts of gin, value 10 s.; two quarts of rum, value 7 s., and ten bottles, value 10 d., the goods of John Craddock Bettridge.
MR. CRESWELL conducted the prosecution.
JOHN CRADDOCK BETTRIDGE. I am a victualler, and live in Ratcliff-highway. Harmer was in my employ, and Carter was there occasionally - in consequence of information, I went into my wash-house, and found some bottles which had recently had spirits in them - I left them there, and on the morning of the 29th, I saw Harmer and my son go down stairs - I followed them, and saw Harmer go back to the wash-house - I then heard Carter ask my son where Stephen was; he said, he was out backwards - I looked out at a window, and saw the two prisoners go out; I went down and looked about, and found a bottle of gin in the copper-hole - I went and asked my son, if Carter was gone - Carter heard me, and said, No, and asked me for a glass of gin, which I served him with - I then went and asked Harmer what was the cause of the bottle of gin being in the copper-hole - he said, he was very sorry for what he had done, but he had admitted Carter to sleep there, in consequence of his being locked out from home, and that Carter had gone into the bar, and drawn the spirits. I asked him if he knew of any more being concealed, he declared, he did not - but I looked about, and under some water-butts, and some saw-dust, I found six bottles more of gin, and three of rum - Harmer was present, and declared, in the most positive terms, that he knew not how they got there.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. All you know of Carter is, that he went into the wash-house; and then called for a glass of gin? A. Yes, and all the rest I heard from the other prisoner in Carter's absence - I believe I had other lodgers in the house - Harmer and my son slept together, but my son is not here - I perhaps might have played at skittles with one of the prisoners for a pot of beer.
MR. CRESWELL. Q. How many bottles did you put there to wash? A. I suppose about thirty - I suppose ten or twelve were left.
WILLIAM SUMMERS. I am an officer. I took Harmer into custody on the 29th of June - he said, he was very sorry, but he had admitted Carter to be in the house two nights - that his master was very good, and he was extremely sorry for what he had done - Carter was taken in about a fortnight.
FRANCIS JACKSON. I am an officer. I received charge of Carter, at the watch-house, on the 21st of July - he said, he knew nothing at all about the gin - I do not recollect his saying any thing further.
HARMER'S Defence. When I go to bed, I cannot come down, except one of the family gets up, because the door is fastened.
CARTER'S Defence. I am quite innocent - I have used the house as a customer, and I am very sorry I did, for that has brought me here.
Two witnesses who gave Harmer a good character, engaged to find him employment.
HARMER - GUILTY. Aged 21.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1 s., and Discharged.
CARTER - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-276]
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1467. LOUISA LLOYD was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, a hat, value 5 s.; two sheets, value 5 s.; a pillow-case, value 4 d.; two shirts, value 5 s.; a shift, value 2 s.; a cap, value 1 s.; a napkin, value 1 s.; a gown, value 3 s.; a set of bed curtains, value 3 s.; a pair of shoes, value 1 s., and a flat iron, value 6 d., the goods of William Billing.
ANN BILLING. My husband's name is William Billing - we live in Ray-street, Clerkenwell. The prisoner lodged in my house for about two months. On the 27th of August I missed an umbrella; I asked her about it; she at first denied it, but afterwards took a sheet off the bed, and got the umbrella from the pawnbroker; I then searched and missed the articles stated in the indictment: she had no authority to pawn them - she had had the linen to wash.
JOSEPH JOHN GOOD. I live with Mr. William Mote, pawnbroker, of Little Warner-street. I have some articles which the prisoner left at our house.
THOMAS LINSCOTT. I live with Mr. Croft, pawnbroker, Turnmill-street, Clerkenwell. I have a number of articles pawned by the prisoner, at different times.
WILLIAM REED. I am an officer. I took the prisoner and found some of the duplicates in her pocket.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I intended to have returned them on the following Monday.
GUILTY. Aged 31.
Transported for Seven Years.

1501. BENJAMIN RIMELL was indicted for stealiing, on the 8th of September, fourteen dozen of pottle baskets, value 7 s., the goods of Allen Malcolm.
ALLEN MALCOLM. I am a cowkeeper, and live at Brentford. My father is a market gardener; I had a number of baskets in my father's potatoe cave. The prisoner is his carter. I cannot swear to them.
HENRY MALCOLN. I am the prosecutor's brother. I was sitting in doors with my father, when a girl said there was some person among the pottles; I went out and found the prisoner in the stable, with the pottles among the horses.
JAMES MALCOLM. On hearing my female servant say there was some person among the baskets, I went out; I did not see the baskets in the stable, but I saw them afterwards in the potatoe cave - I am certain that they had been moved and put back again.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Can you swear to these baskets? A. No.
HENRY MALCOLM re-examined. Q. Did you see the prisoner move any of these baskets? A. I did not see him bring them out; but I saw him take them back.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-278]
1502. PETER WIGMORE was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, two brooches, value 10 s., the goods of James Franklin.
JAMES FRANKLIN. I am a jeweller, and live in High-street, Bloomsbury. The prisoner came to my shop on the 10th of September, between one and two o'clock; I was not present when he came in, but I afterwards saw him standing by the counter, begging - my wife said she suspected him of having taken something; I did not miss any thing, and sent him away - I missed the brooches almost immediately; I then pursued, and brought him in out of the street; he at first opened his waistcoat, and said I might search him. I found the two brooches in the inside pocket of his coat.
GUILTY. Aged 9.

1505. WILLIAM ARTHUR was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, a tin box, value 2 d.; seven half-crowns, and a shilling, the property of James Cripps.
JAMES CRIPPS. I keep a cook's-shop, in Chapel-street, Mary-le-bone. On the 15th of July I heard a noise in my shop, and saw the prisoner by the side of the counter - he asked for some pease-pudding - I said I had none, and on going round the counter I saw my till was out, and the money taken from it, some of which had been in a tin-box, and some loose in the till - I had seen it about a quarter of an hour before, and no other person had been in the shop; I then looked on the counter, and saw the tin box, and the prisoner's hand under the scale on the counter. I said, "You have got my money;" the money I had in the till was two half-crowns, a shilling, and a sixpence; he said, "I have 6 s. 6 d. of my own." The whole of the money in the till and box was five half-crowns, some silver, and some copper.
J - WILLS. I am an officer. I took the boy, and found upon him two half-crowns, a shilling, and a sixpence.
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined Six Months.

1507. ROBERT JACKSON was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, a coat, value 20 s., the goods of Walter Phillips.
WALTER PHILLIPS. I keep the White Hart public-house, Little Windmill-street . The prisoner lodged with me - I hung my coat up on the Monday night, and missed it on the Wednesday morning; he was taken up on another charge, and a duplicate of the coat was found - he has been with me three months, and conducted himself well.
JOHN VIRGO BUCKLAND. I took up the prisoner, and found the duplicate of a coat upon him, which had been pawned at Mr. Tate's, in Cambridge-street.
THOMAS BAYLEY. I live with Mr. Tate, a pawnbroker. I have a coat, pawned by the prisoner, I believe, but I cannot swear to him. This is the duplicate which was given.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I met two men who used the house - they had got the coat; we went and pawned it together.

1509. CHRISTIAN M'CAA was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of September, a basket, value 2 s., and 200 girkins, value 2 s., the goods of William Drury.
WILLIAM DRURY. I sell fruit about the streets. On the 2d of September I had a basket with some girkins, near the corner of St. Martin's-lane. The prisoner asked me for a penny to get a pennyworth of bread to eat. I left her in care of my basket while I went to sell somegirkins, and when I came back I lost her, the basket, and girkins; I have not seen them since.
WILLIAM COOK. I am an officer. I found the prisoner last Saturday night, in consequence of information, and under her bed I found this basket.
(Basket identified.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went into a house in the Seven Dials, where I met with this man; he asked me to go with him; I went and lived with him three days; he told me if I would live with him he would support me like a lady. On the evening in question he asked me to assist him down St. Martin's-lane; as we were going along he said he was about to take a room in King-street, Drury-lane; I told him I had an objection to that, and it was my intention to return to my brother, who is a Justice of the Peace in Dumfries; he went to a public-house, leaving me his basket to make what I could of it, and to get a yard of muslin, which I wanted; I waited from half-past seven o'clock till half-past nine; I then went home and told my landlady that I had got permission to dispose of the things; but on Saturday last the prosecutor came to the house and said he had got a letter from my husband, and he asked me to go home with him; I said I had no wish to live with him; he said "D - nation to my soul, I will either hang you or transport you;" he went out and got a constable.
WILLIAM DRURY re-examined. Q. Had you ever lived with the prisoner? A. No, I never had any thing to do with her in my life; she came to my place one night about a fortnight before; I and my comrade gave her a lodging.
Q. Upon your oath did you ever permit her to raise any money upon your cucumbers or basket? A. No.
Q. Did you tell her you would be revenged on her, and transport her or hang her? A. No.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-286]
1510. JOHN SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, a clock, value 26 s., the goods of Timothy Sullivan; and ELIZA WATTS was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen.
SULLIVAN pleaded GUILTY.
Judgment Respited.
TIMOTHY SULLIVAN. I am the father of John Sullivan. I am a labourer, and live at No. 2, Johnson's-court. I went to the prisoner Watts' house, who keeps a marine-store-shop in Tothill-street. Thomas Welsh, who works in the gas-works, went with me on the evening of Monday week, the 12th of September, about twelve o'clock, I had missed my clock from my room that day - my son was with me, and directed me to the house - the door was open, and Watts was at home - I said "Ma'am, do you know this boy?" she denied knowing him - I asked her son, who was in the room, if he knew him - he said he did - I said to Watts you must know my boy, as he has been here selling a clock this afternoon - she said she did not know him. Welsh said, "Mistress, here's the boy's father who owns the clock: we don't want to hurt you nor the boy; all we want is the clock; and here is the three shillings for you which you gave for it." She said to my son, "You bad boy, you are not trusty, to come into my house after you have robbed your father; you told me you had picked up the clock over the water, on a gentleman's steps; did you not say so?" - he said he did. Welsh then said, "Mistress, give us the clock, we don't want to hurt you." She then asked if I would take payment for it till the next morning, and she would return it, as it was not then in the house, and asked if thirty shillings would do till she sent the clock home. The boy had said before, in her presence, that she had given him three shillings for it. I said I would not leave the house till I got the clock, or she sent for an officer to take me up. She then whispered to her own boy, and he said would not two pounds do till I got the clock back again. I said I would take no money; I wanted nothing but the clock. She then told the girl to bring a pen and ink for me to write the number of my house, and she would send the clock home next day. She then laid down sixpence for a pot of beer to treat us. My wife then came, and brought the watchman to take my boy and her. She said to my wife, "Go home, woman; and your clock is at home before you." I told her to go home, as we had made it up, and I expected to have the clock every moment. As we were going out she came and took hold of my arm, and said she was a lone woman, and begged me not to hurt her, and she would send it home the next morning. She whispered to Welsh to make it up with me - she then went away, and I thought she was going to fetch the clock. I remained there till after eleven o'clock, when my wife and another woman came to call me, but the prisoner did not return. Her boy asked leave to go out to look for her, and he did not return. The next morning my wife went to see if she could get the clock - the prisoner said she knew nothing about it - she did not buy it. She was taken into custody. I have never seen the clock since.
Prisoner WATTS. He said, if I would give him 5 l. he would make it all right. Witness. I said it was not in my power to do so - on my oath I did not.
THOMAS WELSH. I am a stoker in the Gas-works. I went with the prosecutor - what he has stated is correct. The prisoner offered me 30 s. in the passage - I said I would go and speak to him. I went to Sullivan, and said "Will you take the money for the clock?" he said "No, I will not leave the house till I have got it." I went away before him, but I was there when the prisoner put a 6 d. on the table. I said "I am very dry," and she sent her girl to get the pot of beer.
Prisoner WATTS. This witness called me into the passage, and said, if I would sleep with him, he would make it all right with Sullivan. I said
"If you have any thing to say to me, come to me to-morrow-morning, and I will speak to you in a proper manner;" he took me round the neck and kissed me. Witness. I declare to my God I did not; she pulled out a purse, and offered a sovereign and 10 s., and I said to Sullivan "If I were you, I would take it and make it up."
THOMAS BOYLE. I know John Sullivan; he told me to stop at the end of the court - he went into his house, and brought out a clock in a bag. I went with him to the prisoner's house, in New Tothill-street - she turned me out, and would not let me go in. I stopped there till Sullivan came out again with 3 s. - he had left the clock and bag in the house.
Prisoner WATTS. I was on my bed at the time.Witness. She could stand, and walk about as well as I could, and got up and turned me out.
(Witnesses for the Defence.)
ELIZA WATTS. I am the prisoner's daughter. When the boy came to offer the clock, my mother asked him where he got it - he said he picked it up by the Marshgate; she said if he had stolen it from any body, she would have nothing to do with it - she asked him that several times; she said clocks were plentiful, and cheap enough, but she would not mind giving him 3 s. for it - she was very ill at the time, and could not get up - the other boy did not come in. I saw him at the door, but my mother did not turn him out - I will swear that - it was the boy's uncle who sent for the porter - it was my mother's 6 d. which she put upon the table to pay for some cheese for supper; my mother did not say to them, that if 2 l. would do, she would give it them. Sullivan said he would make it up for 5 l. My mother did not offer a sovereign and 10 s., she had not a sovereign about her. I heard of the man kissing my mother, and wanting to sleep with her, and I was quite shocked at it. I do not know what my mother usually buys. I was at home at the time. I had left my service at a public-house, and was at home, but I do not know what became of the clock.
ALEXANDER WATTS. I am thirteen years of age. I am the prisoner's son-in-law. The boy came in with the clock, and asked 12 s. for it; my mother asked where he got it; he said he picked it up at the Marsh-gate, at the steps of a door; my mother said clocks were very cheap, and said she would give him 3 s. for it. I then went out, but was at home in the evening, when Sullivan and Welsh came. I did not say that if 2 l. would do to make it up it should be given - I did not see my mother take out her purse, nor hear her say that it would be at home the next morning; she said it would be at home soon; and she did not offer to treat them with porter; Sullivan sent for it, and put the penny into his pocket; I did not hear the man say "I will take no money - I will have my clock;" but Welsh said he would take 5 l. to make it up with Sullivan.
GUILTY.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-287]
1511. RICHARD WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, five books, value 5 s., the goods of Alexander Black and others, his partners.
PETER PICKTON. I am in the employ of Alexander Black; he has two other partners - he is a bookseller, and lives in Tavistock-street, Covent-garden. The prisoner was porter there; I missed some books from time to time, and on the 10th of September, at seven o'clock in the morning, I saw the prisoner with an octavo volume under his coat, coming down stairs - he then went into another kitchen - I then went into the front kitchen, where he went first, and searched among some shavings, and found this octavo volume.
ALEXANDER BLACK. I have two partners. This book is one that was found at a house (the Welch Harp) where the prisoner said he had left it; I was present when it was brought back by the clerk; the prisoner told me that he had taken it on the day before.
WILLIAM FENNER. I am a pawnbroker. I have three books pawned, I believe, by the prisoner, on the 1st of September.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Three Months and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18250915-288]
1512. BRIDGET RODWELL was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of August, two pairs of stockings, value 3 s., the goods of Mary Blackburn, widow.
MARY BLACKBURN. I am a widow and live at Clerkenwell. The prisoner had washed for me five weeks - I missed several things, and, on the 17th of August, I missed these two pairs of stockings; I then sent for the officer.
WILLIAM JORDAN. On the 17th of August, Blackburn sent for me; I went to the prisoner's lodgings, and asked her about them - she denied any knowledge of them - I found them wet in her pocket.
GUILTY. Aged 33.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-289]
1413. GEORGE BARTON and JOHN RAVENOR were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Sophia Isaacson, widow, about one o'clock in the night of the 4th of August at St. Anne, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, a spoon value 8 s., the goods of George Ferriman, six spoons, value 12 s.; a tea-pot, value 2 s; a jug, value 1 s., and an apron, value 6 d., the goods of the said Sophia Isaacson; two shoes, value 2 s., and a desk, value 1 l., the goods of Mary Roat; two boots, value 15 s., the goods of Nicholas Fairless, and a shirt, value 5 s., the goods of Thomas Matthews .
SOPHIA ISAACSON. I am a widow. I live in Canton-place, East India-road, in the parish of St. Anne, Limehouse. I believe I was up last on the night of the 3d of August - I was as late as a quarter before twelve o'clock; the house was then secure every where, and my property was safe. My servant was up first next morning; one of my lodgers came and told me that my house had been broken open in the night. I came down and found the back door leading from the garden broken open - I believe it had been secure when I went to bed. I missed some spoons, some knives and forks, a tea-pot, a jug, and an apron, of my own property; two shoes and a desk, the servant's property, and two boots of Mr. Fairless, and one shirt of Mr. Matthews - that part of the property which was not my own, I do not know the value of. I afterwards saw the spoons shewn to me by the officers. I never saw either of the prisoners till they were before the Magistrate.
MARY ROAT. I am the prosecutrix's servant. I got up about six o'clock in the morning on the 4th of August; it was quite light. I cannot say how the back door had been opened - it had been fastened with bolts the night before, when I went to bed; but it was open when I got up - the front kitchen shutters were open, and one of the windows thrown up. I missed the property which my mistress has described. I did not find any phosphorous, but found some matches which were not there when I went to bed - they appeared to have been lighted - some of them I swept up, and others I have taken care of. I am sure I fastened all the doors when I went to bed - the desk which I lost was worth about 1 l.
THOMAS GRAY. I am a Thames Police officer. I heard of this transaction about half-past five o'clock on the morning of the 4th of August. I went in company with Yates to a house in Higham's-buildings, Bluecoat-fields, and saw the prisoner Barton in bed undressed - two more men were lying on the bed dressed. The two men walked out of the room; and Ravenor who was also in the room followed them. Yates followed Ravenor and brought him back to me with his hat in his hand - he had hardly left the room a moment. I put my hand in the hat, and found a silver table-spoon, a tea, and salt spoon, and part of a metal spoon. I then searched him and found in his pocket a bunch of phosphorous matches. I asked him how he came by the property - he said he found it in Stepney-fields. Barton said "Here's a pretty job you've done for me." I asked Barton if he lodged there - he said Yes, and paid 8 d. a night for his bed. Yates then examined Barton farther.
Cross-examined by MR. CRESWELL. Q. What kind of a morning was it? A. It was light. I do not know whether it had been raining in the course of the night - it was dry when I saw the prisoners. Barton was undressed - the others went away.
HENRY YATES. I am an officer. I went to the place in question, between five and six o'clock in the morning with Gray. I saw the persons in the room, and knowing two of them in particular I said "Gray its not all right;" I then saw a pair of women's shoes under the bed. The other two, whom I knew, then walked out - Ravenor followed them, and I said to Gray "I think there's something in that boy's hat," for I heard something chink. I then went after him, and took hold of his hat, and two silver spoons dropped out of it. I took him back to Gray, who took the rest of the property. I then searched the bed, and found an apron on the top of it, which has been identified. I then asked Barton what he had got in his pocket - he said, nothing he was afraid of. I searched his pockets, but found nothing relating to this. I asked how he came possessed of the shoes, and he said Ravenor put them under the bed.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
RAVENOR'S Defence. As I was going over Stepney-fields, I saw a bundle lay, and I took it up and opened it. I found the matches, these spoons, and an apron. Two boys called out, "That is ours." I said it was not theirs or it would not have laid there - they followed me, and I went to ask Barton to let me in, as it rained very fast.
RAVENOR - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 15.
BARTON - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-290]
1514. MARY READ was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Whitcombe, about eight o'clock in the evening of the 11th of August, (no person being therein), and stealing a pair of trousers, value 5 s.; and a cap, value 2 s. 6 d.; his property.
MARY WHITCOMBE. I am the wife of John Whitcombe, who is a drummer in the Guards. I live in Rochester-terrace, Vauxhall-road. I went out, about eight o'clock in the evening, of the 11th of August, to take my work home - I left no person in my house. As I went out I saw the prisoner standing near my door, and told her, if she saw my husband come home, to tell him I was gone home with my work. I returned in about half an hour; the prisoner was still standing at the same place - I found my door had been opened with a false key, but did not miss any thing that night; next morning I missed a pair of trousers, which I had left in my room, when I went out the night before, and a cap. I saw her that morning, about nine o'clock, and asked her what she had done with the trousers and cap, which she took from my room. She at first said, she had not seen them; and then said, she had sold them. I said, if she would tell me where she sold them, I would not hurt her; she then said she had pawned them. I went with her to one pawnbroker's - but she said they were not there, they were over Westminster-bridge.
JOSEPH COOPER. I am an officer - the prisoner was given into my custody on the 12th of August, and I found this cap in her bosom - I asked her what she had done with the drawers; she said she had pawned them for 6 d., to get a breakfast.
(Cap produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Of stealing the cap only. - Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-291]
1515. SARAH BUCKLEY and DENNIS CONNER were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Jane Chapman, about eleven o'clock in the forenoon of the 11th of August, (she and others being therein,) and stealing a shirt, value 4 s.; a waistcoat, value 4 s.; three frocks, value 4 s.; two pairs of stockings, value 2 s.; two pieces of flannel, value 8 d.; a handkerchief, value 3 s.; and a cap, value 1 s.; the goods of Charles John Anderson .
HANNAH ANDERSON. I am the wife of Charles John Anderson - we lodge at Mrs. Chapman's. I do not know her Christian name - it is in St. Giles's parish. On the 11th of August, I went out between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning - returned in about an hour, and missed this property - I had left some on a chair, and some on the floor. I left Mrs. Chapman at home, some persons in the garret, and people in the next room. I never saw either of the prisoners till they were before the Magistrate. The property I lost, was one shirt, one waistcoat, three frocks, three pairs of stockings, two pieces of flannel, a handkerchief and a cap, the whole was worth about 1 l.
EMILY WINTER. I keep a clothes-shop in Monmouth-street. I bought a shirt for 2 s. 6 d., and a waistcoat for 1 s. 6 d., of a young woman, whom I took to be a flower girl. I went to the office, and signed my name to a paper, the contents of which were read over to me; I cannot swear I did not say that it was the female prisoner, as it was not explained to me; but I only know it was a short woman - I cannot say it was the prisoner.
ELIZABETH MONCK. I am in the clothes line, and live in Monmouth-street. I bought a pair of stockings and two small pieces of flannel, which the officer has; of a person, who, I believe, to be the female prisoner, but she was differently dressed to what she is now.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Will you say positively, upon your oath, that she was the person? A. I should not like to say positively - her bonnet was very much over her face, and I did not particularly notice herfeatures; I do not know how I came to sign a deposition, stating that I purchased the stockings and flannel of the prisoner, but I was a good deal frightened.
THOMAS CLEMENTS. I received information on Thursday, the 11th of August, about seven o'clock in the evening - I was on duty, and the female prisoner was brought in, and examined before the Magistrate.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the examination taken down? A. No. This was when she was first charged with the offence - neither of the clerks were in the office at the time. In consequence of what she said I went to Mrs. Monck's, No. 77, Monmouth-street; I there found two pairs of stockings, and two pieces of flannel; I then went to Mrs. Winter's, and got a waistcoat and a shirt. I heard part of their examinations before the Magistrate, and part of their depositions read to them.
Q. Will you undertake to swear that you heard the whole of their depositions read to them? A. Yes; those of Winter and Monck.
JAMES HOWEL. I am a pawnbroker. I produce three frocks - they were pawned by a woman, in the name of Maxwel - she is not here.
JEREMIAH BEECH. I took the female prisoner into custody - I found on her a parcel, not belonging to the prosecutor, and some other articles, which led to these.
PHILIP RILEY. I took Conner into custody, and asked him what he did with the handkerchief - he said "I gave it and the frocks to Kit Maxwel;" Maxwel was taken, and admitted evidence against them, but she has not been found since.
BUCKLEY'S Defence. I went with a young woman to sell the things - she said they were her's; I know no more about them.
BUCKLEY - GUILTY. Aged 19. Of stealing only. - Confined Six Months.
CONNER - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-292]
Middlesex Cases, Third Jury, Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1456. THOMAS PHILPOT was indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of George Hawkins, about two o'clock in the night of the 5th of September, with intent to steal, and stealing a time-keeper, value 15 s., the goods of John Sheen .
JOHN SHEEN. I live in the front room, on the ground floor, of Mr. Hawkins's house, in George-street, St. Pancrass. I had a time-keeper on the mantle piece - it was safe when I went out at six o'clock, in the evening of the 5th of September - I returned soon after one, and it was gone - a pane of glass was broken in the upper sash, fronting the fastening of the window - I know it was fastened when we went out, as my wife went to fasten it. I went on Hawkins' bed along with him, because there were two women in the house, who could not go to bed - I was sober - when we had been on the bed about three quarters of an hour, we heard a knock; he got up and went down - I do not know any thing else.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you go to amuse yourself at Bartholomew-fair? A. Yes; my wife is not here - the prisoner lived in the house with me, and he was at the fair, but I did not see him.
GEORGE HAWKINS. I keep the house - Sheen lodged with me, and the prisoner also. On the evening of the 5th of September, about six o'clock, I went with my wife and Sheen to the fair - I saw the time-keeper on the mantelshelf in the front parlour - I saw Mrs. Sheen fasten the sash down with a thing that goes across, and keeps it down - the back door was fastened - we went out and fastened the street door - we returned about one o'clock, and found a pane of glass had been cut on the outside, and some person had put their hand in and opened the window, which was left open - Mrs. Sheen had left the tinder box on the table - we found it was there, but the steel had been thrown down - I went to my room and got a light - I heard a knock at the door about two o'clock in the morning - I went down and saw the prisoner, and said, "Philpot, you have been home before, and taken the time-piece from the mantel-shelf" - he said, "I have not, I can bring witnesses to prove I have been at the fair all day;" he then went away but in consequence of what Mr. Hollis, my neighbour, said, I followed, and overtook him about two hundred yards from my house - I took hold of him, and he said, "Hawkins, I will tell you all about it;" I said, "It is no matter what you tell me, here is the watchman, and I will give charge of you;" he put his hand into his pocket and took out the time-piece, which he gave to me.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not go away from the house? A. Yes, because I refused to let him in - I did not agree with him to come again the next day, but he said, he should call - he was sober, and so was I - I had had something to drink, he did not tell me he had been home tipsy, and had taken the time-piece in mistake, and was going to put it back again.
JAMES SMITH. I am a watchman. On the morning of the 6th of September, I saw Hawkins and the prisoner - I took the time piece - the prisoner appeared pretty sober; he gave no account of how he got it - Hawkins asked how he came to take it - he said, he had taken it and was very sorry - he hoped he would forgive him, and he would give him a week's wages to make it up.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Five witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 24. Of stealing only. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18250915-293]
1517. JOHN MAY and WILLIAM MOORE were indicted for stealing, on the 2d of September, at Hillingdon, a gelding, price 17 l., the property of Thomas Bowell.
THOMAS BOWELL. I am a carrier, and live at Oxford. On the 31st of August I had a black gelding, which I turned out in the Port Meadow - there were many other horses there, and I took particular notice of the marks on my gelding, that I might know him again. On the 2d of September I went to get it, and could not find it any where. I went in search of it for two days, and then heard that two suspicious characters had gone to Uxbridge. I saw an account in the paper, and went to Uxbridge on the 9th. I found my horse in the stable of the George Inn, and took him back. I did not see the prisoners till I got to Bow-street.
WILLIAM INGRAM. I am a constable of Uxbridge. On the 2d of September, in consequence of information, I went to the George Inn, Uxbridge, and saw two horses standing inthe yard - one was a black one. I asked the hostler where the man was who belonged to them - he said in the tap. I went to the tap with the man who gave me the information, and found the two prisoners there. I asked the price of the grey horse; May said 4 l. I asked if he would go in harness - he said Yes, he had worked him at Thane for four years. I then asked the price of the two horses together, and May said 14 l. I said "Let us go and look at them;" May, the man who gave me the information, and I, went to look at them. I said the black horse looks as if he had lain at grass. He said "Yes, I took him out this morning at four o'clock." I then went to Moore, who was in the house, and asked him how far they had ridden the horses - he said from this side Thane. I said, "Then you have been travelling all night - what time did you set off - at nine o'clock?" - he said Yes. I said, "Was it dark?" - he said Yes. I then went to the door and called May in, and said "I will see if I can deal with you." He came in - I said I had strong suspicions of their being stolen, and I should take them into custody, and detain the horses. I searched May, and found nothing on him - I found on Moore three spoons. I took them before the Magistrate at Hillingdon. Mr. Bowell saw the horses in my presence - the black one he saw was the one they had attempted to sell me. While they were in custody Moore said he knew nothing of the horses, but he was coming along, and that May asked him to have a ride.
MAY'S Defence. I was coming down the street in Oxford, and I met Abraham Hyams and Thomas Jackson, who had the horses with them. I intended to have come by the waggon to London, to get advice for my arm and leg, but I came with them; and just before we got to Uxbridge, they said they were authorised to sell the horses for 14 l. or 15 l., but they would not turn 14 l. away. Mr. Ingram saw the two men I had them from.
WILLIAM INGRAM re-examined. Q. Did he give you this account when they were taken? A. No; I saw two men at the cage door, but they said they knew nothing of them.
MAY - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
MOORE - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-294]
1518. JOHN MAY and WILLIAM MOORE were again indicted for stealing, on the 2d of September, at Hillingdon, a gelding, price 6 l., the property of John Pacey.
No evidence.
NOT GUILTY.
