1519. WILLIAM ROBINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of August, at St. George, a chest of drawers, value 8 s.; two pairs of trousers, value 8 s.; two silk handkerchiefs, value 9 s.; a scarf, value 10 s.; two gowns, value 10 s.; two bird cages, value 3 s.; twenty dozen of fishing hooks, value 10 s.; a sieve of currants, value 3 s.; three frocks, value 2 s.; two shirts, value 2 s.; two brooches, value 6 s., and seven sovereigns, the property of Joseph John Harrad, in his dwelling house; and MARY COLDECOTT was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
JOSEPH JOHN HARRAD. I am a green-grocer, and live at No. 1, James-street, Commercial-road, St. George's, in the East. I and my wife went out to market, about half past four o'clock, in the morning of the 2d of August - I left a widow woman, her son, and my child at home - I returned about a quarter before eight, but my wife remained at the market - I got the key into the lock, and opened the street door, but could not get it out again - I sent for a smith, who took the lock off, and got it out - I opened the front of the house, and took in some things off a waggon; my little girl went into the room, to put some play things on a shelf - she then came to me, and gave me information; I went into the room and found a small mahogany chest of child's drawers gone, which had in them some beads, some fishing tackle, and seven sovereigns, and some silver, and a pocket-book which had been locked in one of the drawers - I had seen the money and the pocket book safe the night before, but did not count it - it was safe that morning, before we went out, when my wife took some money to go to market with - I heard it chink in the drawer, but did not see it - I then missed some birds, and in a short time afterwards, a sieve of white currants. I sent my little girl to tell my wife, who came home and opened the large drawers, and found that they had been opened, and some things taken out.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When did you see the prisoner in custody? A. I think about three weeks afterwards.
MARY ANN HARRAD. I am the prosecutor's wife - I kept the money and the pocket-book in the top drawer of a small chest of drawers - on the morning of the 2d of August, I saw seven sovereigns and the book, which I left there with some silver; there were some handkerchiefs, and other things, taken out of a large chest of drawers - the small chest was altogether gone, and two birds and cages, a sieve of currants, and three frocks.
MARIA MINTO. I am the wife of John Minto, and live at the corner of James-street, opposite Mr. Harrad's - on the 2d of August, about a quarter before six o'clock, I was standing at the window, and saw the prisoner Robinson, come out of their house with a light pair of trousers on, and a large bag, which appeared to be half full in his right hand; he shut the door with his left hand - I had not seen him before, but I particularly noticed him, because I knew they had a new lodger, and I thought it was him.
Cross-examined. Q. How far were you from him? A. Just across the street - he opened and shut the door gently - he was as much as three minutes shutting the door, and crossing the road - I suspected something, but did not give an alarm - he had his hat on, and a flannel jacket, and was marked with the small pox.
SUSANNAH BOWERS. I live in Wentworth-street - I have known Mrs. Coldecott for three years - I was accustomed to deal with her - I have seen Robinson at her shop - he has served me and bought the bones which I took there to sell - he went by the name of Mr. Robinson.
JAMES LEA. I am an officer of Whitechapel. On the 21st of August, I went, in company with Foster, and apprehended Robinson, in New Gravel-lane; there was a woman there, whom he called his wife - he said he wanted to go and speak to her - I saw him hand something to her - Foster immediately caught hold of her hand, and found it was three keys - I found thirteen duplicates, which she said, were her property, and a ticket amongst them of a pair of trousers; I asked her if they were her's;she said, they belong to a young man who had left them there; Robinson said, "Yes, they belong to an acquaintance of ours;" there was also a duplicate for a silk handkerchief, they were both in the name of Robinson - I then went to Coldecott's house, in Wentworth-street, and found a small chest of drawers in her room - I asked who they belonged to, she said to her - she opened the top drawer, and took something out to put it into her pocket - I took it from her, and found it was some fishing tackle, which she said a person had left there - I found some little beads in the drawers, and some duplicates in the name of Robinson.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not persons who have stolen things generally give another name at the pawnbroker's? A. I do not know. I did not find any tickets on Robinson - I do not know where that woman is - she is gone.
JOHN GRAMSHAW. I am shopman to Mr. Kennedy, a pawnbroker. I have a pair of trousers, pawned by a woman, in the name of Ann Robinson, on the 3d of August.
WILLIAM FOSTER. I was with the other officer. I saw Robinson, and said, "I want you;" he said, "Will you allow me to go up and speak to my wife?" he went up, and I heard something chink as he was giving it to his wife; I took hold of her hand, and found three keys in it - she said, "Do not hurt my child" - I said I would not. I have a handkerchief which was taken in by a person belonging to Mr. Reynolds, the pawnbroker, but he is ill.
JOSEPH JOHN HARRAD re-examined. Q. What size was the chest of drawers? A. About a foot high. There were two bird cages and a sieve of currants stolen - I do not think they would all go in one bag, or that one man could have carried them.
ROBINSON'S Defence. A young man was taken in company with me, who was. I believe, a nephew of the prosecutor - he was suspected of robbing his uncle; after the second examination they had him out privately, to see what they could make of him, and promised him his liberty. The prosecutor told him he was his uncle, and if he told where the property was he would not give charge of him; we were then remanded, and the young man's mother went to the grandfather, and they made up among their friends 21 l., and gave it to the prosecutor, not to say any thing against the young man. When we went up again Lady Minto, as she is generally called, came up half tipsy, and said she knew I was the person, because I was so very much pock-marked - the Magistrate asked if there was any charge against the young man - they said, No, and he was discharged.
JOSEPH JOHN HARRAD re-examined. Q. Was there any other person taken up? A. Yes: a young man named Goat; my sister married his father's brother - he was taken up on suspicion of robbing me, but I do not know where he is now; I have seen him since - he passed the door on the Saturday following; there was no such thing as any money made up. I do not know who took him up.
COLDECOTT'S Defence. I keep a lodging-house, and used to buy bones and rags, and who comes in or out I do not know. The chest of drawers was brought in for 2 s. - 8 d. of the money was for an old man's bed, and 1 s. 4 d. I let them have.
Two witnesses gave Robinson a good character.
ROBINSON - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 43.
COLDECOTT - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-296]
1520. ELIZABETH SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of July, at St. George, 11 lbs. of Cantharides, value 5 l., the goods of Samuel Foulger, in his dwelling-house.
SAMUEL FOULGER. I am a chemist, and live in Ratcliffe-highway. The prisoner used sometimes to come to my house, as an assistant to my servants. On the morning of Saturday, the 23d of July, I got up about six o'clock, for the purpose of watching what was done; I did not know whether the prisoner was in my house that morning - my family were out of town; I stopped on the landing-place, and saw the prisoner come out of the parlour, through the counting-house, with some mats in her hand, which she put down, and took some parcels off the cask of cantharides, which I had placed on it, and among the rest, a 14 lb. paper parcel - she then went back into the parlour, and came out again, with a bag, which she filled with Cantharides; she took it into the counting-house, and placed it under my desk; I went up to my son, and told him to get up, and go and tell Jackson, the officer, to watch the house, lest she should leave it. I then went into the shop and staid there till eight o'clock, and then left my son there while I went up to dress.
SAMUEL FOULGER, JUN. I am the prosecutor's son. On the morning of the 23d of July my father desired me to watch the prisoner, and to follow her if she went out - she went away - I followed her, and found a bag under her clothes - I then went for the officer.
THOMAS TRIPP ALLEN. On the 23d of July the last witness came for me, and gave me charge of the prisoner and the bag, which I delivered to the watch-house-keeper.
FRANCIS JACKSON. I am watch-house-keeper. The prisoner was brought to me at the watch-house, with this bag.
MR. FOULGER. It is mine - there are 11 lbs. - it cost 9 s. per pound.
Prisoner's Defence. I have been many years in my prosecutor's family, and never touched any thing before, but distress induced me to do it. I throw myself on the mercy of the prosecutor and the Court.
SAMUEL FOULGER re-examined. She has been many years in my family, and I believe always conducted herself well.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 38.
Strongly recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
The prisoner pleaded pregnancy in stay of the execution of her sentence; a Jury of Matrons being impannelled, decided in the affirmative.
[trialAccount t18250915-297]
1521. JAMES DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of July, twenty-one handkerchiefs, value 4 l.; two shawls, value 30 s.; a collar, value 1 s.; three yards of drill, value 7 s.; five yards of lutestring, value 5 s., and four pairs of stockings, value 8 s., the goods of William Shoosmith, his master, in his dwelling-house.
WILLIAM SHOOSMITH. I am a linen-draper, and live at the corner of Oxford-street. The prisoner was between two and three months in my service. On the morning of the 13th of July, I missed from my shop a linen shirt collar - I asked if he knew any thing about it - he said he did not - I said he must, as I had seen it inthe drawers a very few days before, and had intended to take it away - there was no one there but him, my wife, and myself - he then acknowledged that he did take it away, but did not tell me where it was - I said I had missed several other things, and he must have taken them away - he denied it - I said I should like to see his box - he went up stairs very readily and opened it, and turned out two or three pairs of gloves, which I believe belonged to me - on opening a hymn-book there, I found a number of duplicates of my property - I asked whose they were - he said they were not his - I said, "How came they to be pawned in your name?" he said they belonged to a youth of his acquaintance, who lived at a cutler's shop in Martin's-court - I said that was not likely - he was then taken into custody by the officer, who found the collar in his coat pocket - we went round to different pawnbrokers, and found most of the articles - they are articles which I deal in, and I had missed similar articles from my shop - there are no marks on any but the shawl, which is worth about 16 s. - there is no single article worth 40 s.
GEORGE POYLE. I am shopman to a pawnbroker of Broad-street, St. Giles'. I have a black silk handkerchief pawned for 4 s. 4 d., and a Belcher handkerchief pawned for 2 s. 6 d., by the prisoner.
JOHN SHILLRICK. I am shopman to a pawnbroker of Wardour-street. I have a handkerchief pawned by the prisoner on the 26th of June.
THOMAS JEROME. I am a pawnbroker. I have a silk handkerchief pawned by the prisoner on the 14th of June, and another, which I did not take in.
WILLIAM MATTHEWS. I am a pawnbroker, and have two silk handkerchiefs - one pawned on the 25th of June, for 2 s. 6 d., and the other on the 14th of June, for 2 s. The prisoner pawned one - I did not take in the other.
SAMUEL SUTHMIER. I am a pawnbroker, and have a handkerchief pawned on the 29th of June, in the name of James Davis. I do not know who by.
MICHAEL TURNER. I am a pawnbroker. I have a handkerchief pawned, I believe by the prisoner, on the 21st of June.
JOHN SIMMONS. I am a pawnbroker. I have a shawl pawned, to the best of my knowledge by the prisoner, on the 5th of July, and three handkerchiefs, on different days.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOSEPH CARTER. I am an officer. I found these duplicates in the prisoner's hymn-book.
GUILTY. Aged 19. Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-298]
1522. PETER DUFFEY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of September, at St. Pancras, a watch, value 16 l.; a chain, value 3 l.; two seals, value 1 l.; a key, value 2 s., and a time-piece, value 3 l., the goods of Charles Wilkinson, in his dwelling-house.
ELIZA WILKINSON. I am the wife of Charles Wilkinson, an attorney, who lives in Arlington-street, in the parish of St. Pancras. On the 1st of September I had been in the parlour all the morning, and went out of it about a quarter past eleven o'clock; I left this watch, the chain, the seals, key, and a time-piece, in the room - I went into the garden and heard an alarm from the servant - I came back and missed them - I went to the street-door and cried Stop thief! - the prisoner was brought back by some persons, and an officer with him - I saw the officer take the articles from him - they had been on the sideboard, and were worth 24 l.
ANN HILL. I am the prosecutrix's servant. On the 1st of September I went out and left the door ajar, and the gate of the front garden open; I went to the next house but one, and returned in about three minutes; I met the prisoner in the passage coming from the parlour - I asked him what he wanted - he asked me the way to Somers' Town; I asked if he went in to ask his way to Somers' Town - he gave me no answer, but ran away directly - I gave an alarm and so did my mistress. I saw him taken up the street, and am sure he is the who person I had seen in the passage.
WILLIAM LUMLEY. I am a coal-merchant, and live in the Hampstead-road. I was in Arlington-street, and saw the prisoner running towards me - he then turned off, and ran towards the brick-fields. I pursued, and said to the brickmaker's who had caught hold of him - "Don't hurt him; let us go before a Justice;" the prisoner said, he had thrown down the time-piece; a boy picked it up, and gave it to me. I then asked him where the watch was - he said, he had thrown that away. I said, "I will not take your word;" I put my hand into his pocket, and found it there - the officer afterwards took it out.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
ABRAHAM LORIMER. I am an officer, and took the prisoner in charge - I took this watch from his left hand waistcoat pocket - the time-piece was given to me.
Prisoner's Defence (written.) I hitherto have borne an unblemished character, which I have witnesses to prove. I have no wish to palliate my offence, but merely to state, that I was taking a walk with a young man - having no money, and being out of employ, I was persuaded to attempt this robbery - an alarm being made he escaped.
Two witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy on account of his good character.
[trialAccount t18250915-299]
1523. JOSEPH PAGE was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of September, thirty yards of lace, value 2 l. 10 s. the goods of Daniel Garsh, in his dwelling-house.
DANIEL GARSH. I live in Tabernacle-walk, Tabernacle-square. On Thursday, the 1st of September, I missed some lace from the shew-board - in my shop-window - near the door - on the left hand side as you come in. I saw it about eight o'clock in the evening, and missed it in about a quarter of an hour - it was about thirty yards - but I do not know the exact length - I should have bought it for about 50 s. Some time afterwards I saw Gayton, and he asked if I had lost some lace - I said, "Yes." We went with an officer to Bridgewater-square, and saw the prisoner coming out of a house; he said he had sold the lace there for 17 s. and left it in the top-drawer.
JAMES GAYTON. I am a secret springer in the watch-trade. On the night of the 1st of September, I was in Tabernacle-square, about twenty or thirty yards from Mr. Garsh's house; I saw the prisoner put his hand round, and take the lace from the window - there was anotheryoung man with him, but he went away, and I could not swear to his person. I had seen the prisoner two or three times near the window, before he took the lace; as soon as he had got it he ran off - I pursued him. I took him after he had got round two or three turnings - he immediately took a bitter oath, that he had not seen the lace, nor any thing of it, or any thing else, nor touched it. I then received a blow from some person, and the prisoner got away; I followed him to two public-houses. When he got to the second house, I sent for an officer - but he was not in the way. I then went back to the prosecutor's house, and took him, and Locke the officer, to the public-house where I left the prisoner - but he was gone. Locke took us to the house in Bridgewater-square - we met the prisoner and some others coming out - they all ran away - but the prisoner fell down, and was taken; he then confessed that had taken the lace, and sold it.
ROBERT LOCKE. I went with Gayton, and set him to watch, while I remained at a little distance - he came to me, and said,
"They are come out of the door." The prisoner fell down in running, and I took him; he said, "He is in it, he has got the money; I sold it for 17 s. to Imey Phillips, and it is in the top-drawer." I took him to the house, but there was such a mob that I could not get in, and I took him to the watch-house. I tried to get a search-warrant, and went for Harrison, the marshalman, to go with me, but I could not find him.
GUILTY. Aged 14. Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-300]
OLD COURT.
SEVENTH DAY. THURSDAY, SEPTEMBER 22.
Middlesex Cases, First Jury.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1524. ANN WILKING was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of August, two pelisses, value 5 s.; two shawls, value 8 s.; nine sheets, value 6 l.; five shirts, value 30 s.; a shift, value 6 s.; five neckerchiefs, value 5 s.; two caps, value 10 s.; two spoons, value 25 s.; three towels, value 5 s.; an umbrella, value 2 s.; five pieces of linen, value 5 s.; a pair of clogs, value 1 s.; two table cloths, value 10 s.; two pairs of stockings, value 2 s.; five gowns, value 20 s.; two napkins, value 2 s.; a pair of stays, value 2 s.; a blanket, value 4 s.; a frock, value 1 s., and three flat irons, value 1 s., the goods of George Thomas Dale, her master, in his dwelling-house; and ANN BLISS was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
GEORGE THOMAS DALE. I am a surgeon, and live in King's-place, Commercial-road. Wilking was in my service. On the 10th of August, a table-cloth was missing and that led to this other property being found.
JAMES LEA. I am an officer. On the 10th of August, I took Wilking into custody in Mr. Dale's kitchen, and asked what she had done with a table-cloth, if she had pawned it - she said No; but her mother had, and she believed it was at Mr. Aaron's, in Houndsditch. I went there with Mr. Dale, and found it - Bliss is her mother. I went to her house two or three times before she came home, and saw another daughter of her's who gave me a pocket-book, containing forty or fifty duplicates of property, which Mr. Dale identified. I took Bliss about four o'clock, and charged her with pawning property of Mr. Dale's; she said she was very sorry - she had done it through distress, and meant to redeem them again. I searched her, and found another pocket-book, with eight or nine more duplicates of Mr. Dale's property.
JOHN AARON. I am a pawnbroker. I have a shirt pawned for 2 s. 6 d., and a towel for 9 d., both in September, 1824, a gown in November; some stockings in December; a shirt in May; a cap, a coat, frock, table-spoon, table-cloth, and gown, all at different times, and all by Bliss.
EBENEZER HODGES. I am shopman to Mr. Baker, of Aldgate. I have a pelisse, shirt, gown, and stockings pawned at different times by Bliss.
GEORGE GILES. I am a pawnbroker. I have a towel, two handkerchiefs, and a table-cloth, pawned by Bliss.
MR. DALE. The property is all mine. Wilking lived two years with me.
WILKING'S Defence. My mother is innocent; she knew not but they were my own. Sickness and poverty induced me to do it.
BLISS'S Defence. I always understood they were her's.
WILKING - GUILTY. Aged 35. Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Transported for Seven Years.
BLISS - GUILTY. Aged 65.
Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-301]
Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1525. JAMES COLLINS was indicted for the wilful murder of Nicholas Dawkins.
MESSRS. BOLLAND and BRODRICK conducted the prosecution.
THE RIGHT HONOURABLE LORD WESTMORELAND. On the 23d of May I was one of a party at Lord George Cavendish 's, and had occasion, about half-past twelve o'clock at night, to go out with my servant, and when I came to the gate two carriages were jammed in together; no person could pass - several people were about. I called an officer, desired him to do his duty and clear the way; I suppose the officer to be the deceased, but I did not know him then; he spoke to one of the coachmen, then went in front of the prisoner's carriage, and was speaking to him; the other carriage gave way a little, and the prisoner's carriage rushed with great velocity up the yard; I cannot say that he whipped his horses - I did not see him do any thing - I thought there must be a good deal of mischief done, but I was so struck with his conduct that I immediately followed the carriage, nearly to the door, and asked the owner's name - he said Lady Lambert; I immediately went into the house, and told her Ladyship. I then came out, and found the Police officer against the wall, with his legs broken - I did not observe the man sufficiently to say whether he is the one I had told to do his duty, but I suppose he was; when he went up he was in front of the carriage - I saw him use no violence.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. I believe there are two gates to Burlington House - one for carriages to enter,and the other to pass out? A. This was the nearest gate - his carriage was on the left side of the gateway; that was the right side to enter - I did not observe his person; he was on the box - the other carriage withdrew but a little.
MR. BRODRICK. Q. Was the coachman your Lordship spoke to, the driver of the carriage which drove furiously in? A. Yes - I immediately followed it.
COURT. Q. What distance had the carriage to go from the gateway to the house? A. Perhaps thirty or forty yards. The gateway is nearly sufficient for two carriages.
Q. When two carriages are jammed in, is it not the proper method for one to go on, or they would get entangled? A. I cannot say.
JAMES PORTER. I was attending Lord Westmoreland. His Lordship came out about half-past twelve o'clock - he came from the front door towards the gate; when I got to the gate I observed two carriages jammed together, in the gateway - an officer was called for - one came through the gate - he went to the front of the horse, with his staff in his hand, and desired both the coachmen to move back from the gate; he then took hold of the reins, and put them back. I cannot say whether he took hold of the reins of one or both carriages - one of them gave way, the other advanced on, rather furiously, at a fast trot; I did not see the coachman do any thing - there was hardly room to get out of the way, as the carriages came on in that way - I went with my back close against the wall - the carriage passed close by me, and struck the officer, who was on the right, and he fell; I went to his assistance, and found his leg broken - he was taken to St. George's hospital - the carriage passed within three or four inches of my feet.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. The officer's right-hand, with his staff, would be in the direction of the carriage on the left of the gateway? A. Yes; I cannot say which carriage backed; if his staff was in his right-hand his left would be at liberty to seize the reins. I moved to the right hand going in - there are two gateways - this is the western one. I heard no intimation of the accident given to the prisoner.
COURT. Q. Suppose the man, who backed, had been in the right line with a string of carriages behind, could he get back without endangering the carriage? A. I cannot say; I did not see the officer cling to the carriage pole.
SAMUEL DAVID DANCE. I am porter to Lord George Cavendish. On the 23d of May, between twelve and one o'clock at night, I saw two carriages in the middle gateway, which is the general entrance - there are three gates only - two were opened that night - the center is westward of the other - the two carriages were jammed together, and the coachmen were flogging their horses.
Q. Were both flogging their horses or only one? A. I cannot say, but I observed the horses flogged - Lord Westmoreland came out, and desired the assistance of an officer, who tried to force them back - he tried to persuade them to go back to Picadilly - I did not exactly notice what he said - I saw him go towards the carriage, and persuade them to come in one at a time; one backed, the other came in with a rush, the officer was forced down - I stood at the end of the gate, the officer was down a little beyond where I stood four or five yards from the gate - I did not particularly notice in what way he fell; he was in front and fell; the carriage wheel went over him in a moment - it was Dawkins, whom Lord Westmoreland called up - I did not leave the gate to go up to him - I afterwards saw him taken away on a shutter.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not go out to see the line of carriages? A. Yes; this was the proper gate to enter; I found two in possession of the gateway, one made way which liberated the other, and it came in and drove the officer on, he was down in a moment, he fell four or five yards from where the carriages were entangled.
Q. Whether the horses went forward of their own accord, on feeling themselves at liberty, you cannot say? A. No; it was certainly an accident.
COURT. Q. What occasioned the accident? A. I do not know; I did not see him hang by the reins or the pole - I cannot say whether the horses forced him forward; he fell a little way beyond where I was - whether he held by the people or reins, I cannot say.
THE REVEREND SAMUEL CHARLES GOODWIN, D. D. On the night of the 23d of May, or rather early in the morning of the 24th, I was in Piccadilly, and at the western gate of Burlington House (it was a very light night) I saw a great multitude of carriages reaching to St. James's-street - they were not in order, not in a line; when I came to the western gate two carriages whipped furiously up together - there was no line; both came in at the gateway, which stopped my passing on the path; they were violently contending for priority of entrance; the gateway being insufficient in width to admit two, they were jammed together - both persevered in whipping their horses. I addressed them in a loud tone, saying,"For shame - you cannot both get in together;" I then exclaimed much louder, "You scoundrels - you will spoil your master's horses and carriages too." I was disregarded; or possibly not heard; they persevered in whipping, though they could not stir. I was very much cheered by the people, saying, "Bravo, Sir - we want some such as you;" I then exclaimed, "Constable - officer." Some one within the yard, whom I could not see, but took for an officer, called out "Put back, stand back" - both carriages then backed a little; one of the drivers renewed his whipping, and darted in furiously; I then heard a cry of, "A man knocked down, run over." The multitude closed on the gate-way, and prevented my seeing what had happened. I passed on, and further I depose not.
Cross-examined. Q. You are a clergyman, and aware that this is a charge of murder? A. Yes. I was a voluntary evidence before the Coroner. I was returning from dining in Burlington-street. There might be one or two hundred carriages; they were not in a line: both the men whipped furiously up together. There was no line within my view.
Q. That you swear - I give you notice there are two or three witnesses to contradict you? A. There was no line near the gate - none within my view - there were carriages behind these two, but not very close; they were so close as to preclude my venturing to pass behind them, for fearof being run over - there was a continuation of carriages, reaching to St. James's, but not in a straight line - I cannot say which carriage had the priority - both drove up together - I was standing on the side of them, not within the gate-way - I think the horses had got within - the wheels were locked.
Q. Then how could you see them whip their horses with the wall on your side? A. It was very light, moonlight. I was between the fore and hind wheel, on the side next the Arcade. I could see the coachmen, for they stood up to whip; the wall is too high to see over; one carriage put back, and the moment there was room for one, he whipped his horses and darted in.
COURT. Q. Did you see them whipping distinctly? A. I did; I did not think he could have got in, the other backed so little; one backed more than the other, which admitted the other to dart in; there was room for one to back.
Q. Supposing that both backed a little to get disentangled, was it not the most prudent course for one to go forward? A. I suppose when one found room he should have gone forward, and did so.
Q. Suppose he had not driven furiously, and gone in without whipping, was it not proper for him to go in? A. Yes, according to my judgment - no foot passenger could pass till he had gone in.
SAMUEL PLANK. I am an officer of Marlborough-street. I was on duty on this night, at Lord George Cavendish's, to preserve order with the carriages. I heard a noise between twelve and one o'clock, and went down to the centre gate, where Dawkins lay with his back against the wall, and his leg broken - he was on the left side of the gate going out. I went with him to St. George's Hospital, and next morning went to Lady Lambert's stables and apprehended the prisoner. I told him I had a warrant against him for running over a man at Burlington-house last night. He said he recollected driving against a man, but had no idea that the wheel ran over him. I told him the man's leg was broken, and he was in the hospital - he said he was extremely sorry to hear it. The carriages should have been stationed with their horses' heads towards Hyde-park, and not to come in till called - that is always the regulation in that place.
THOMAS DAWKINS. I am a servant to his Majesty. Nicholas Dawkins was my brother; he was fifty-five years old - he was an officer of Marlborough-street; I visited him daily in St. George's Hospital - I believe he lived nearly twelve weeks - he said, full two months before he died, that he knew he must go, and that he was a murdered man. The night before he died, he said he must go, that it was all dicky with him - that if the coachman had pulled up in a proper manner, he might not have been run over, if he had not whipped on so; he said so a fortnight before he died, and the night before also - he was aware that he should not recover - he said he went down by Lord Westmoreland's order, to avoid the carriages coming in in that manner - that he got them to back a little, and Lady Lambert's carriage whipped on - that he laid hold of the pole and reins, and down he fell, and the fore and hind wheels went over his legs.
Cross-examined. Q. At one time the surgeons considered him out of danger? A. I understood so - he died of a fever.
Q. At the time he used these strong expressions was he not in a state of suffering? A. Yes.
GEORGE SKINNER. I am butler to Lady Lambert - the prisoner was her coachman.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he not a humane careful coachman? A. Yes; he was bailed in consequence of the deceased being pronounced out of danger - he has always expressed his ignorance of the accident, if it occurred through him; and his regret at it - he has never been out of the way.
MR. HENRY PITMAN. I am house-surgeon of St. George's Hospital. In May last, I remember Dawkins being brought to the hospital; he had received a compound fracture in the right leg; he was attended to immediately, and went on favourably till the 3d of June, when he was attacked with erysipelas, caused by the fracture; it extended up the leg and thigh, and terminated in the formation of an abscess, which was opened, as occasion required - there was a great discharge - his constitution suffered very much - he died on the 11th of August. I have no doubt of the fractured leg causing his death.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you pronounced him quite out of danger on the 11th of July? A. Yes, I considered him so at that time: he was a robust man, and not likely to be afflicted with erysipelas without injury.
MR. HENRY JEFFRIES. I am a surgeon of St. George's Hospital. The deceased was under my particular care; he had a compound fracture of the right leg, both bones were broken, in consequence of which an attack of fever followed: on the 31st of May, or 1st of June, that fever subsided; on the 3d of June he had a relapse - abscesses formed in several parts of his leg and thigh - they were opened several times - I considered him then in considerable danger - fever followed, and he died on the 11th of August - the compound fracture was the cause of his death.
Cross-examined. Q. In the state he died it is very probable that expressions of a hasty nature would escape him? A. The whole system was in a state of extreme irritation.
Prisoner's Defence. I am heartily sorry the accident happened, and was not aware of it having happened.
RICHARD FENNER. I am footman to Lady Lambert. On the 24th of May I was in attendance, and received directions from her Ladyship - the carriage was called, and I went for it; I found it drawn up at the corner of Duke-street, with the horses' heads in a direction to come on - it was not close to the gate-way; I told the prisoner to draw up; he at that time was not in the line to come in, because he could not get up; I went again, a second time, and found him jammed in the gateway, with another carriage - I repeated to him the order I had received for him to drive up as soon as he could - there was a great quantity of carriages behind him, at the time; I said her Ladyship was waiting, and that the carriage had been called for, I supposed, half an hour, and he must come up as soon as he could. I went to the house, and in about a quarter of an hour he came up, her Ladyship got in, and he drove away - I did not hear of the accident till next morning.
MOSES HOLLAND. I am servant to the Honourable Mr. Cavendish. I was at the gate of Burlington house, atthis time, and saw two carriages jammed into the gateway - I was within the gate, and saw the carriage come through - I observed no whip used, and must have seen it if it had been used. I got out of the way of the carriage myself without difficulty.
MR. BOLLAND. Q. How near were you to the gate? A. About three yards. I saw no whipping, and if it had happened I must have seen it. I was not there when the carriages were jammed, but when it drove in.
COURT. Q. Did you see the carriages jammed together? A. Yes; and saw a man between them, forcing them back - they both receded, then one advanced, which occasioned me to go out of the way a little. I saw no more till I saw the man down.
HON. LADY LAMBERT. The prisoner was in my service for two years, and bore a remarkably good character for humanity - I have every reason to be satisfied with him; I had been at Lord George Cavendish's, and waited, I suppose, in all, half an hour for my carriage after I had sent for it. I was anxious to get away, and told my footman to try and hurry him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-302]
Before Mr. Justice Gaselee.
1526. WILLIAM LEDLEY was indicted for killing and slaying Jane Ledley.
MR. THOMAS BLAKE, surgeon, of Shadwell, deposed that his opinion was, that the deceased died from the extreme heat of the weather, aided by excessive drinking, and not from personal violence.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-303]
1527. MARY GOGGEN was indicted for feloniously assaulting Catherine Sweeney, in a certain field near the King's highway, on the 12th of June, putting her in fear, and taking from her person, and against her will, a ring, value 16 s.; a shawl, value 7 s.; two keys, value 2 s., and two sovereigns, the property of John Sweeney .
CATHERINE SWEENEY. I am the wife of John Sweeney, and knew the prisoner before she was a year old. On the 12th of June I saw her in the parish of All Saints, Poplar - I was looking for my husband, who was in a public-house, and had the key of our house. I was coming home, and met the prisoner with Patrick Brown and Timothy Sullivan; she asked me to go and drink a pot of beer - I said I would not. I had left my husband in the public-house; she and the two men wanted me to go and have the beer; I refused, and Brown said I must go, and put his hand round me - I said I must go home. The prisoner came and put her hand under my arm, and said I must go; I went with her - she took me across the road, out of my own road, and by the water; she wanted me to go into a public-house; I said, "Goggen, I won't go into the public-house to night;" she said, "We shall both get home sooner by going this road than the other, because we can cross the fields" - she went to a public-house, and called for a pot of beer at the door - it was refused her, as she owed for some. I said, "Goggen, you have had too much liquor" - upon which she struck me a blow in the face; she turned round to the two men, who were standing behind us, and talked to them - I heard them whispering, thought there was something bad going on, and went away, and when I got into the fields Goggen called me, and said she would be with me; I waited in the field for her till she came to me - the two men came after her - she turned and talked to them - I ran away, and the first words she said were, "There, she is gone;" then the three followed me. I was knocked down by Brown - he fell himself at the same time - I was not long enough with them to know whether he was drunk or not; I was sober. I got up; I had the key of the street door in my hand, and when he came close to me to catch hold of me, I struck him in the face with the key - I got up before him; he caught hold of my pocket, tore my gown, and took the key of my box and two sovereigns out of my pocket - Sullivan came directly, and took my shawl; he whipped it off, and took it to Goggen, who stood about three yards off.
Q. Did you try to prevent his taking it? A. No; I did not see him till he pulled it off - because I was struggling with Brown. Goggen said to Sullivan, "D - n you, why don't you take the gold ring off her finger" - he came back, and knocked me down - and took the ring and beat me; I was more than half dead - he left me there. I would rather have lost the last drop of my blood, than he should have the ring. I resisted, and would not let him have it until he knocked me down, and took it and the key of the door away - all this was in the field. I went back to a man named Charley Brian. I was more than a quarter of a mile from home, and thirty or forty yards from the public-house. I went to Brian, he was in bed. Next morning I went to Goggen's house, and asked what was the reason she took and robbed me, along with two men, and why she went drinking with men, whom she did not know; she said, she would drink a pot of beer with any one who would pay for it. She said she did not know the men, but I have since found out that they lodged with her. She sent for me the same evening, and said she would give me my property up before to-morrow evening - but did not.
Q. Before this, had you told her she had better confess? A. I do not know what I told her - I was really out of my mind - I was three weeks in bed - I have found none of the property. I advised her twenty times over to give up the property, and if not, I would make her. I told her to give me my own, and bring the robbers up - she said she would not give Pat Brown up, if she was twelve months in Newgate. I said, if she did not give the property up, I would put her where she would not get out.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What business is your husband? A. A poor labouring man; he worked at the gas-works, or wherever he could get work. I go out with apples and fish.
Q. What time of night was this? A. Between eleven and twelve o'clock; the public-house, is the Eeel-pie-house by the canal, in the Merchants'-field - if I had screamed out, nobody could have heard me. I did scream and call murder. I knew the prisoner, but did not know where she lived. I went to her at six o'clock the next morning; a neighbour told me where she lived. I went to Lambeth-street about three days after, and had a warrant for Brown and Sullivan as well, but they were not taken. I had known them for two years, they are bricklayer'slabourers. I went before the Justice, who dismissed the charge - I did not speak much against the woman, because she had promised me my property. She took my shawl, and I told her to give it me up, when I went in the morning. I went to Hicks's-hall, and preferred this bill, and then had her taken again, as the Justice told me to indict her. Charley Brown saw me about one o'clock - when I went to his house, he saw my cap full of blood, and my clothes torn.
Q. Had the prisoner a child in her arms that night? A. Yes. I know Daly and Harrington - I did not tell them I had 2 1/2 d., but no more money.
JOHN SWEENEY. I am the last witness's husband. On Sunday night, the 12th of June, I went out and took the key in my pocket; and as I came home, I saw the prisoner in Gunn-lane, Limehouse - my wife and Sullivan were with her - it was about eleven o'clock. I did not see Brown. I left them talking to her at the corner of the street.
Cross-examined. Q. At what time of night did your wife come home? A. Not till seven o'clock in the morning; she said she was robbed by Goggen, Brown and Sullivan; that the prisoner decoyed her out of her way - it happened in the centre of a field, which is a thoroughfare - there are no watchmen; she could not be heard if she called out - the place is noted for robberies.
LUCY BRIAN. I was at the public-house, when the prosecutrix came to look for her husband, and in about half an hour I saw her at the top of the street, with a man, whose arm was round her neck. I saw the prisoner go towards her, and know no more. I saw the prosecutrix next day - she was ill used.
Cross-examined. Q. Was she struggling with the man? A. She seemed inclined to go home - he said No, she must go with him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-304]
Before Mr. Justice Gaselee.
1528. GEORGE HAMPTON was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of July, a looking-glass, value 30 s.; a piece of needle-work, value 30 s.; a pair of trousers, value 3 s.; a half handkerchief, value 1 d., and two aprons, value 3 d., the goods of William Peters, in his dwelling-house.
WILLIAM PETERS. I live in Grove-street, Hackney-road. The prisoner lodged with me for six weeks; I took him in distress, having known him. On the 27th of July, at six o'clock in the morning, I came down stairs, and found the shop door open - it was fastened the night before. I could not find him in the house. I had seen him go to bed the night before; he usually opened the shutters, which was not done. I missed this property, and found the watch in pawn. I waited at Mr. Laws, West-street, Smithfield, from seven o'clock till half-past five, when the prisoner came in with a bundle. I asked what he had done with the property he had taken - he did not tell me; he had got my hat on, and had left his own behind. I said he was not going out till the officer came; he went backward to the privy for ten minutes, and was just coming out when the officer came, and found on him, a pair of trousers, two aprons in a bundle, and a half handkerchief in his pocket - a duplicate was found in the privy.
WILLIAM WILLIAMS. I am an officer. I was fetched, and in the privy I found the duplicate of a piece of needlework; pawned in Helmet-row, Old-street. I found 24 s. and a handkerchief in his pocket.
FRANCIS GIDDENS. I was with Williams, and have a duplicate of a looking-glass pawned at Chapman's. Mr. Laws gave it to me.
JOHN LAWS. I live in West-street, West Smithfield. I found this duplicate in my privy, after the prisoner was gone.
JAMES FURRELL. I am shopman to Mr. Chapman, pawnbroker, St. John-street. On the 27th of July, about ten o'clock in the morning, the prisoner pawned this looking-glass for 12 s. in the name of John Clark. I gave him the duplicate produced.
CHARLES LEPLAISTRE. I am shopman to Mr. Cresswell, pawnbroker, Old-street. On the 27th of July, the prisoner pawned a piece of needle-work for 7 s.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 25. Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Confined One Year.

1530. CATO COURTHOUSE was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of July, at St. Bridget, alias St. Bride, 20 lbs. of lead, value 3 s., belonging to the Mayor, Commonality and Citizens of London, and fixed to a certain dwelling-house there situated.
EIGHT OTHER COUNTS varying the manner of laying the charge.
MESSRS. BOLLAND and BERNARD conducted the prosecution.
JOHN THOMAS KIRKUP. I am in possession of two houses, No. 4 and 5, George-alley, Fleet-market. On the night of the 7th of July, I was in No. 4 - I had information, and came home about eight o'clock at night, and between eight and nine, I saw the prisoner look over the wall leading out of Rose and Crown-court into George-alley - he was on the top of the privy - I had seen him before. I got my stick, mounted the wall, and found him crouching down with his hands on this lead. I secured him, and another person took the lead; we gave him to Johnson, and next day, by order of Mr. Montague, I compared the lead with the top of Mr. Eastwood's house in Shoe-lane - it corresponded exactly, nail holes and all; the house had been taken possession of by the Corporation - it was taken from the back gutter.
ALEXANDER JOHNSON. I am a constable, and took charge of the prisoner, the lead weighs twenty lbs. - I matched it with a short gutter on the roof of No. 27, Shoe-lane - it corresponded.
WILLIAM MONTAGUE, ESQ. I am the City surveyor. This house was taken by the Corporation; Mr. Eastwood occupied it, and Mr. Watson, was the freeholder - the prisoner was not employed there.
Prisoner's Defence. I was working next door, and got over there, for a necessary purpose - I did not move the lead.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Confined Six Months and Publicly Whipped, near Fleet Market.
[trialAccount t18250915-307]
1531. SARAH DEAN was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, three aprons, value 3 s.; three shifts, value 6 s.; a petticoat, value 2 s.; a night-gown, value 1 s.; a neckerchief, value 1 s., and a napkin, value 6 d., the goods of William Boswell.
SARAH BOSWELL. I am the wife of William Boswell, and live in Little Trinity-lane, on the ground floor; the street door is generally open. On the 8th of September, I had these articles to mangle - they lay in the window cill, pinned in a napkin - it is a large room; I was sitting in the back part of it, about eleven o'clock, at night, and heard a noise in the passage; I went towards the street door, and saw the prisoner going out - my door was open; she is a stranger - she must have come in, and gone rather behind my door to get them. I told the watchman, who laid hold of her, and the things were found pinned up in her gown.
JAMES BEAUMONT. I am watchman. I was two doors off, and laid hold of the prisoner by Boswell's desire, about two hundred yards off, and found all this linen in her gown; she said, she had been sent for them, but did not say who by; she seemed rather in liquor.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. They were given to me to hold by a woman, who said, she lodged there - I followed her.
JAMES BEAUMONT. There was no woman near.
GUILTY. Aged 44.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-308]
1532. WILLIAM ANDREWS was indicted for embezzlement.
SAMUEL REDDISH. I am a baker, and live in Little Britain. The prisoner lived two years with me, and received money on my account - his duty was to give me every day all he had received. George Gerard dealt with me, and owed me 11 l. 9 s. 4 d. On the 6th of September, he did not account to me for any money received from him; I gave him into custody that day week, and charged him with receiving money from Gerard, and concealing it - he said, it was through poverty - he is single, and had 22 s. a week, and as much bread as he wanted - he bore a good character.
GEORGE GERARD. I am a trunkmaker, and live in Milk-street. About the 12th of September, the prosecutor applied to me for 11 l. 9 s. 4 d., but I proved to him by my bills and receipts, that I did not owe him a farthing - the last bill I paid myself to the prisoner - it was 1 l. 11 s. he always brought the bread - that amount was up to the 3d of September; I produce the bill and receipts, which he gave me - I paid him a sovereign, a half sovereign, and a shilling.
JOHN LACY HAWKINS. I apprehended the prisoner - he said he did not mean his master to be at any loss - that he had assisted some friends in the country whom he expected to pay him.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that his master had kindly advanced him 20 l. for the support of an illegitimate child, and deducted from 15 s. to 18 s. a week, from his wages in payment, which so embarrassed him, that he applied part of this money to his own use.
SAMUEL REDDISH. I advanced him money to pay the parish, but left him to pay as it suited him.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Recommended to Mercy. Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18250915-309]
1533. JOHN CLARK was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of July, a handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of John Gibbs, from his person.
JOHN GIBBS. I am in the employ of the Excise, and live in Kinder-street, St. Georges's, in the East. On the 11th of July, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon, I was going from the Excise-office, Tower-street, home - I stood in Rosemary-lane, about two minutes, to look at something, and felt a hand at my right hand coat pocket: I put my hand down and missed my handkerchief - I turned round and said to the prisoner, "You have picked my pocket;" he was the nearest person to me, and was close to me - he said, "I have not;" I laid hold of him, and in pulling his coat open, found the handkerchief concealed under his left arm; I took him to the watch-house - it happened on the north side of the way, eight or ten doors from the Minories.
JOSEPH STONE. I am a constable, and the prisoner was delivered to me; he allowed that the handkerchief was in his possession; he said, it was given to him by another person - the spot the prosecutor has described, is in the City; it extends fourteen or fifteen houses down.
JOHN LACY HAWKINS. I am a marshalsman; this spot is in the City.
Prisoner's Defence. It was given into my hand by a young man.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18250915-310]
1534. GEORGE PATTERSON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of August, four live pigs, price 5 l. the property of David Wheeler.
DAVID WHEELER. I live at Newington, and had four pigs, they generally went out of the back premises, about eleven o'clock in the morning, and ran about near the house, and always came home by themselves at night to the stye; I had had them ten weeks, they were bred there. On the 17th of August they were let out about eleven o'clock. I missed them about three, they never strayed above one hundred yards or so from the house. I searched for them, but could not find them till the 19th when they were at the Coach and Horses, public-house, near Smithfield. I knew all four of them - the prisoner who was a stranger, was in custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you any mark on them? A. Yes; on one - a bit was cut off his ear. There was nothing to hinder their straying, but they never did.
WILLIAM HARVEY. I am a cow-keeper, and live in Lock's-fields, near to Wheeler. I knew, on the 17th, that he had lost his pigs, and on Friday the 19th, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I was in Smithfield-market, and saw four pigs in a pen by themselves, and knew them to be Wheeler's - the prisoner stood by them. I said - "You have got some very nice pigs there;" he saidYes. I went and informed the officer, and returned to the pen with him, and asked the prisoner what he wanted for the pigs; he said 5 l., or guineas, I do not know which. I offered 4 l. He said he could take no such money, for they cost him more. I then gave charge of him. He said he had bought them at Brentford, and did not take sufficient notice of the person to describe him; they looked very thin, but were not much altered.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you certain they were Wheeler's? A. I knew them immediately; I had no mark on them - they used to run about before my gate. I turn my own out in the same way, and never lost them. The prisoner said he got them from a man named Davis, or some such name. I should think them worth 5 l., or guineas.
CHARLES MATTHEWS. I am a constable. I was in the market on the 19th of August. Harvey fetched me to the pens - the prisoner was there. Harvey asked the price of the pigs; he said 5 l., or guineas. I said "They are stolen, and I shall take you." He said, he and another person bought them down below Brentford. I did not hear him mention the person's name whom he bought them of.
Cross-examined. Q. Harvey was present at this conversation? A. Yes. Some conversation passed before I got up.
THOMAS BRANSCOMB. I am an officer. I went with Matthews to the pen - the prisoner said they were his pigs - and after we took him to the public-house, I heard him say, he bought them near Brentford, of a man with a tilted cart, and a net over it, but did know his name or residence. He said there was another person with him, who saw him buy them. I had apprehended one Davis at that time in Smithfield, on suspicion of being concerned; he saw Davis - and said, he was the person who was with him, at the time he bought them. Davis was discharged.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you discharge him. A. He was examined three times. Brentford is about eight miles from Hyde-park corner.
(Witness for the Defence.)
WILLIAM LEE. I live in Canister-street, and have known the prisoner eight or nine months; he was in the habit of buying pigs and sheep, and selling them.
COURT. Q. Where has he bought any? A. I have seen him take part of a sheep about for sale. His mother is a laundress, and lived in Bedfordbury, and has now moved to Lambeth. I was never present when he bought any cattle, or killed any. I think I know of his killing one sheep at Lambeth-marsh. I have seen him with live sheep, I do not know when - and have seen friends buy meat of him.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy.
Confined One Year, and Publicly Whipped.
[trialAccount t18250915-311]
NEW COURT. (7th DAY.)
Middlesex Cases, Third Jury,
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1553. GEORGE CROFT and WILLIAM SULLIVAN were indicted for stealing, on the 3d of September, a desk, value 50 s., the goods of Robert Biggerstaff, in his dwelling-house.
ROBERT BIGGERSTAFF. I live at Thames-bank, in the parish of St. George's, Hanover-square. I had a folding mahogany brass-bound writing-desk, with brass handles and corners - the brass plate was off - it was an old one.
ELIZA PIDDINGTON. I am servant to the prosecutor. On the morning of the 3d of September, I saw the desk, about eight o'clock, standing on a table in the front parlour, and a bird-organ standing on it; about one o'clock, I was cleaning the passage on my knees, and saw a man go out of the door and along the garden towards the Thames; he walked across the Green, and got to the path - he had on a black coat and dark trousers; he was about the size of Croft. I did not see any other person about the premises; soon afterwards, in consequence of what my mistress said, I went to the parlour, and found the door bolted on the inside; I got in at the window, which looks towards the garden; I found the little organ standing on a chair by the window, and the desk gone; I gave an alarm to Brown, who lives next door, and then went over to the counting-house to my master.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. How far was the person from you when you first saw him? A. I do not think he was more than twenty yards. I think it was a black coat he had on - I only saw one person.
RICHARD HAWKINS. I am in the employ of Messrs. Cook and Co. the distillers. On the 3d of September, I went to dinner at one o'clock; I passed the door of Mr. Biggerstaff - I saw the two prisoners near there; Croft had a black coat on, and some crape on his hat: he had dark cord trousers on; he was sitting on the rails at the front of the house, and Sullivan was by him, with his hands full of apples; he had a blue jacket and trousers, and a seal skin cap; they had a piece of green baize by the side of them, but no desk; I went back in about twenty minutes; I met the two prisoner's again, and Sullivan had a desk on his shoulders, half covered with green baize, but I could see all the back part of it; it shut up in three pieces, and was bound round with brass, and had a brass handle at the end; neither of them spoke to me; they were coming towards Westminster; I saw Mr. Biggerstaff directly afterwards - he said he had lost a writing desk - I told him what I had seen.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you stop them to examine the desk? A. No; but I could see very plainly that it shut up in three pieces. I stopped and looked at it; Mr. Bickerstaff had not told me that it shut up in three pieces, till I told him what I had seen; I saw the end of it, and half way along it - I did not stop it because I had no knowledge of whose it was.
WILLIAM SCOTT. I live at No. 3, Vauxhall-road, with Mr. Kendall, an oil and colourman. I was going to Mr. Smith's, on the bank, on the 3d of September, and saw the prisoners at Johnson's-place, about half-past one o'clock, about a quarter of a mile from Mr. Biggerstaff's - they were going towards the Vauxhall-road, in the way to Westminster. I knew Sullivan - he had a small mahogany desk on his shoulder - the green baize had slipped off it, and hung on his shoulder - the desk was partly open - it had bits of brass round the corners. I did not say any thing to him, as he was on the other side - he had a youngman by his side, and I thought he was carrying the desk for him - I cannot say who it was, as I did not take notice - they turned round by Mr. Lewis', the market gardener's - he put the desk down on the ground, and covered the green baize over it again - when I got to Mr. Royal's, the boat-builder's, a man made some inquiries about it, and I stated what I had seen. Some person came to my house the same afternoon, and I went to Queen-square. Sullivan had a blue jacket and trousers on, and a seal-skin cap. I saw him again at the watch-house on Sunday morning - he then had a long white coat on, and an old hat. I went to Queen-square to give evidence on the Saturday afterwards. A strange young man came up to me and talked with me; and Sullivan came and said he would give me a shilling if I would say it was not him.
JOSEPH COOPER. I am an officer of Queen-square. I apprehended Croft on Saturday night, the 3d of September, in Peter-street - he was then dressed in a blue coat and dark trousers. I found this bit of crape in the crown of his hat, folded up, and pinned with black pins. I have seen him wear black crape on his hat of the same width as this. I asked him where he had been all day, and he said at his mother's. I asked where that was - he said at No. 52, Duck-lane. I went there, but could not learn that he had been there. I afterwards asked if he knew Sullivan - he said he did not. About one o'clock on Sunday I apprehended Sullivan, secreting himself in Mr. Sullivan's premises, the boat-builder, at Millbank - he had got over the garden pales, and was behind some articles on the premises. I asked if he knew George Croft - he said No, he did not. I asked him again, and he admitted that he did know him, but had not seen him for three months. Sullivan had the same coat on then as he has now, and a black hat. I knew both the prisoners before. I found at Croft's lodgings a letter signed "George Croft." A girl of the name of Mary Alexander lives there.
Prisoner SULLIVAN. I was walking up and down the field - I was not concealed. Witness. Yes, he was concealed - there is no way to get into that field but over a paling.
CROFT - GUILTY. Aged 17.
SULLIVAN - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18250915-314]
1538. WILLIAM RUSSELL was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, sixteen pieces of deal boards, value 10 s., the goods of Thomas Trott.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS TROTT. I am a builder. The prisoner was employed by me as a carpenter, in some buildings in the New North Road, Hoxton. I found all the materials, and gave him charge of them. I told him not to let any thing go off the premises on any account, as I would prosecute the first person I found taking any thing, having been a loser before. I had occasion to take the prisoner before a Justice, for leaving his work (I think on the 1st of September) and after that some discoveries were made. I had had no suspicion of him before. I had had things hinted to me, but I did not know the person.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had not the young man been some time in your employ? A. Yes - he employed a person under him, whom he turned away, but I did not oblige him to do so; I believe he did so by keeping part of his money back - he told me he turned him away because he was not able to earn the money he charged me for him. I had not requested two men to take the prisoner's place, and said I would turn him away before I had him before a Magistrate - I there charged him with leaving his work; the case was heard by the Magistrate - I believe it was not dismissed. Mr. Twyford asked me, when I went again, whether I would go into the higher charge. I swear I never heard the Magistrate say "I don't like your turning round in this manner, and preferring another charge when you find the first won't do." I saw Mr. Harmer there. The prisoner went to Clerkenwell for some days, and was then admitted to bail. When I went to make the first charge against him I did not know of this. I think he left my service on the 29th of August, and I think I made the charge on the 2d of September, but I cannot be certain, unless I had my book here. John Provan had left my service about three weeks before Russell did. I did not state at the office that Provan had told me Russell had robbed me - I said he had hinted that some one was robbing me, and I thought it was Russell.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you employ Provan, or did Russell? A. He did; I had nothing to do with him at all, but to pay him his bills. I had no distinct information as to carry conviction home to the prisoner, before I took him to the Magistrate. I gave directions to have my property better watched, and it was after that I received this information. Kearney is the person I set to watch.
JOSEPH CLIMPTON. I was employed at Mr. Trott's building by the prisoner, to do task work for him, and when my master and he fell out, master set me to work after he left him. I saw the prisoner and the apprentice take away fifteen or sixteen of the mitre strings of the stair-case, at breakfast time, one day in August last, but I cannot recollect when. I sat on the bench, eating my breakfast at the time, but I did not say any thing, as I thought they were fire wood. On the next day, which was a Wednesday, Mr. Trott sent me to work in Aldermanbury. I did not tell what I saw till the prisoner was in custody.
JURY. Q. You are a carpenter - did you ever know an instance in which the carpenter was prevented from taking such pieces away as chips? A. No - I have done it myself hundreds of times, and never was denied. I come here to tell the truth.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you ever do so when you had received an express order not to do it? A. No, of course I did not.
THOMAS TROTT re-examined. Q. What was the charge you gave to the prisoner? A. To take care of every thing, and work up all the stuff, and not let a chip be taken away, as I would prosecute any one who did. I told him I meant that nothing should be taken away, and I would prosecute him as well as another who took it.
MR. PHILLIPS to JOSEPH CLIMPSON. Q. Were these little bits of any use but as fire-wood? A. No.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Were they not of use to Mr. Trott? A. Yes, as fire-wood. I would not give a halfpenny for three of them. I never saw the prisoner do it but once; there was no person present but him and me.
EDWARD KEARNEY. I was employed by Mr. Trott to watch his premises, the week after Russell left. I had had particular orders for two years if I saw any body take any thing away to let him know - this was generally known on the premises.
Cross-examined. Q. Would you not have lit your pipe with a chip? A. I do not know. I had orders to take the chips home to Trott's, as fire-wood.
JOHN PROVAN. I was employed by the prisoner on these buildings for about a month; I have heard Mr. Trott mention in Russell's hearing, that no chips or any thing was to be taken away.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-315]
London Cases - Third Jury.

1543. RICHARD TAYLOR was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM GIBBS ROGERS. I live in Church-street, Soho - the prisoner was my porter - he was intrusted to receive money for me. On the 28th of July, between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, I sent him with a cheque for 17 l., on the house of Fry and Co., to pay an acceptance of mine for 15 l., to Mr. Evans, a gold refiner, in Little Love-lane, and bring me the change. He was to act at his own discretion, either to get the cheque changed by Mr. Evans, or at the bankers. In the course of the day I received a letter, but I do not know who wrote it. I did not see him again till he was in custody, about six weeks afterwards.
JOSEPH THACKERY. I am a clerk in Messrs. Fry and Chapman's. I paid the cheque for 17 l. on the 28th of July. I do not remember the person of the prisoner.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-320]
1544. JOHN MITCHELL was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of September, a lace veil, value 8 s., the goods of Jane Elizabeth Wilson, from her person.
JANE ELIZABETH WILSON. I am single, and live in Antelope-gardens, Holywell-mount. On the 6th of September, between one and two o'clock in the morning, I was returning from Smithfield with two young men and three young women - between Cloth-fair and Bartholomew Church, three young men passed me; the prisoner then passed me, and I felt my veil torn from my head; I turned round and said "Stop thief! I am robbed;" the officer then had hold of him, and saw him take it. I have never seen it since.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What colour was it? A. Black. I had been to Holborn, to a singing party; I had drank something; Emma Weightman was with me, and was as sober as I was; I did not see who took it; I had passed the evening at the King's Arms, between Hatton-garden and Ely-place; we had had three sixpenny glasses of gin and water - it was the first day of the Fair.
GEORGE PROCTOR. I saw the prisoner at two o'clock that morning, between Bartholomew Church and Cloth-fair; he snatched something from this lady's bonnet, but I did not see what; she turned round and said "I am robbed;" I said "I know you are;" I pursued the prisoner and took him about twenty yards off - he had nothing - there were three others with him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ever ask one Jones if the business could be compromised at all? A. No; I never said I should get 10 l. if this man could be convicted.
EMMA WEIGHTMAN. I was with the prosecutrix; she had a veil on her bonnet; I did not see the prisoner take it, because there was a crowd, but I saw him tucking a black lace veil into his bosom; I heard Proctor say "I have got the man."
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I go to service in general, but am out of a situation. I live in Fore-street; I had been at the King's Arms with Miss Wilson - I never told Mrs. Hoyle that the veil was a white one; I slept at a friend's that night; I did not return home till ten o'clock in the morning; I was sober when I went home - my landlady did not turn me out on account of my drunkenness. I never told her I should get 5 l. by it; I state, positively, I never had any conversation with Proctor, in the presence of Mrs. Jones, on this business.
MARY ANN CLINCH. I was with those two young women. I saw the prisoner run by me, and put something into his bosom, but I do not know what.
The Prisoner's Counsel called.
EDWARD JONES. I am a constable of St. Bartholomew the Great. I knew Emma Weightman when she was young, but I can not speak of her character now; I should doubt whether she is to be believed; I saw her and Proctorat Guildhall, and Proctor called me out, to know if they could not compromise matters; I said they might have made proposals before they got there, but not afterwards, I am certain he said that - and Emma Weightman heard it, but she said nothing; I said I had no doubt the man was innocent, and there should be no compromise - I had seen Weightman and Proctor in conversation several times.
GEO. PROCTOR re-examined. Q. Did you ask this question of Jones? A. Yes, after some proposals from his friends.
ANN HOYLE. I was Weightman's landlady. She came home the next morning at ten o'clock, and looked quite stupid; I said "You have not been in bed all night;" she said she had; I said "Look in the glass; I am sure you have not;" she changed her clothes, and said she had been with some friends, and was then going to Guildhall, for a young woman, who was with her, had lost a veil; there was a young woman there at the time; I saw her speaking to Proctor in the court, and heard her say the man was a stranger to her, and she would get paid for it; why should she lose her time - I turned her away when she had been but a fortnight with me - I do not know how she gets her living.
MARY BELL. I lodge at Mrs. Hoyle's. I had some conversation with Weightman about this veil - she told me it was a white lace veil - the prosecutrix said it was black.
COURT. Q. When did Emma Weightman tell you this? A. On Tuesday afternoon, the 6th of September; they had been at the office that morning.
THOMAS MITCHELL. I am the prisoner's brother. I had some conversation with Proctor - he stated he expected to get ten pounds by this conviction. I was present when he asked Jones if it could not be compromised. Jones said No, the young man would be acquitted.
EDWARD JONES re-examined. Q. What made you so confident that he would be acquitted? A. Because I thought him innocent.
FREDERICK WEIGHTMAN. I am Emma Weightman's brother - she is a servant out of place - it is my painful duty to say that she is not a person fit to be believed on her oath - I could not believe her myself.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-321]
1545. JEREMIAH SULLIVAN, JOHN KEMP, and RICHARD BUTLER, were indicted for stealing, on the 22d of July, nine pairs of stockings, value 12 s., the goods of William Kipling.
JOSEPH WILSON. I am in the employ of Mr. William Kipling, haberdasher, of Cheapside. On the evening of the 22d of July, between seven and eight o'clock, I saw Kemp and Butler come into the shop - I was at the farther end of the shop, tying up a parcel - they asked to see some braces, and tendered a shilling to pay for them - the officer then came in and took them. Sullivan was brought in soon afterwards with these nine pairs of hose - I had seen them safe in the shop, within about a foot of the door - any body going by might have taken them.
Cross-examined by MR. CRESWELL. Q. Did the prisoners select a pair of braces? A. Yes, and paid a shilling for them - they were at the end of the shop - I was astonished when the officer came in and took them.
CHARLES HERDSFIELD. I am a City officer. On the evening of the 22d of July I saw the three prisoners in company in Leadenhall-street. I followed them to Mr. Kipling's shop. Butler had this bag in his hand. When they got to the corner of Queen-street they remained there some time - then went up Bow-lane and returned, and went into a shop three or four doors before they came to Mr. Kipling's. They then looked at another shop - a boy came out and looked at them. Butler and Kemp then went into Mr. Kipling's shop. Sullivan took the stockings from the door, and put them into the bag, which he rolled up under his arm, and crossed the road - I followed, and took him - I got assistance to secure him, while I took the other two.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go into the other shop which they went into? A. Yes, and they had bought there a pair of stockings. which they paid for - I searched Kemp and Butler, and found the stockings.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
SULLIVAN'S Defence. I was going to Fleet-market to meet my brother. I saw this parcel of stockings lying on the ground. I took them up, and put them into my bag. The officer came and took me. I had never seen them others before.
KEMP'S Defence. I met Butler, he said he was going to buy stockings, and asked me to go with him, which I did; he gave two shillings and sixpence for them. When we came out, I said, "You want a pair of braces, you have forgotten them;" we then went into this shop to buy them, and gave one shilling for them; while we were there the gentleman came and took us.
BUTLER'S Defence. I met Kemp at the corner of Castle-street, Whitechapel. I said I was going to buy stockings and braces. I went and bought the stockings, but forgot the braces till I got out of the shop. I then went into Mr. Kipling's to buy them, and was taken - but I know not for what.
SULLIVAN - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
KEMP - GUILTY. Aged 17.
BUTLER - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.

1546. EDWARD KNIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of August, a table spoon, value 10 s., the goods of Thomas White.
THOMAS WHITE. I am a carpenter. METCALF HOPGOOD. I am a silver-smith, and live in Bishopsgate-street. The prisoner brought this spoon to me, on Monday, the 1st of August, between three and four o'clock, for sale.
JOHN GREEN. I took the prisoner into custody. He said he would take me where the spoon was.
Prisoner. I did confess to the Lord Mayor that I took the spoon.

1548. BENJAMIN HUGHES was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of August, a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of Ann Garton and Eliza Garton.
JOHN FAREY. I am in the employ of Ann and Eliza Garton - they are hosiers, and live in Cheapside; this handkerchief is their property. On the 10th of August, it laid on the right hand counter, where we expose goods for sale. I did not miss it, till the officer brought in the prisoner about twelve o'clock.
DANIEL FORRESTER. On Wednesday, the 10th of August, I was going past the shop with a prisoner, and saw this lad come from the door with something yellow under his jacket; I went into the shop and said to the young man, "Have you sold a yellow handkerchief;" he said, No; I said, "If you will take care of my prisoner, I will go and see if I can get it;" I went to Bow-lane, and took the prisoner with this handkerchief under his coat.

1549. JOHN KIRKHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, a handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of Samuel Buckingham, from his person.
SAMUEL BUCKINGHAM. On the 12th of September, about twenty minutes before seven o'clock in the evening, I was walking down Holborn, an officer stopped me, and said I had been robbed of my handkerchief; I felt my pocket, and missed it, and saw the officer take it from under the prisoner's coat; I find he has a good character; his father is ill, and his mother in great distress.
FRANCIS KEYS. I am an officer. On the 12th of September, I saw the prisoner and two others in Gray's-inn-lane - I watched them for half an hour - when they got into Holborn, they followed the prosecutor, who was coming toward the City - one of them made an attempt at his pocket, but did not succeed; the other two were close to him - when they came to Mr. Thompson's wine vaults, I saw one of them take the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket, and give it to the prisoner - I seized him and found it on him.
The prisoner received an excellent character.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Strongly recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Fined 1 s. and Discharged.
