1550. SARAH KIRBY was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of August, a counterpane, value 3 s., the goods of Jacob Metzgar, in a lodging-room, in his dwelling-house.
ELIZABETH METZGAR. I am the wife of Jacob Metzgar, he lives in Long-lane, West Smithfield. The prisoner came and hired a ready furnished room of me; there was another woman with her; this counterpane was a part of the furniture; I missed it from the bed on the 20th of August, and told her of it - she said she did it through distress.
CHARLES SAUNDERS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Long-lane. This counterpane was pawned on the 20th of August, by the prisoner.
EDWARD JONES. I took the prisoner; I searched her lodgings, and found the duplicate of this counterpane, pawned in the name of Ann Kirby.
Prisoner's Defence (written). Three weeks before my apprehension, I nursed Mrs. Turner, who occupied the prosecutor's furnished lodging; she gave me the things to pawn - I did not know but they were her own; and all the duplicates were found upon her.
ELIZABETH METZGAR re-examined. Q. Did the prisoner say she pawned it for Ann Turner? A. No; she took the room.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18250915-326]
1551. SUSANNAH DOBELL was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of September, a coat, value 20 s.; a pair of trousers, value 18 s.; a waistcoat, value 4 s.; four gowns, value 10 s.; two handkerchiefs, value 6 s.; three pairs of stockings, value 3 s.; two shifts, value 4 s.; fourteen caps, value 6 s.; four frills, value 2 s.; five other handkerchiefs value 2 s.; five aprons, value 2 s., and a towel, value 6 d. the goods of Richard Williams.
RICHARD WILLIAMS. I am a labourer. The prisoner lodged in the same house with me, in Sea-coal-lane - she left on the 7th of September - all my clothes were missed - I have only seen two gowns since.
MARY WILLIAMS. I am the prosecutor's wife. The prisoner was in my room on the last day of Bartholomew fair, 1824. I was going out about six o'clock, and asked her to go up stairs; she said I will do no harm in your room. I went out, and returned in three quarters of an hour. I found the place stripped of every thing. I have seen some of the things since, and some of them are here now. I did not see the prisoner for twelve months - she then came down to Billingsgate. I asked her what she had done with my things - she said she had pawned them in Gray's Inn-lane.
THOMAS HAMMOND. I took the prisoner. She gave me a direction to Leather-lane, where I found there had been a suit of clothes pawned but they had been redeemed;
she admitted that the articles which are here were at High street, Borough, where I found them. I found this flowered gown at Barber and Jupps'.
DANIEL BROWN. I am a pawnbroker. I have a gown and a handkerchief, which were pawned with me - I do not know who by.
CHARLES HALL. I live at Messrs. Barber and Jupps. I produce a gown piece. I cannot swear to the person who pawned it.
Prisoner. I throw myself on the mercy of the Court.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-327]
1552. JAMES SHORT was indicted for that he, on the 8th of July, a mare, price thirty-five guineas, and another mare, price thirty-five guineas, the property of Edward Sherman, unlawfully, designedly, and feloniously did kill, to the great damage of the said Edward Sherman.
MR. BRODRICK conducted the prosecution.
JOSEPH WINDSOR. I am turned nine years of age (the witness appeared perfectly to know the nature of an oath) On the 4th of July, I was in the Bull and Mouth Inn, yard. I know the stable where the horses of the Oxonian coach stand - the prisoner has the care of that stable, it is under ground, and slopes down from outside the premises. I was in that slope on the 4th of July, about a quarter past four o'clock. I could see plainly into the stable - there was nobody there but the prisoner. I saw him go to the mare next but one to the window, and put the handle of the fork up and down in her body for a few minutes; he then went to the one next the window, which was lying down; he hit her, and made her get up; he then poked the handle of the fork up her body, and did it up and down. I called to Charles Ray, while the prisoner was doing it, and he saw it; there was a lad named Davis there sweeping the steps, and Ray went and told him - he came, and saw what was doing; soon after Davis's mother called him away, and Ray went away too. I stood in the ride, and saw the prisoner do the same then to a black mare; he came up and went to a privy, and then went away out of the ride. I went down to the stable in about half an hour afterwards, while the farrier was there. I saw the stall where the mare was which he had first served so - it was lying down, and its tongue hanging out. I saw the second mare sweating very much.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Had you not been there, and been told to go out? A. Yes, the foreman told me to go out. The prisoner did not whip me on the Sunday before - we did not say that in consequence of his whipping us we would have something done to him. I know it was the handle of a fork, because I saw the iron in his hand. The stable is dark, and I was at the top of the hill, a great deal higher than the prisoner. As you go down there is a grating in the floor of the coach-house. I have never seen candles used there in the day-time. I saw him poke the handle first into one, and then into another horse. It is a street where many people are passing - we did not speak to any people, but told Davis of it, and he would not believe it - this was while it was doing. I did not tell any person he was killing the horses - I did not know he was hurting them. I mean to say, upon my oath, that it went into the horses - it did not go under them - they were standing with their tails nearest to me - I did not see any blood come from them - I was too far off to see that - it went in at the back of the horses. I told Thomas Davis that Jem Short was doing something to the horses. I did not say he was running the fork into them, because I did not know he was hurting them - I thought he was doing them good - we did not call on any one to stop it.
Re-examined. Q. Could you see the people go by in the street? A. No, not till I got to the top of the hill.
COURT. Q. What did you tell Thomas Davis? A. It was Charles Ray that told him first. I said "Here is Short doing something to the horses," and he said "Gee up."
CHARLES RAY. My father is a watchmaker. On the day in question I went to the cistern to get a drop of water - it is opposite to the slope of the stable. Windsor called me to see what the man was doing. I went to the top of the ride, but did not go down. I plainly saw the prisoner in the act of pushing the handle of the fork into the body of the mare, which was second from the window. He had hold of one prong in one hand, and his other hand was on the handle, and he worked it backwards and forwards. I do not know how long he was with that mare. He then went to another mare next the window, and worked the fork backwards and forwards in her body - I am sure the handle of the fork was in the body of the mares. There was a boy of the name of John Davis sweeping the stairs, and I beckoned to him to come and see it - there was a man who asked what we wanted. I and John Davis went and told Thomas Davis what we saw - he was busy, and did not pay particular attention to it - he said "The fork, is he?" and went on with his work. I went and looked a bit longer, and then went home. I did not see any one else in the stable.
Cross-examined. Q. Did Windsor go to Thomas Davis? A. I do not know; I saw him standing at the top - I had seen the prisoner two or three days before; I was going to the cistern on the Sunday before, and he gave me a thump with a broom, but did not hurt me; I did not tell the people, who were going by, because I did not exactly know whether it was cruel or not, or I would. I mean, upon my oath, to say, I did not know that he must be injuring the horses; I told Thomas Davis "Here is Short putting the handle of the fork into the mare's body;" he said "Sure," and went on with his work; I did not call out to the prisoner; the stable is not very dark - it is light enough to see what any one is doing; I do not know of candles being used there in the day time; the light comes from two windows in the street, and some grating in the coach-house; I was the length of the stable from the light; there was no conversation between me and the other boys as to what we saw him doing; I am sure it went into the mare's body, under the tail; I was not there when he went away; I left Windsor and Davis there - I went home but did not tell any one about it.
JOHN DAVIS. I am sixteen years of age next July. I was at the Bull and Mouth, sweeping down the steps facing the Oxonian stables; Charles Ray called me - I went just to the beginning of the stable, and saw James Short with a pitch-fork in his hand, which he was forcing backwardand forward into the mare's body, which was the second from the window; we all came away together, and then went down again in about a minute; I saw my father there, when I came up a second time, cleaning a horse in the yard; the two boys came with me, and Charles Ray went and told my father - he called me to prove what he said.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not you think he was hurting the horses? A. No, we came up and laughed at it; I did not call to any one to stop it; I did not tell my father but the boys, Charles Ray and Joseph Windsor, told him. I do not know whether they went separately to him - my mother called me to go to my place.
THOMAS DAVIS. I am hostler at the Bull and Mouth. I was working in what is called the ride, over the Oxonian stable; my son and Ray came to me, and said something, but I did not go down to the stable then; I went in about an hour, when the farrier's man said something to me. I saw a mare lying dead in the second stall from the window, and the next one to her, under the window, was in a dangerous state; I took her out of the stable to Mr. Surman's; she was in a great perspiration; it is light enough in the stable to see without candles.
Cross-examined. A. Do you know the grating? A. Yes, it is not far from the place where the mares stood; I should think the face of a man could be seen distinctly from there - the stable is rather dark at all times - a candle is used sometimes in dark foggy days in the winter; a person could have seen a man at the distance of the end of the stable, in fine weather. I was told of it by two boys, but not by Windsor; Ray said the man was using the fork about the mare's body, but I did not believe it; he made me understand that the handle of the fork was in the mare's body.
JOHN STUBBING. I am one of the foremen of the stables. The proprietor is Mr. Edward Shearman. I went down into the Oxonian stables, and met the mare coming up in great agony; I found in the further stall but one, a mare lying dead; I do not think the two could be replaced for less than seventy guineas. I ordered them all up, as I thought it might be from heat; I went for Mr. Surman, our veterinery surgeon; he attended the mare, which was alive, but it died in about an hour - I saw a great quantity of blood.
Cross-examined. Q. Had not the prisoner returned himself to the stable? A. No. The constable came and took the fork from the stable; I know it to be Mr. Sherman's. It had been used by the prisoner; the handle appeared as if it had been wiped; the prisoner was generally away from two o'clock until about six, but that depends on circumstances.
THOMAS DAVIS re-examined. Q. Did you see the prisoner about the stable, at the time you received the information? A. No; not until the alarm was given about the death of the first mare. I had seen him in the morning, and again in the evening after he was taken; I do not know whether he had returned to the stable or not.
RICHARD BETRIDGE. I am hostler at the Bull and Mouth. On this afternoon I went for the prisoner, about five o'clock, in consequence of what was said about the horses. I found him on the bed at his lodgings, in Golden-lion-court. I said, "James Short, do you know one of your horses is dead?" He said, "You do not stand that, do you." I said, "It is very true." He said "It cannot be, surely, can it?" I then came out, and never saw him after I left the room; he did not appear to be drunk. I went to let him know of it as an old servant; no one sent me - he is generally away from the stables from two to six o'clock.
HENRY SURMON. I am a veterinary surgeon, and am employed at the Bull and Mouth. In the month of July, a blind mare was brought to my shop - I was not there at the time - she died in about an hour in great agony. I then saw another mare which was dead - I opened them, and found the bladders of each of them divided, and the urethra much inflamed, which no doubt would have been produced, by putting the handle of a fork up the urethra. It was from no disease, it must have been done by mechanical means - there was a great deal of blood inside.
GEORGE THOMPSON. I am a constable. I took the prisoner into custody on the 8th of July, between five and and six o'clock. When he was at the Compter, I found one of his shoes with the upper leather all bloody. I found a pitchfork in Mr. Stubbings's room, the handle was to a certain length stained with blood, but it had been wiped.
Prisoner's Defence. When Beteridge came to me, I got up and went down to the stable. I saw Stubbings there. I went immediately to the mare, and would not believe any thing of the kind; I put my hand on her, and during her plunging about, some of the blood came on my shoe. I knew nothing of it until I got to the court, and then one of the book-keepers was taking down the names - he looked down and said, "What is that on your shoe?" He took it off - and that was the way it came so. The stable was always open - any person might have gone in, there is no door to it. I never had a blemish in my character.
Three witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 50.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18250915-328]
1553. JOHN WARD was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, two needle work bell-pulls, value 30 s., the goods of John Danford.
RAYMENT PROCTER DANFORD. I am a fancy trimming manufacturer, and live in Leadenhall-street. On the morning of the 12th of September, while I was at breakfast, I saw the prisoner go out of my shop, with a paper parcel under his arm, wrapped up in an old newspaper - I followed him immediately, and brought him back with it; this is the parcel - it is the property of Mr. John Danford. I had seen it on the counter about ten minutes before.
JOHN OVENDON. I am the officer. Mr. Danford gave me these bell-pulls. I took the prisoner into custody.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. A man came up to me, and said he would give me 2 d. to go and get the parcel off the counter, and if any body said any thing to me to say it was my brother's; I went - this gentleman came out, and took me. The man who sent me went away.
GUILTY . Aged 14.
Judgment Respited.

1555. WILLIAM HODGES was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, a printed bound book, value 6 s., the goods of William Simpkin and Richard Marshall, his masters.

CHARLES PAYNE. I am a pawnbroker. The prisoner came with this book to pawn, and having a suspicion of him, I asked him where he got it from; I forget what answer he made, but I said I should stop him - he ran away; I pursued, and took him - he then said he lived with a Mr. Robinson, in Paternoster-row.
ABRAHAM GRIFFITH. I took the prisoner, and found several duplicates upon him. I did not hear any thing of this book.

1556. WILLIAM HODGES was again indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, thirty-two printed bound books, value 5 l., the goods of William Simpkin and Richard Marshall, his masters.
MARK LOCKWOOD. I know these books to be the property of Messrs. Simpkin and Marshall - we have had a number of them so bound - I do not know how many; I do not know of any of them being missed in March last.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. Have you any private mark upon them? A. No. There are a great number of this description sold.
CHARLES PAYNE. Two parcels of these books were pawned at our house, by the prisoner - these two were pawned on the 25th of June, for 4 s. I shewed them to Messrs. Simpkin and Marshall, who identified them as their property - they have not been out of my possession. Here are three books pawned, and six more, which I bought of him at different times.

London Cases, First Jury, Before Mr. Serjeant Arabin.
1557. HENRY SUMNER was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of September, twenty-six yards of floor-cloth, value 4 l. 14 s. 6 d., the goods of James Webb Southgate and Jeremiah Mitchell.
ROBERT SPARROW. I am in the employ of Messrs. James Webb Southgate and Jeremiah Mitchell, of Fleet-market. Last Thursday night I was down in the cellar with three of our men - we heard some one in the shop; I went up, and found the prisoner, who was a stranger; he had got the roll of floor cloth from the place where it was, and removed it about half a yard - he was endeavouring to put it on his shoulder; I closed the gate which had been left open, and asked what he was going to do; he then begged hard for mercy, and said he had come from Birmingham, and left his parents.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. Might he not as well have thought of putting St. Paul's upon his shoulder. A. I should think not quite; he said, before the Magistrate, that he came by the desire of a friend, to make a purchase of some floor cloth; the roll consisted of twenty-seven yards - he was endeavouring very hard to get it on his shoulder; it is a load for any person, it weighs about one hundred pounds - it would have been madness for him to have attempted to carry it.
Q. How long had you been in the cellar? A. About a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes - it was safe when I went down, and when I came up, he was attempting to put it on his shoulder.
THOMAS BARKER. I am one of the City police. I have had this cloth in my care.
JEREMIAH MITCHELL. I am in partnership with Mr. Joseph Webb, Southgate. This is our property - there is eighty-one feet of it; it is worth four guineas and a half.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it not very heavy? A. Yes, it is not likely, that a lad in a week state of health, could carry it.
Prisoner. I went into the shop, and when the man asked me what I was going to do, it confused me very much.
THOMAS BARKER re-examined. Q. Did not the prisoner state, when the charge was made, that he wished to be taken to a friend, for whom he had looked at this oilcloth? A. No, at that shop, he said nothing, but when we got to Mr. Mitchell's, he said he hoped he would forgive him, and he wished to speak to him in private, but Mr. Mitchell said, "What you have to say, say;" he then said, I have been obliged to abscond from my friends at Birmingham, through having a bad disorder, and I wanted to get something that I might go home to them."
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-333]
1558. JOHN WILKINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of July, six pieces of printed cotton, containing one hundred and sixty-eight yards, value 10 l., the goods of John Bate.
CATHERINE BATE. I am the wife of John Bate, and live in Fore-street, Cripplegate - he is a haberdasher and draper. On the 22d of July, about two o'clock in the afternoon, I saw the prisoner with two other men about the house, and sometimes at the corner of Basinghall-street - I continued to watch them till about five o'clock, I was then about to leave the shop to go to the parlour, when I turned my head and saw the prisoner taking the cotton - I lost six pieces; I ran into the street, and saw him endeavouring to cover it with his apron - he turned up Moor-lane; I afterwards saw a gown on the back of the woman he lives with, made of some of the same cotton - he was taken on the 2d of August - when he committed the robbery, he had a fustian coat like a shooting jacket on, and a leather apron with a bib to it. I had observed him about the house previously to that day.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. Were there not three who engaged your attention? A. Yes, the other two had blue coats on. I particularly observed the prisoner - he was dressed different to the others. I have not said that the gown I have seen is not to be bought in a shop in London. We buy our cottons in Queen-street, and I do not know how many thousand pieces of the same cottonmay be sold. I was the only person in the shop that day, and was not out the whole afternoon. I was watching the shop, as I have been robbed four times since Christmas, in a very audacious manner. I did not see the prisoner have a double-barrelled gun and a little pointer with him. We have not tried to make up this matter. I have not seen him but in custody.
COURT. Q. Are you correct in stating that you had seen the prisoner, and two others about your house in the course of that day? A. Yes - I said when the prisoner was taken up that he was the man.
JOHN TWEEDY. I am a constable. On the 1st of August I received information that Wilkinson was wanted. I went and searched his premises, but I found no jacket nor cotton. I found two leather aprons, and one has a bib to it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you find any close shooting jacket? A. No; I saw his father, who is a decent man.
MARGARET MARTHA EVANS. I live at No. 83, Fore-street. I saw the prisoner lurking about No. 82, with two other persons - he had a leather apron on like this, and a shooting jacket with pockets in it - I saw him turn round many times - I do not know whether he had trousers or breeches on - he had no gun - I had noticed him about there for a week before, in the same dress - I was called in by my grandmother at the time of the robbery, and did not see the cotton taken - I gave information of it the same afternoon - I saw the prisoner again on the 2d of August, in custody.
Prisoner. On the day of the robbery I was at work with my father. Her husband stated at the office that he had lost three pieces, and she comes and states she lost six - it is not likely I could steal one hundred and sixty-eight yards of cotton and run away with it.
CATHERINE BATES. The property was taken as near five o'clock as possible, on the 22d of July. I did not see the man who took it till he was apprehended - the apron was not then produced.
JURY. Q. How was the cotton taken? A. He came into the shop, and took it from a pile at the right hand of the shop - they were tied flat and close in different ties, squeezed tight on one another.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-334]
1559. JAMES HAYNES was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of September, a wooden box, value 6 d.; thirty shillings, twenty sixpences, two hundred and forty penny pieces, and four hundred and eight halfpence, the property of John Jones.
JOHN JONES. On the 7th of September I was with an exhibition of wild beasts, at Bartholomew Fair ; I had a box containing 2 l. in shillings and sixpences, and some copper; I did not miss it till I was called to, between one and two o'clock in the morning, by a man who had the prisoner by the collar; he asked if it was not my box; I said it was; I had left it locked in my bed-room, in a tilted caravan; when I saw it it was broken on the side - some of the money was gone, but I do not know how much - the prisoner was a stranger.
RICHARD HUNTER. I am the proprietor of the exhibition. I saw the prisoner, about two o'clock in the morning, going from under the caravan; he went under the next van; I went and asked him what he wanted he said to ease himself; I saw him stoop down and pick up the box - he said some chaps had given it to him; I then inquired of my neighbours if they had lost any thing, and Mr. Jones owned the box.
GEORGE ADAMS. I took the prisoner, and have the box - it contains 3 l. 7 s., and there were 10 s. in his pockets, or in the crown of his hat.
(Box produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I had sold my fruit at the Fair - the money I had was what I had taken for it.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18250915-335]
1560. ISABELLA WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of July, three yards and a half of kerseymere, value 25 s., the goods of Thomas Hester.
MARY HESTER. I am the wife of Thomas Hester, who is a woollen-draper, and lives in Cloth-fair. The prisoner came to our shop about four o'clock in the afternoon of the 22d of July - there was another woman with her - they wanted a waistcoat pattern; the other woman went to the outside of the window, and pointed to some which I shewed them. I then missed a piece of kerseymere from the counter, which had been lying about half way on it. I do not think the prisoner moved from the counter while the other went out. I felt timid, and did not say any thing to them; they went out of the shop without buying any thing, and I followed; they got to a turning, and I saw the piece of kerseymere at the bottom of the prisoner's gown. I caught hold of her with one hand, and drew it from her; she said "I picked it up." - I was very much confused, and do not know whether I said any thing to her or not.
GEORGE HADDON. I am an officer. I took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going out to market and met a slight acquaintance, who asked me where I was going; she said she was going to buy a bit of stuff for a waistcoat, where could she get it. I said, in Cloth-fair. When we got to the shop, the lady was playing with an infant on the floor - my child was cross, and the young child on the floor attracted my attention, and I played with the two children. In the mean time I suppose it was the other young woman who took the piece; the lady pursued us, and the young woman dropped the parcel at our feet. I had but one hand at leisure, and could not have concealed it, if I had been disposed.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-336]
1561. MARY MOULD was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of September, twenty-two yards of printed cotton, value 30 s., the goods of William Richard Town.
WILLIAM BATES. I am shopman to Mr. William Richard Town, of Bishopsgate without. Between eleven and twelve o'clock in the morning of the 6th of September, I was at the bottom of the shop, waiting on a customer; a woman came to the door and cried out -"Come forward, a woman has stolen a piece of cotton from the door." I ran forward, and saw the prisoner with this cotton under her arm, and part of it hanging down. I brought her back with it.
SARAH WALTON. I was going along Bishopsgate-street, and saw the prisoner take the cotton from the side of Mr. Town's door, and put it under her arm; a friend of mine, who was with me, went into the shop, and gave an alarm.
SAMUEL SHEPHERD. I am an officer, and have had the custody of the prisoner.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. Last Tuesday week I was passing down Bishopsgate-street, and saw this cotton laying under the confectioner's shop - I took it up, and this young woman said, "Let it alone;" I said I should not; it was as free for one as another - she then came out, and took it up - by the appearance that young woman is the person who took it.
GUILTY. Aged 46.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-337]
1562. ANN MULLINS was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of July, three yards of linen cloth, value 7 s., the goods of Robert Graham, her master.
MARGARET GRAHAM. I am the wife of Robert Graham, a licensed victualler - we live in Gutter-lane, Cheapside. The prisoner was in our service about three weeks. My daughter had cut off some cloth for her brother's shirts, and left the rest on the floor in the dining-room.
MARY GRAHAM. I am the prosecutrix's daughter. I had cut off some linen for my brother's shirts; I do not know what quantity, but I saw that some had been cut off, by its being cut and folded differently to the way I left it - I then looked for a pin which I had left in it, for the length of the sleeves - that was gone, and a corner was cut off; there were about three yards of the cloth gone. My mother then inquired of the prisoner, and the other servant, who both denied it; my mother then said, "To end all dispute, you had better produce your boxes;" the other servant opened her's first, and then the prisoner opened her's and took the piece of cloth in her hand; my mother said, "What is that?" the prisoner said it was not ours, an Irish woman, a friend of her's, had brought it to her, but she could not tell her name, nor where she lived. I found it was the piece which had the pin in it, and I am certain by the length and width of the sleeves that it had been cut from the piece which I had left.
WILLIAM SHEPHERD. I am the officer. I have the cloth - here are three yards of it.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. When my mistress missed this linen, she said I should show her what was in my box - I opened it, and the first thing she did was to knock me down.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18250915-338]
1563. ELIZABETH BLACKLOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of July, three pairs of sheets, value 34 s.; four handkerchiefs, value 14 s.; three shifts, value 10 s.; three shirts, value 14 s; two towels, value 1 s. 6 d.; a napkin, value 1 s.; a night-gown, value 1 s.; five tablecloths value 18 s., and a gown, value 8 s., the goods of Frederick Boulton.
HARRIET BOULTON. I am the wife of Frederick Boulton, a publican, who lives at Whitefriars' New-wharf. The prisoner lived with me as a servant. I missed some articles on the 17th of July, and sent for an officer.
SAMUEL BEAVAN. I am a constable of Whitefriars. I was sent for, and found several duplicates upon the prisoner.
JOHN FLOWERS. I am shopman to Mr. Cotterel, pawnbroker, of Shoe-lane. I have several articles of table linen, some sheets, gowns, and shirts, pawned by the prisoner, at different times.
JOSHUA MORTIMER. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Fleet-market. I have eleven separate articles, partly pawned by the prisoner.
WILLIAM HOLDING. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Fleet-market. I have a handkerchief, pawned for 3 s., by a person in the name of Ann Smith. I am not certain whether it was by the prisoner.
THOMAS LINSCOT. I am a pawnbroker. I have a handkerchief, pawned on the 11th of July, in the name of Ann Hawkins.

1564. JULIA MULLINS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of July, a half-sovereign, and a shilling, the monies of David Hay, from his person.
DAVID HAY. I am a harness-maker, and work for Mr. Parry, of Moorfields. On the 9th of July, about twelve o'clock at night, I was in Houndsditch - I was quite sober, and was going home; the prisoner spoke to me, took hold of my hand, and pulled me quite round - she asked where I was going; I said I was going home: she asked me to go with her, but I would not - she then put her hand into my pocket, and took it out again; I saw her put something into her other hand; which I took hold of, and felt my pocket with my other hand - I missed a half-sovereign and a shilling. I called the watchman, and as she was going to the watch-house she dropped the half-sovereign upon the flags; a young man picked it up; she dropped the shilling a little further on.
WILLIAM BRAND. I am a shoemaker. I was on my way home, and heard the prosecutor call the watchman - he gave charge of the prisoner for having robbed him of 11 s. - the watchman took her by the wrist, and she dropped the half-sovereign; I picked it up - she dropped the shilling soon after, which the prosecutor picked up.
WILLIAM TOOBY. I am a watchman, and live in Houndsditch. The prosecutor called me, and said he was robbed of a half-sovereign and a shilling - I took hold of the prisoner's hand, and led her a few paces. I saw her hand fall, and the half-sovereign fell, and the shilling soon afterwards - I have no doubt that she dropped them.
Prisoner. They are swearing my life away.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-340]
1565. ANN HERITAGE was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, a half-crown, two shillings, and sixpence, in copper, the goods of Thomas Beaumont, from his person.
THOMAS BEAUMONT. I am a solicitor for periodical publications - I am married. On the night of the 11th of August I was in Smithfield, between eleven and twelve o'clock; I had been drinking, and might be a little theworse for liquor; the prisoner, who was a stranger, met and asked me to give her something to drink, which I did; we went to Bride-lane, and had something more - I had porter in both places, and she had spirits; when I came out of the White Bear public-house, in Bride-lane, I had occasion to turn down a court - she followed me - she pulled me about, and my waistcoat was open, and while we were there the watchman desired us to walk on; I was going on when he came and took me to the watch-house. I found I had lost 5 s. which I had seen safe in my left-hand pocket when I left the White Bear, in her company. I stated to the watchman that I had been robbed; he went in search of the girl and she was brought in a little time afterwards; I saw the half-crown taken from her bosom, and some copper - there was no mark on it, but I know I had it while I was in her company.
Prisoner. You gave me the half-crown at Mr. Wood's, the market-house. Witness. I never gave her a farthing; it was mere necessity that took me into the court - I could not get away from her - I treated her to get rid of her.
HENRY BOLTON. I was watch-house-keeper. The procutor was brought in, and made this charge against the prisoner - I was not present when she was searched, but I saw her kneel and take something from a corner of the room and put it into her bosom.
JOHN BRADY. I was on duty and heard a talking down the court - not quarrelling; I went to see what was the matter, and saw the prosecutor there, and the prisoner had hold of his waistcoat which was wide open - he had a bundle in his hand - I told them to go on about their business - I did not know what was in the bundle - I took the prosecutor to the watch-house - he then told me of the girl having robbed him; I pursued her and brought her in - the prosecutor had been drinking but knew what he was about - she said she had but 9 d., but the officer found 2 s. up in a corner.
THOMAS ARNOLD. I am a constable. I sent the watchman for the prisoner; she stated that she had been in Holborn, and received 1 s. 6 d. from a gentleman; I found 2 s. 4 d. in her pocket, of which she could give no account; I then put her aside into another room, and when we went to let her out the watchman saw her pick up half-a-crown; and three half-pence I saw her take from her bosom.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-341]
OLD COURT.
EIGHTH DAY. FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 23.
Middlesex Cases, Second Jury. Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1566. EDWARD BITTON was indicted for feloniously assaulting Elizabeth Panting, on the King's highway, on the 14th of September, putting her in fear, and taking from her person and against her will, a purse, value 6 d.; two crowns, and thirteen half-crowns, the property of Joseph Panting .
ELIZABETH PANTING. I am the wife of Joseph Panting, who is a labourer in the East India House. On the 14th of September, about three o'clock in the afternoon, I was in Church-lane, Whitechapel, carrying a basket with a purse in it, containing two crown pieces, and thirteen half-crowns; several persons came up, and crushed me very hard - they surrounded me, and crushed me up against a shop window. I cannot say how many there were - there were two before me, and the prisoner was one of them, on my right hand. I cannot say how many were behind. I said to the prisoner,"Why the deuce do you push so?" He made no answer - he was pushing very hard - my basket was pushed down by my side - I still held it - they left me. I immediately missed my purse with its contents from the basket - it could not have got out, without being taken out. I ran after the prisoner directly, he turned the corner of Union-street. I laid hold of him, and said "You villian! you have robbed me of my purse." - He had got about six yards from me. He said, "You have mistaken me?" He took his hat off, and wished his eyes might roll out into his hat, if he had taken it. I said "It is of no use young man making such oaths, for I am sure you are one of those who robbed me." He said, "He had not got my purse." I said, if not, his companion had. I gave him in charge. One of his companions ran on before him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Would you call me the companion of any body in Court, because I am near them? A. No; but he was with them pushing me. I kept him talking for five or ten minutes, until the officer was fetched; a crowd was collected - he could not have got away.
WILLIAM FRYER. I am a labourer to the East India Company. I was in Church-lane, and saw the prisoner coming towards me in a great bustle. I had a bag under my arm - he nearly pushed me off the curb-stones. - I turned round, and saw him forcibly pressing this woman against the shop window. I am certain he is the person. On my looking that way, the prisoner and another came from the woman - but the prisoner was the only man I saw about her - they came walking along very fast, and presently the prosecutrix said to the other "Go it." Immediately after that, the prisoner come after them - the other ran away as fast as he could. She caught hold of the prisoner's coat, and said, "Stop him! you villain you have robbed me." He denied it. I said, "You are one of the party." I saw you hustling her against the shop window. He took off his hat, and wished his eyes might drop out into it, if he knew any thing of the other man; he was secured, he could not have got away.
Cross-examined. Q. He made no attempt to get away? A. No, I never took my eye off him till the prosecutrix took hold of him, and did not see him give any thing to any body; he was the only person I saw near her, his back was towards me - nothing was found on him; I was three or four yards from the woman - plenty of people were passing - he ran about six yards before she seized him.
MOSES FORTUNE. I am an officer, and received the prisoner in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. I was passing by a crowd a jeweller's shop. this good woman came and took hold of me, and said, I had her purse - I said not; she then said,my companion had; this man came up and said, "There was a man with you" - I heard you say, "Go on."
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Of stealing from the person only.
Transported for Life.

Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1568. WILLIAM SIDNEY SMITH was indicted for feloniously assaulting Charles Waters, and with a certain sharp instrument, cutting and maiming him with intent to murder him.
TWO OTHER COUNTS, stating his intent to be to disable or do him some grievous bodily harm.
CHARLES WATERS. I keep the Three Tuns public-house, Brook-street, Holborn . On the 16th of July, between eight and nine o'clock at night, the prisoner and several others were in my tap-room - several bricklayers and labourers were there - one of them made a complaint - the prisoner at that time had his head down on his hat, apparently asleep; I roused him, and told him a man had lost his handkerchief, and they had seen him take it; I insisted on having it - he said, he had not got it; one of the labourers said, he thought he had given it to another man - I insisted on his leaving - he got further on to the box - I went to turn him out by force, and he struck me. I struck him again - my wife and servants came and moved the table from the box, and I lifted him out of the box - he stood up in the middle of the room; I changed my hand, and caught him by the left hand collar with my right hand, to lead him to the door - he instantly threw himself down on his back, and began hitting me; I stood over him, holding his collar; I did not know that he had a knife, but he stabbed me three times in my arm - people called out, "He is killing you, he is stabbing you;" I then saw the blood running from my arm; I let go of him, and went to the doctor's - he was not sober, but knew what he was about.
The Court ruled, that as, if death had ensued, this offence would not have been murder, it did not come within the meaning of the Statute. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-344]
Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1560. EDWARD MASON, WILLIAM CROOK, ANN GABLE, and SAMUEL CROOK; were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, at Christ-church, fourteen pieces of manufactured silk, containing in length five hundred yards, value 150 l, the goods of Thomas Waterlow, the younger, and George Sawyer, in the dwelling-house of the said Thomas Waterlow.
Messrs. ADOLPHUS and LAW conducted the prosecution.
JOHN CHAPPELL. I am apprentice to Messrs. Waterlow and Sawyer - the prisoner Samuel Crook was also an apprentice, and slept up stairs. On the 12th of September, about seven o'clock at night, he went out, and said he was going for a candlestick; they were kept in an another house; he came home about twenty or ten minutes before ten o'clock. I opened the door to him - he came up stairs, and sat down - he then went down - called the dog down stairs, and went out with it; he stopped out about five minutes - I opened the door to him; I did not see the dog come in with him, for the light went out. I had not seen the dog go out, but I did not see it after he called it down, till half-past eight o'clock the next morning - it then came up the court - it was outside the house. I fastened the shutters before he came in; Beechey was in master's employ, and held me a light while I fastened them. I went up stairs to bed, and fell asleep while Samuel Crook was undressing himself, and cannot say when he came to bed - but when I awoke in the morning he was in bed. It was twenty minutes before seven o'clock when I went down stairs, he was then in bed - I was the first person down stairs. I found the warehouse door wide open - it had been locked the night before, and the key left in a room under where we slept, in a table-drawer near the window. I had put both the warehouse key and the key of the door facing it there - the back door was open - but not the street door. I ran up, and called out "Samuel - all the house is wide open;" he asked if it was true? I said "It was" - and he said he should have all the blame of it. He put on his stockings and trousers, and came down with me. I found the counter-doors open, and a piece of umbrella silk laid on it; the slides of the counter had been broken open - and the desk also. I told him I thought he had taken the dog out, and if he had not taken it out, it would not have been done. The dog used to sleep by the warehouse-door, or up stairs; he said he had not taken it out; that it went down stairs.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Whether the dog went out with him or not you cannot tell? A. No; we generally go to bed about ten or eleven o'clock - we both went up to bed together, and he brought the light down - one of us always bring the light down. I was undressed and in bed before he came up again - he sat on the bed, and began to pull off his clothes - we both slept in the same bed. I did not awake till morning, and then got up directly. I found him in bed when I awoke, and had not felt him get out in the night. We work in the bed-room in the day time making silk; any person belonging to the house goes in and out of the room in the day time.
MR. LAW. Q. Did you hear him call the dog down as he went out? A. Yes; he was not in the habit of calling it down. I generally took the light down, but he said he would take it down that night. I said I would take it; he said No - he would take it. I had it in my hand to take down - he took it out of my hand.
JURY. Q. Were you going to take it down before he was undressed? A. Yes; he was making the bed, and when the bed is made, the light is taken down. I never knew the dog absent on any other night; I saw it come in next morning at the back door, which was open an hour after I got up. I locked the warehouse door, just at dusk; the dog was in doors then - the key was up stairs.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You found the back door open; the dog might have gone out there in the night if it was open? A. Yes.
JAMES BEECHEY. (the witness appeared perfectly to understand the obligation of an oath.) I am between eleven and twelve years old, and am draw boy to theprosecutors. I lighted Chappell while he fastened the door up, on Monday night, the 12th. On the Friday before that Samuel Crook wanted me to go to Mr. Mason's, two or three doors up White's-row, Spitalfields, and told me to ask Mr. or Mrs. Mason at what public-house he could meet his brother Bill, on the Saturday night, or Sunday morning. I was going, but Chappell refused my going.
JOHN CHAPPELL re-examined. I did not go to the drawer in the morning for the keys, as I found the doors open.
MARY ANN FOX. I am single, and live at No. 22, White's-row, on the second floor; Mason lives on the first floor - it is the third door up White's-row. On the Monday evening, previous to the robbery, between six and seven o'clock, I saw the prisoner Samuel Crook there - he asked me if Mr. Mason was there. I said, I did not think that he was - he went up stairs; he had a fustian jacket and trousers similar - they were light. (looking at a jacket) I saw him in that jacket.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he not dressed in the blue coat he has on now? A. No, he had a fustian jacket. I get my living by wharping canes for the weavers.
SARAH CARKETT. I am the wife of Hector Carkett, we live at No. 22, White's-row, on the second floor. On the Friday morning before the robbery, a little after seven o'clock, I opened the street door, and saw the prisoners William and Samuel Crook - they came in and followed me up stairs; one of them knocked at Mason's door, and called Ned - he was let in, and when the door was unfastened I went up to my own apartment, and soon after heard a Christian name called, but what I do not know. I looked into the street out of the window, and saw Samuel Crook come from the step of the street door, and stand under the window. William Crook soon after came from the door, and joined him; and soon after Mason came up fastened his neck handkerchief round his neck, and all three went away together - I watched them altogether as far as I could see. On Monday night, the officers came to the house and took Mason, and part of the property. Barrs, the officer, told me to mind the door of Mason's room. I went down to the street door, then returned with a candle in my hand, and was going to close Mason's door, and saw William Crook sitting in the room. I said "Oh! there you are, are you." I then double locked the door on him, and told Barrs.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the Crooks before? A. I had repeatedly seen them, but not to know their names. Mason is a braca maker, not a weaver.
HECTOR CABKETT. About eight o'clock on the Friday morning, previous to this robbery, I saw the prisoner, Samuel Crook, in fustian jacket, with a roll of black silk; he went up stairs, and came down in about two minutes without it.
THOMAS ALMOND. I am inspector of the watch, and live in Fashion-street, Spitalfields. On the Monday, the night of the robbery, about a quarter past twelve o'clock, I and Barrs were in White's-row; I observed two men coming down White's-row; they passed the door, talking, then returned, and went into the house - it is where Carkett lodges. I went into the house, knocked at the door, and was answered by the female prisoner; I said, there were two men come into that house; she said there was not. I said I was confident of it - that I had seen one of them come out again, and there must be one there then; I asked her for a light - she assisted me in searching the lower part of the house; I found nobody there. Barrs was stationed there - he called me, and said there was a man coming out of the first floor window. I ran to the door, and saw a man getting in at the window - I believe it to be the prisoner Mason - we ran up to the first floor room; the window of which room I had seen the person at, and Mason stood there with the female prisoner - I laid hold of Mason. Barrs found some silk on the bed, and the moment it was found the female prisoner ran down, and escaped. I followed her - she was taken, and brought back. Next morning I went to Mr. Waterlow's premises, and examined the three outer doors - none of them appeared to have any marks of outward violence, nor inside, not on the doors - two of the slides under the counter, and a writing-desk, all had marks of violence on them - there were only two slides with locks, and they had marks of violence upon them; the two locks secured the whole four slides. I questioned Mr. Waterlow, and Samuel Crook was produced to me - I thought by his appearance he was the man I had seen go out of the house, in White's-row, and who had gone in with the other - he had neither coat nor hat on - I wished him to put them on; he went up, and did so. My opinion was then stronger that he was the person I had seen; I do not swear positively to him. I went up stairs to his room, and found a piece of iron behind his bed - I applied it to the marks upon the desk and slides - they corresponded particularly on the slides. I also found a pair of dirty shoes in his room - I asked him who they belonged to; he said they were not his, and afterwards that some person had given them to him; I found the jacket produced in the room.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you call that iron? A. I do not know what to call it - it is not a crow-bar; it is common for thieves to enter premises by false keys. I saw nobody let the men into the house in White's-row - the door was open - it was a quarter or twenty minutes past twelve o'clock at night. I was about twenty yards from them; I had never seen Samuel Crook before: there is a gas-light over the door. I am not positive of him; I am not certain whether the one who came out was the one who went in.
MR. PHILLIPS to JOHN CHAPPELL. Q. Did you see him clean his shoes when he came in that night? A. No; I saw the shoes there in the morning, when the bed was turned up.
JOHN BARRS. I am an inspector of the watch, and was with Almond. I observed a man standing over the rail of the sewer, which was open - he went away. I was stationed at the door of this house afterwards - when Almond was going up stairs, I observed the prisoner Mason coming out of the first floor window - I had a good view of him by the gas light, and am certain he is the man - I called Almond down; Mason then went at the window again, and broke a square of glass; I ran up and collared him in the room; Almond came and laid hold of him - I turned my head aside, and under the counterpane of the bed, I found a bundle of silk - took it to the watch-house, the prosecutors have since seen it and claimed it - the female prisoner ran away, but was taken, both were taken to thewatch-house - I returned to the house with a watchman, found the room door double locked - Mrs. Carkett unlocked it, and I found William Crook inside - I searched under the bed, and found seven more pieces of silk in a sheet - Crook was about three yards from me - he must have been under the bed, when we were taking Mason; there was no other hiding place. I went to the premises, no violence had been used to the outside of the doors - the desk and slides had been forced open, the iron corresponded with the marks on them.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you searched Mason's room, to see if any other man was concealed? A. After we took Crook, I did, but not at first; I had seen a man resembling Samuel Crook about the house by the sewer, but will not swear to him - I have had the care of the silk ever since, and have brought it here.
COURT. Q. Do you know Mr. Waterlow's house? A. Yes, it is in the parish of Christ-church, Middlesex, commonly called Spitalfields - Christ-church, Middlesex. is the correct name - I know of no other Christ-church, in Middlesex.
MR. THOMAS WATERLOW, JUN. I am in partnership with George Sawyer. We carry on business at No. 27, Church-street, in the parish of Christ-church, as silk-weavers - it is my dwelling-house, I live and sleep there, but not my partner. On this Monday night I slept out of town, and I returned about half-past nine o'clock, next morning - I had left fifteen shillings in my writing desk - that was gone - I missed seven hundred and sixty-eight yards of silk altogether, worth 150 l., and found the whole of it at the watch-house; it belongs to me and my partner - in consequence of what was told me, I called Samuel Crook down, and gave him in charge - I saw his hat on the table in the room under where he sleeps, near the window - the keys are kept in that table drawer - he had no business with the keys - he takes his supper in that room, they sup about twelve o'clock - the whole of the silk is mine, every piece is worth more than 40 s. - there are fourteen pieces, seven in each parcel - I know some by my private mark, some by the border and figure - they are worth from 8 l. to 12 l. a piece.
Cross-examined. Q. Is your father alive? A. Yes, he has no concern in the business - Mr. Sawyer lives some where at Islington - I pay rent and taxes; we have only been in business nine months - I shall have to make a charge for the warehouse.
Q. His hat might be in that room every night? A. He supped there - the table is not the place for his hat - the servant brought the keys to me. I have no doubt but they were found in the table drawer - I never saw his hat there before; I returned on Tuesday morning; I had left town about four o'clock on Monday afternoon.
GEORGE HUBBARD. About twenty minutes past twelve o'clock, I found a hat in the street - Mason claimed it at the watch-house.
MR. WATERLOW. This iron instrument is mine, it was used in the silk trade four years ago, but never at this house - the slides under the counter, where we kept the silk was broken open - there are bolts to the ware-house door, back and front, but no locks - they were all inside fastening - there is a lock to the street door, but not to the back door, that bolt is inside - the wash-house opens into the passage.
JOHN CHAPPELL re-examined. A female servant and an old woman, also slept in the house that night.
Five witnesses gave Mason a good character, and six deposed the same for Crooks.
MASON - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 21.
SAMUEL CROOK - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 26.
GABLE - NOT GUILTY.
WILLIAM CROOK - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-345]
Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1570. JOHN CLARK and HANNAH GALE were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Elizabeth Green, widow, about one o'clock in the night on the 1st of August, at St. Dunstan, Stepney, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, two pieces of crape, value 2 l.; four yards of calico, value 2 s.; a handkerchief, value 5 s.; two pairs of breeches, value 6 d.; two frocks, value 1 s.; two petticoats, value 1 s.; four yards of flannel, value 4 s.; a table-cloth, value 2 s.; eight napkins, value 3 s.; a tippet, value 2 d.; a purse, value 2 d.; four caps, value 1 s.; a pocket, value 4 d., and a half crown, the property of Maria short, widow .
ELIZABETH GREEN. I am a widow, and rent a house in Silver-street, Stepney. Mrs. Short lodges in the back room on my first floor. On the 1st of August, about half-past one o'clock in the morning, I was alarmed - it was moon-light, or it would have been dark. I got up, and found a ladder which is kept at the farther end of the yard placed under Mrs. Short's window - it was at the other end of the yard the night before.
MARIA SHORT. I am a widow, and lodge at Mrs. Green's, on the first floor. On the 1st of August, about twelve o'clock, I went to bed. I shut the window down, and put a gimblet into it, but whether it caught the outer sash or not I cannot say, but am certain it was quite shut down. I went to sleep, and was alarmed about half-past one - it was moon-light - I could see my black petticoat on the floor - I was laying on my right side, with my baby sucking at my left breast, and on opening my eyes I saw a man reaching inside the window. I saw him take some of my clothes out of my box - the window was open, and he was leaning within it - both his arms were in the room. I was so alarmed I could not call out - I endeavoured to kick my eldest boy, who laid at the foot of the bed, but could not awake him - he took the clothes out of my box, and threw them out of the window - after that I heard the window above stairs open - the man was then within my window - I then saw him go gradually down - I got out of bed, and gave an alarm - Mrs. Barns gave the first alarm. I went into the yard, and found the ladder under my window. I had seen him going down the ladder, and Mrs. Barns said, "There is a man in the yard." I noticed that he had a dark coat on, but cannot swear to his person. I first saw him in the yard, and afterwards saw him get over the wall into the field. I missed the articles stated in the indictment (enumerating them). I gave information of the robbery next morning, and went into the taproom of the Red Lion public-house, and saw a man sitting down with a yellow silk handkerchief round his neck. I came out, and said I was sure he was the man who had robbed me. I felt certain of it, by the appearance of his coat - it was a particularly long coat, and came down tothe calves of his legs - I noticed it as he stood in the yard, before he went to the wall - I thought it exactly like the coat, and consider that the handkerchief was mine, and one which I had lost that night. I am certain the prisoner is the man I saw in the public-house. I went down to the office, and met Foster, the officer. He went with me to the Red Lion, but he was gone then. I found him by the turnpike-gate, and pointed him out to Foster, who went up to him. He was taken to the Black Dog public-house, and my handkerchief taken off his neck by Foster. The prisoner Gale came into the public-house, and he said to her, "I believe all is right." Foster told Lea to follow her home. I followed her, and went up to her room. Lea let down the bed, and I found some of my flannel - it was a petticoat, partly made, and some calico - a child's tippet, and my purse, was found in her pocket. I had seen no woman about the house. Gale solemnly declared she knew nothing of the robbery, and said her husband gave her the purse.
MARY BARNS. I lodge in the room over Mrs. Short. On the 1st of August, about twelve o'clock at night, I opened my window, and heard the church clock strike twelve. After that, not finding myself very well, I struck a light, put it out again, and tried to go to sleep, but could not rest. I got up and opened my window again, and thought it was break of day, it was such a very moonlight night. The watchman called half-past one when I opened the window. I saw a man in the yard, and saw him go to climb over the wall - I gave an alarm of Thieves. He had on a dark coat, which seemed to hang low, and to be full behind, but it was not a great coat. He had a high crowned hat, which was narrower down at the brim.
WILLIAM FOSTER. I am an officer. On Tuesday, the 2d of August, in consequence of information, I went to look for Clark, and found him by Mile-end turnpike; I asked where he was on Monday night - he said at home - I asked what time he was at home; he said he could not say to an hour; I asked where he got the handkerchief which was round his neck - he said he bought it yesterday morning in Petticoat-lane for 1 s. 6 d. - I said he must go into the public-house, for I wished to search him; we went backward, into the parlour - I saw both his hands up to his neck, and when we got to the parlour door, he threw the handkerchief off his neck into the parlour, and said, "Go in there, and I will be with you directly - I want to go backward." I said "No, you must go in now;" he had the door half open - I called him: he said, "Let me go; I am taken very bad;" I took him into the parlour, and told Mrs. Short to look at the handkerchief; she exclaimed "That is my handkerchief, and what my husband wore when he died?" she nearly went into a fit; he said "Be quiet, my good woman, I dare say all things will be right;" I searched him and found a key, which, he said, was the key of his door; I asked where he lived; he said at No. 23, Easterfield-street, Stepney; I then went back into the yard with him, returned to the parlour, gave the key to Lea, and told him to go and search there. I took Clark to the office, and as I took him through the passages of the public-house, Gale stood there; he said something to her, and then said "It is all right;" I took him to the office; Lea brought Gale and some property there - the prosecutrix's house is in the parish of St. Dunstan's, Stepney.
JAMES LEA. On the 2d of August, between ten and eleven o'clock, in the morning, I saw Clark; Gale came running into the passage of the public-house, and said, "Where is my husband?" Foster took Clark away, but before he went they whispered together; Gale crossed over the road; Short and I followed her to No. 23, Easter-field-street, Stepney; she went up, opened the door, and Short claimed some calico which laid on a chair there, and between the mattress and bed I found some flannel, which she claimed, and, between the bed and sacking, some more flannel; and a tippet in the room; and in Gale's pocket, I found a purse - she said Clark gave it to her.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
ELIZABETH GREEN re-examined. It happened at a quarter-past one o'clock, for the watchman looked at his watch afterwards, and said it was half-past one - there was no day-light.
CLARK's Defence. I bought them in Petticoat-lane. Foster says he collared me; he never put his hand on me - I went quietly with him.
One witness gave Clark a good character.
CLARK - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 46.
GALE - NOT GUILTY.

1571. WILLIAM DUNCKLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of August, a clock-case, value 2 l.; six yards of cloth, value 6 s., and a shift, value 2 s., the goods of Joseph Grove, in his dwelling-house.
JOSEPH GROVE. I live in Richmond-street, St. Luke's. The prisoner is my son-in-law. I married his mother fifteen years ago - he is twenty years old, and is married himself - he was in the habit of coming to see his mother. On the 19th of August I went out, and on returning missed a clock-case, six yards of linen, and a shift; the linen was locked up in a trunk in the bed-room, and the clock-case was in the cupboard. I know nothing about the linen - I might not have seen that for a month. I found the clock-case at Worship-street on the Tuesday following - they were stolen on Friday.
JOHN TWEEDY. I am a constable. I was sent for on the 23d of August, and took the prisoner in charge. I found three duplicates in a tea-caddy in his room - one for the clock-case, the others for the linen and shift. He said his father gave him the clock-case to pawn. I took him to the office, and he was bailed for an assault. Grove was examined.
ELIZABETH CHOPPING. I was at my door in Richmond-street, and saw the prisoner coming out of Grove's house, with a bundle in a yellow handkerchief, on a Thursday or Friday in August, and when he returned he shewed me seventeen shillings - he said he had been to the pawnbroker's for his mother.
WILLIAM SHARP. I am servant to Messrs. Reeve and Co., pawnbrokers, Red-cross-street. This linen and shift were pawned by a woman in the name of Mary Groves - I am sure it was a woman.
NOT GUILTY.

Before Mr. Recorder.
1572. GEORGE GRAY was indicted for stealing, onthe 23d of August, a scarf, value 20 s.; a shawl, value 12 s.; a gown, value 12 s.; a shift, value 4 s., and nineteen shillings and a sixpence, the property of Elizabeth Mole, in the dwelling-house of William Watson .
ELIZABETH MOLE. I am a servant at the White Horse public-house, Buckingham-gate - William Watson keeps the house - the prisoner is my fellow-servant, and slept in the next garret to me - this property was in my box - every thing was safe about eleven o'clock on Friday morning, and the box locked, but the key was left in a small box on the table - on Tuesday I missed them - I never saw him in the room - master discharged him on the Friday following.
MARY ANN GOLDFINCH. I live at Watson's. After Mole missed her things, I saw the prisoner take a bundle out, but cannot say what was in it.
WILLIAM WATSON. I rent this house - the prisoner was my pot-boy; I questioned him about taking a bundle out; he said it belonged to Brown.
WILLIAM BROWN. I am a labourer; the prisoner had no bundle of mine.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-348]
London Cases, Second Jury.
Before Mr. Recorder.
1573. MICHAEL BURKE was indicted for a misdemeanour.
MESSRS. BOLLAND and LAW conducted the prosecution.
ROBERT ABLETON. I keep a chandler's-shop in Fenchurch-street. On the 22d of August, about twelve o'clock in the morning, the prisoner came in and asked for a two-penny loaf - my wife served him - I was called from the parlour - he tendered a half crown, which I told him was bad, and he must know it - he said he would give me a good one - I said No - I beat it up with a hammer, and threw it into the street - he went out to pick it up, and threw it down the grating - he then returned and said he would have a good one for it, which I refused.
THOMAS FARNELL. I keep the Horns public-house, Upper Thames-street . On the 22d of August, about ten or eleven in the morning, the prisoner came and had a glass of gin - he did not drink it, but took out half-a-crown, and laid it on the bar - I said it was bad - he said his eyes were very bad, and he could not see, but if I gave it him again he would give me another for it - I said No - I took out my knife, cut the letter T on it, and nailed it on the tap-room table - he wanted me to throw it down the kennel - Dady, the constable, called that evening, and I gave it to him.
SARAH CORNISH. I live at the Bull's Head public-house, Crescent, Jewin-street, with my aunt - I am eleven years old. On the 23d of August, between three and four o'clock, the prisoner called for a pint of beer, and gave me half-a-crown, which I rung on the table, and called my aunt, as I thought it bad.
JOHN DINES. I live in Charter-house-lane. I was in the bar when the prisoner came in and paid this girl half-a-crown - she immediately sounded it on the table. I told her to call her aunt, saying I did not think it good. I afterwards cut it with the shears, and gave it to the officer - it is base metal - he wanted to go into the yard, but I would not let him.
SARAH TAYLOR. I keep the Bull's Head public-house. I found the prisoner at the bar-door - the bad half crown was given to me - the prisoner said he would give me another, and did so; I said he should not have the other till Dines cut it.
RICHARD DADY. I am a constable. On the 22d of August Farnell gave me a half crown, which he had nailed to his counter.
WILLIAM PAYNE. I am street-keeper of Barbican. I was fetched to Taylor's, and took the prisoner. I found a good half crown upon him in a bag, and 2 s. 7 d. in copper; Dines gave me a cut half crown.
MR. JAMES FIELD. I am inspector of counterfeit coins; those half crowns are both bad, and from the same die, merely washed.
Prisoner's Defence. My eyes are very bad. I took them in the street for my oysters.
GUILTY.
Confined One Year, and to find Sureties.
[trialAccount t18250915-349]
1574. SAMUEL PHILLIPS was indicted for a like offence.
WILLIAM DAVIS. I am servant to Messrs. Wilson and Co. of King's Arms-yard, Coleman-street. On the 1st of August, the prisoner came and asked if I had any old clothes to sell; I agreed to sell him some for 1 l. 9 s. 6 d., he gave me 9 s. 6 d., and said, he would owe me 1 l. - he then gave me two half crowns, then took them back again, and said, he would bring me 1 l. - he returned in half an hour, and gave me eight half-crowns; I looked at them slightly, and he went away; I examined them again, they seemed to sound odd - I left three with Hicks, and went out, and overtook him in Sweeting's-alley; he was running slowly with clothes on his back, and three hats - I stopped him, and took him back; he said, "Are you going to get me into trouble for nothing - do not send for an officer;" I had told him, I would expose him, for it was scandalous; I gave him in charge with the eight half-crowns.
RICHARD HICKS. I am servant to Messrs. Wilson and Co. I examined these eight half-crowns; I saw that two were bad; Davis left three with me - he returned in about five minutes with the prisoner - we found in all that four of the half-crowns were bad.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. Did you put the three into your pocket? A. No. I did not examine them very minutely.
WILLIAM MACKAY. I am a cellarman. I saw the prisoner running out of King's Arms-yard, at a very rapid rate - I pointed out to Davis, which way he ran.
JOHN ELLIS. I am a constable, and took the prisoner in charge. Davis gave me eight half-crowns; the prisoner said, "I will defy you to find any bad ones;" I said, there was; he said, "You may find one, but not more;" I said four were bad; he said, he took them in change for a sovereign, of a stranger in the street, of one of our people.
Cross-examined. He meant Christian? A. Yes; I found a few half-pence in his pocket, and in his left hand, four half-crowns, and two shillings, all good.
MR. FIELD. Four of these are counterfeits, and of one die.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Confined Six Months, and to find security for his good behaviour for One Year.
[trialAccount t18250915-350]
1575. HARRIET WRIGH O'CONNELL and MARY ROACH, were indicted for a like offence.
HENRY BARKER. I keep the Holland's Coffee-house, Lower Thames-street . On the 28th of July, about nine o'clock, Roach came in with another woman for a quartern of gin, she paid a shilling for it - I put it by itself being busy; I did not think it good; I gave her 8 d. change - O'Connell came in directly with a cream jug, and had half-a-quartern of gin, and paid a shilling, which I put by itself, and followed them out; I found them waiting together, and brought them back - Boach had then got the cream jug - Mrs. East gave me one shilling - I gave all three to the officer.
HARRIET EAST. I am the wife of Samuel East. While Barker was looking for the prisoners, O'Connell came in and had a glass of peppermint, and paid me a bad shilling; I said, it was bad - she said, she did not know it; I said she could not have received it, without knowing that it was had - she said, "Give it me again," I refused - I gave it to Barker, when he came out; I detained her.
DANIEL JUSON. I took the prisoners in charge, and found four good shillings on Roach, and sixpence in copper.
SAMUEL EVANS. I am a carter. On the morning of the 28th of July, I saw the two prisoners and another woman at the corner of Beer-lane, Thames-street, near Barker's, they were looking at a shilling - I saw O'Connell go into Barker's with a milk pot in her hand, and afterwards saw her looking after one of the others - I assisted in securing the prisoners.
MR. FIELD. These three shillings are counterfeit, and of the same die, merely washed.
ROACH's Defence. I gave him 1 s., which he put into the till.
O'CONNELL's Defence. I was taken ill - she gave me 1 s. to get some peppermint.
O'CONNELL - GUILTY.
ROACH - GUILTY.
Confined One Year and find Surities.

NOT GUILTY.
