London Cases, Second Jury.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1577. JAMES HERBERT was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of July, a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of Henry Barnes, from his person.
HENRY BARNES. On the evening of the 10th of July, I was walking towards Temple-bar. I felt a person's hand in my pocket - I turned round, and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand, putting it into his bosom. I seized him, called the watchman, and gave charge of him - he threw it down as I seized him. I took it up, and gave it to the watchman - it was in my hand not ten minutes before - no other person was near.
Prisoner. Q. Was it not possible for it to have fallen from your pocket? A. It was possible.
WILLIAM COX. I heard an alarm, and took the prisoner - he denied the charge, but said another person did it who passed by.
THOMAS HODMAN. I was officer of the night. I received this handkerchief at the watch-house.
Prisoner's Defence. Mr. Barnes put the handkerchief into his pocket; I was on the same side of the way, and saw it drop - I took it up - he turned round and seized me.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-353]
1578. JAMES ROBINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, at St. Botolph without, Aldgate, eight jackets, value 8 l., the goods of Henry Early and Thomas Early, in the dwelling-house of the said Thomas Early.
THOMAS EARLY. I am in partnership with Henry Early, we live in the Minories, in the parish of St. Botolph, Aldgate; our counting-house is a part of my dwelling-house, and is at the bottom of the warehouse. On Saturday, the 20th of August, about a quarter past five o'clock, I was sitting in the counting-house - there was one young man left to attend the warehouse, while the rest were at tea; he came to the counting-house to speak to me, and while he was there, I saw a man enter the warehouse, and take eight cloth jackets, which I had just laid out with forty-four others - he made off with them - they cost us 1 l. each. I saw four of them again at the Mansion-house on the Monday following.
JOHN FORRESTER. I am an officer. I saw the prisoner on the 20th of August, about five or six o'clock in the evening, with another person, about three minutes walk from Mr. Early's shop. The prisoner had these four jackets tied up in a handkerchief - his companion got away. I asked the prisoner what he had got - he said some jackets, which were his property, and he had brought them from some vessel, which he named. I afterwards heard that Mr. Early had lost them.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 18.
[trialAccount t18250915-354]
1579. SAMUEL BARNETT, HENRY COPAS, and HARRY WARD, were indicted for stealing, on the 23d of July, at St. Martin, Ironmonger-lane, a table clock, value 25 l., the goods of Edward Watson, in his dwelling-house.
MR. ANDREWS conducted the prosecution.
HENRY LESTER. I live with Mr. Watson, watch and clock maker, in King-street, Cheapside. On the night of the 22d of July, I saw the house secure, and there was then in it a table chime clock, value twenty-five guineas. The housekeeper sleeps in the house. Joseph Lester is the person who opens the shop in the morning. I kept the key that night myself, and gave it to Joseph the next morning about half-past six o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Does Mr. Watson live in that house in the winter? A. Yes, it is his dwelling-house - he was not there that night; he sleeps in the country in the summer time.
JOSEPH LESTER. I went to the shop about a quarter before eight o'clock on the morning of Saturday, the 23d of July; I took down the shutters, and carried them into a little court that leads into Ironmonger-lane. While I was so doing, the prisoner Barnett came and asked me the way to the back of the Bank. I told him I did notknow what Bank he meant - I had then about three shutters to take down. When I took down the last shutter, he asked me where Threadneedle-street was - he then asked me for London-wall, then shewed me a note, and asked me again for several places, and then for Threadneedle-street again; he then went away, and turned to the right-hand, to the best of my recollection. I then went and got a broom to sweep the shop. When my brother came about nine o'clock, he missed the clock from off the counter. I had not noticed it that morning, but I had the night before - I then went up stairs and got my breakfast. At the time Barnett spoke to me, I saw a man at the corner of the shop, at the top of the passage, with a sailor's jacket on - it was neither of the prisoners; he was standing in the corner as I thought, to prevent my going up, while Barnett was engaging my attention. I have not seen that man since - I did not see any other man. Barnett was with me about three or four minutes.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you ever seen Barnett before? A. No; I saw him again when he was taken up, which was some weeks afterwards. I have not been talking this business over with any one. I am not certain which way he went, but think he turned to the right. I did not stand watching the man - I was putting up the shutters, and happened to turn my eye round and saw him. I was at the Mansion House, and I there said positively that it was Barnett who occupied my attention in the court. I have no doubt of him now. That court leads into Ironmonger-lane.
FREDERICK CALDER. I am in the service of Messrs. Faulke and Co. who live in the same house with the prosecutor - I was cleaning knives in the kitchen while Lester was taking down the shutters. I looked up, and saw two men standing on the iron grating - one of them was Copas; to the best of my recollection the other was dressed in blue, I believe, but I cannot say positively; the men were there a minute or two, and were looking in at the shop window - there were other persons standing there.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Where were you? A. I was in the kitchen standing at the ironing board which is about half a yard broad - they were looking in at the window, but they were not close, they stood on the board of the rail way Copas stood rather further back than the other - I saw him again at the Mansion House, in about a fortnight or three weeks afterwards.
Q. Did not they exclude the light from you? A. Yes, but I had seen him before loitering about.
Re-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. Had you such an opportunity of seeing him, that you can be positive he is the man? A. Yes, I am certain he is the man.
MR. LUCY IRWIN. I am the wife of Mr. Irwin, a warehouseman - we live in King-street, opposite to Mr. Watson's shop. A little before eight o'clock, on the morning of the 23d of July, I was looking out at the window for my husband, and saw four men about Mr. Watson's - I am certain the three prisoners were there - I saw them for about five minutes - I saw Barnett talking to the boy in the passage - I saw Ward and the other at the window, and Copas standing a little back from the house - I saw Ward go to the court, while the boy was taking down the shutters - when he had took down the shutters, he went into the shop - Ward immediately went into the shop close at his heels, and brought out the clock - Ward and the other one then went away, turning towards Bucklersbury - the other man had his handkerchief in his hand - I do not know what then became of the other two - they might have been there for all I know. I am quite sure of the three prisoners.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I suppose you gave an alarm immediately? A. No, I did not for some time, till I saw my husband - it did not strike me at the moment - I told my husband as soon as he came home - Barnett was at the corner of the court, and I was at the opposite side of the street - it might occupy about two or three minutes.
Q. Had the men their hats on? A. I think they had - I am positive they are the men.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. At your first examination did you speak to any of them positively? A. At my first examination I did not know where to find any of the parties, but when I knew where to look for them I cast my eye upon that man, and said "I am sure that is the man who was talking to the boy; and I think I have some recollection of the man in the middle."
Q. Upon your oath did you pretend to swear to Ward the first time? A. As soon as I saw him I did. I told Mr. Watson about the robbery afterwards, I suspected that they had stolen the clock, and I told my husband of it.
Q. Did you suspect any thing before your husband came home? A. Yes, I did, but I did not like to leave my house, having a good deal of property in it. The first time I saw Ward I spoke to his being one of them. I swore to Barnett when I knew where to look for him.
Q. Now do you mean to abide by the answer you gave me before; that you had so much property in your own house that you was afraid to give an alarm? A. I was so timid I was afraid to give an alarm.
Q. Did you sign your examination each day? A. No, only on the second - I was not asked to sign it the first time. I said at the first examination that Barnett was the man who detained the boy, and afterwards I swore to Ward.
Q. Had you seen him before? A. No, not till that morning.
Q. Were you not looking at four different persons? A. Yes.
Q. And do you mean to say that, before an individual or two were pointed out to you, that you said "these are the persons;" they were looking into the shop, were not their backs towards you? A. Yes.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. If you saw there were four person there, how is it you can not recollect the faces of more than you do? A. Because my attention was more directed to Barnett.
Q. How could you see the man when he was speaking to the boy - was his back to him and his face to you? A. No, he was looking about the street part of the time.
MR. ANDREWS. Q. How soon afterwards did your husband come home? A. In about ten minutes; he looked for Mr. Watson immediately.
Q. How many of the men were in custody at the Mansion House the first time? A. Only two; on the second examinationthe three prisoners were in custody - I then pointed out Ward.
COURT. Q. You say you saw Ward come out with the clock? A. Yes, he was close behind the boy when he went into the shop.
Q. What space of time might elapse between Ward's going in and his coming out? A. Not an instant scarcely.
Q. Did you say that you did not know or suspect that any robbery was being committed? A. Not, till my husband came home, and I told him. When I first went to the Mansion House I saw Barnett and Copas - they were standing before the Lord Mayor.
Q. Was there a great crowd? A. Yes.
Q. Did you pick them out from the crowd? A. Yes; the first examination was about a fortnight or three weeks before the second.
Q. Who directed your attention to that part of the room where the prisoner's were? A. No one pointed them out to me, but when they were brought before the Lord Mayor, I knew the two prisoners, and said, that was the man who was talking to the man in the passage, and Copas was the other - when I knew where to look for them, I knew them.
Q. Had you seen them before they were brought before the Lord Mayor? A. No, not before they were charged before the Lord Mayor - on the second examination, I saw Ward, and knew him.
Q. Were you examined on the first day? A. No, pointed out Barnett to Mr. Watson.
Q. Was any oath administered to you on the first day? A. Yes, he asked me, if there was any body there that I knew; I said, No; but afterwards, when I saw them standing before the Lord Mayor, I told Mr. Watson that they were the men - the Lord Mayor said almost directly, they had better be remanded.
JOSEPH LESTER re-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. Where had you to go when you had taken down the shutters to get your broom? A. Through the shop along a passage to the pump, which leads down stairs into the kitchen - I was not aware that any body followed me into the shop.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When Barnett was speaking to you, had he his face towards you? A. Yes; I was in the court, leading down to Ironmonger-lane - I could not see this lady at the window in the court, nor she me - but the man was standing near the end of the court.
MR. EDWARD WATSON. My house is in the parish of St. Martin's, Ironmonger-lane - the clock was on the counter, within two yards of the door - I was present at the first examination; the prisoners were remanded, because one of the parties was not taken - Mrs. Irwin pointed out Barnett and Copas.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had Mrs. Irwin any question proposed to her at the first examination? A. She was told to look round and see if there was any body there she knew - Barnett was then about three yards from her - she said, there was not any one she could particularize - they were standing before the Lord Mayor at the time.
MR. ANDREWS. Q. What lead her to discover the men afterwards? A. The second witness (Lester), being called, she recognized them, and said, "Oh! these are the men" - I several times attempted to get it heard before the Lord Mayor, but it was postponed to get the other persons.
SAMUEL KINGSBURY. I am a carpenter, and live in Black Lion-square. I know the prisoners. On the 23d of July, about eight o'clock in the morning, I was going down Bucklersbury, and saw Barnett and a man (not here now) together; the absent man had a clock, and a handkerchief tied over the face, which was carried towards him - they were close by the Hope eating-house; knowing them I went into the Green Man, public-house, and inquired if Watts, the officer, was there, and they went on to Size-lane; I could not get an officer. I came out again, and saw the other person with the clock; they went on towards the Mansion House; I ran on to the Mansion House, to try to get an officer, but I could not; I lost sight of them at the end of Bucklersbury - it was then half-past eight o'clock. I then went to the Mansion House again, and gave information to Drinkwater, the officer. On the Saturday following I was in Leadenhall-street, and saw Barnett, Ward, and the man not here, all coming towards the Mansion House; I ran to the Rose and Crown public-house for an officer - I got Mr. Brown and another, and we came running after them, as far as the Mansion House, but they had got away again; after this I heard of their being in custody - I went to the Mansion House, and saw Hesketh, the officer, with Barnett and Copas outside the house. I knew them to be the same men, and told Hesketh so.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Have you seen the clock since? A. No; it was brass inlaid.
MR. ANDREWS. Q. What part of it did the handkerchief cover? - A. Only over the top.
J - HESKETH. I apprehended Barnett and Copas on Saturday, the 6th of August, in Bishopsgate-street. I had had information of Mr. Watson's robbery, and took them to Bishopsgate watch-house, and afterwards to the Mansion House. Kingsbury came and said he knew them.
BARNETT'S Defence. I know nothing of the robbery.
COPAS' Defence. I was at work at the time - I am a bricklayer, and what that woman has sworn is as false as God Almighty is true. The Lord Mayor asked her on our first examination, and she said she knew neither of them.
WARD'S Defence. As I was going down Whitechapel-road Mr. Lea met me, and told me he wanted me for a crime that he knew I was innocent of. I went to Lambeth-street, and when I had been there about an hour and a half, Mr. Watson and that woman, who looked through the window came, and they neither of them said they knew me; and after the woman had been there half an hour she said she thought I was the person.
JURY to KINGSBURY. Q. Did you know the persons of the prisoners before that morning? A. Yes, perfectly well. Barnett and the one not in custody had the clock, but Copas had nothing; they were all four there, but on different sides of the way.
BARNETT - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
COPAS - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
WARD - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 19.
[trialAccount t18250915-355]
1581. JOHN ADEY and SAMUEL RICHARDSON were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of July, three clothes baskets, value 12 s., the goods of John Rider.
JOHN RIDER. I am a basket-maker. On the 27th of July, between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, I sent Jonathan Primatt to Bethlem Hospital, with three clothes baskets.
JONATHAN PRIMATT. I was eleven years of age last March. I am in Rider's employ. I was sent by him on the 27th of July, from Crispin-street, Spital-fields, to take three baskets to Bethlem Hospital; when I was near London-bridge I saw the prisoner Richardson, who asked me to go on an errand for him, and he would give me 2 d. - I said Yes; Adey was on the other side of the road, and Richardson gave him a penny to mind the baskets; I went on the errand, to carry a note to a gentleman - the gentleman said "It is not mine." I went back in about five minutes and the baskets were gone - I looked about and saw the officer with two men.
Prisoner RICHARDSON. I saw the witness, and asked him to go for a pennyworth of biscuits, and he picked up the note and took it to the house. Witness. No; he gave me the note.
DANIEL FORRESTER. On the 27th of July I saw the prisoners together in Cheapside; I saw Richardson go to a lad who had a bag on his head, and speak to him for some time; I then followed the prisoners toward London-bridge, and when they got near to Eastcheap, Adey sat down on the end of the curb - Richardson went up to the witness who put the baskets down; in a few minutes Adey took them up - he and Richardson went away together - Adey pitched them down in George-street, and I took them into custody. I came back the same way, and found the witness, who was crying - he had two halfpence and this note in his hand.
ADEY'S Defence. I never saw this prisoner before, but he asked me to carry the baskets for him.
ADEY - GUILTY. Aged 20.
RICHARDSON - GUILTY. Aged 12.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-356]
1582. ELIZABETH CLARK was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, a shift, value 6 s., the goods of Mary Ann Roper.
GEORGE THOMAS HANNINGTON. I am a calenderer, and live in Sea-coal-lane. On Saturday, the 27th of August, between four and five o'clock; the prisoner came to my house and inquired if a person was there who washed for Mr. Roper; she said she was sent for a shift for her, as Mrs. Roper was afraid to come out as it was wet; I gave it to her myself; the duplicate was sent to me afterwards, - I went to the pawnbroker's and got it.
MARY ANN ROPER. This is my property; I never gave the prisoner any authority to fetch it.
GEORGE GODFREY. I took up the prisoner - the shift was given me by Mr. Hannington.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to live in the house where the prosecutrix lives, and because I owed them a little money they turned me out.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-357]
1583. WILLIAM KING was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of Samuel Oviatt Goldney, from his person.
SAMUEL OVIATT GOLDNEY. I was on Ludgate-hill on the 13th of September, about eight o'clock in the evening - a person asked if I had lost any thing, and I missed my handkerchief; it was safe two minutes before - I saw the prisoner about one hundred yards off.
JAMES LEA. I am an officer. I was on Ludgate-hill on the 13th of September - I saw the prosecutor, and the prisoner and three others closed upon him, and followed him - I saw the prisoner, whom I knew before, lift up the gentleman's right-hand pocket, and take something out, but I could not see what - I crossed over and asked if he had lost any thing; he said Yes, his handkerchief; the prisoner had got on as far as the Belle Sauvage inn, where I took him - his companions had turned towards St. Paul's - one of the other boys were taken, but he was discharged by the Alderman.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going down Ludgate-hill, towards Bridge-street - I stopped for a minute by the Belle Sauvage, as it was wet, and the officer took me.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-358]
1584. JOHN STOCKS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, two sheets, value 8 s., and a blanket, value 7 s., the goods of William Onslow, in a lodging-room.
WILLIAM ONSLOW. I live in Lower Whitecross-street. The prisoner lodged in my house for about three weeks before the property was missed. Here are two sheets and a blanket, which were part of the furniture of his room.
MARY ONSLOW. I am the prosecutor's wife. I saw the prisoner with a bundle a short time before I went to the Magistrate - I think on Tuesday, the 13th of July. I followed him out, and saw the officer take the bundle from him. I saw it opened up stairs, and a blanket taken from it.
GEORGE LOCK. I am an officer. I took the prisoner - he had been stopped, and the bundle taken from him. - I found upon him three duplicates, a of blanket, a sheet, and another sheet.
JOHN KILLINGWORTH. I am a pawnbroker. The prisoner pawned two sheets and a blanket at my house.
The prisoner pleaded urgent distress in mitigation of his offence.
GUILTY. Aged 55.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18250915-359]
1585. ABRAHAM SUMMERFIELD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of June, fifteen pairs of boots, value 3 l. 15 s., the goods of George Miller, his master.
GEORGE MILLER. I keep a shoe shop in Skinner-street. These boots are my property; they have my initials on them, but are not made by myself; I had missed three or four dozen pairs at different times previous to the detection of the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was not he in your employ? A. Yes; he lived twice with me - the first time for about five years; the last time he came to me in great distress, being out of work, and I said I would give him 10 s. a week, and the moment he could suit himself better he could leave. He is not a good workman - he was to go on errends; the second time he worked as a clicker - I give my other clickers 24 s. or 25 s, a week.
CHARLES PAIN. I am a pawnbroker. These boots were pawned at our shop, by the prisoner, on the 27th of of June. Here are some others, but I did not take them in.
ABRAHAM GRIFFITH. I took the prisoner into custody on the 9th of July. I told him what for, and asked him for the duplicates - he said he had destroyed them. I went and searched his lodging; I found some duplicates upon his mother.
Prisoner. My friends went to Mr. Pain, and asked him if he could swear to these goods - he said No, only by my own confession.
CHARLES PAIN. I swear I did not say any such thing, nor did I hear the person say so to my recollection. I cannot swear to what passed in conversation with him.
(Witness for the Defence.)
WILLIAM WRIGHT. I live with my father in Fleet-lane. I went one morning with the prisoner's mother, and inquired of Mr. Pain what he had - he said he had the stock of boots, but he could not swear to any boots which the prisoner had pawned.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18250915-360]
1589. LETITIA PENNY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of September, a gown, value 10 s., the goods of Thomas Church.
ELEANOR CHURCH. I am the wife of Thomas Church; we live in Cockpit-court, Poppin's-court, Fleet-street. - On the 1st of September, I went out to Fleet-market - I left my daughter Mary, (who is eleven years of age) at home. I was gone about a quarter of an hour - when I returned I missed the gown belonging to my daughter Ann Church, which I had washed the day before, and hung on the drawers - at that moment the prisoner came down from the second floor - she did not lodge in the house, and had no right there; her father died in the court about three years ago. She had a bundle tucked under her arm, I followed her, and cried - Stop Thief! - she was taken in the Blue Posts, public-house. The bundle was opened, and it contained the gown.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMAS HARMAN. I heard the cry of Stop Thief! - and saw the prosecutrix pursuing the prisoner - she ran into the Blue Posts, public-house, and I took her with this gown.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18250915-361]
1590. HENRY GREENACRE WHALL was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of August, 306 lbs. of worsted value 40 l., the goods of William Barlow, his master.
MR. ALLEY conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM BARLOW. I am a general commission agent - my warehouse is in Bull's-head-court, Newgate-street - my house is in Godliman-street. I took the prisoner into my employ in May last, in consequence of having a good opinion of him. I was to give him 50 l. a-year, and to find a lodging in my warehouse for himself and family. I left town on the 12th of August, and left fifty bundles of worsted in my warehouse; when I had been absent a little time I received a letter, and returned to town. I went to my warehouse, but the prisoner was not there. I saw him on the afternoon of the 20th, and asked him what worsted had been sent out in my absence. He said none. I missed a considerable quantity. I went home, and the key of the warehouse was sent me the following evening about seven o'clock. I went next morning, and found the prisoner, his wife and child were gone away. There were several boxes left belonging to him - they were opened, and some papers found in his desk were open, and this paper was found in it - reads "Messrs, Block and Son, Dr. to - ." as if it were the beginning of an invoice. I afterwards saw Mr. Block, who shewed me this paper in the prisoner's hand-writing. I caused a search to be made, and found him on the Thursday evening following, in the Edward-road; he ran away - I set up a hue and cry, and he was taken - here is a sample of worsted which was found in his box.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. From what part of the kingdom did the prisoner come? A. From Birmingham - about two years ago - he had been in good circumstances - his wife is related to mine; I had perfect confidence in him - my suspicions were excited by the letter I had received - when I returned the prisoner was there - I did not use any harsh expressions to him - when he went he left his desk open, and his boxes locked - he had no business to deal for any other person. I cannot swear the worsted - all I can say is, that this is like some I had in my possession, and which other persons must have likewise. The property is my own, and is regularly consigned to me by invoice.
MR. ALLEY. Q. Has your worsted any particular number? A. Yes; this is No. 12 slack worsted - the bulk of my worsted is on the ground floor, to which the prisoner had access - when I returned there were twenty-six bundles missing.
(Invoice read)
Messrs. Block and Son, To Wm. Wright, Bradford.
(Sold by Son and Agent.)
25 bundles super worsted, 300 wt. at 2 s. 5 d. 36 l. 5 s. 0 d
Allowance for immediate cash, 1 d. per lb. 1 l. 5 s. 0 d
35 l. 0 s. 0 d.
Received of Block and Son, Thirty-five Pounds, for
WM. WRIGHT, Aug. 19, 1825.
SAMUEL BLOCK. I live in Newgate-street. A man like the prisoner came to me, and said he had a quantity of worsted from his father, at Bradford, to sell; I repulsed him at first; but he stated that he had served a person in the neighbourhood with some, and I gave him this order, for which I paid him ready money - he did not ask for the money - it was my own act and deed - he brought it in open day with two porters.
WILLIAM PORTER BADLEY. I am clerk to Mr. Block. The prisoner was the man who brought the worsted to my masters.
JAMES CASTLE. I live at No. 7, Bull's-head-court. About nine o'clock in the morning of the 17th or 18th of August, I saw a light cart, painted green, draw up to the door of No. 1, and sixteen or eighteen brown paper parcels were tossed into it, two at a time. I have seen thebundles that remain at the prosecutor's warehouse, and they seem exactly like those.
WILLIAM BARLOW re-examined. Q. Had the prisoner any authority to sell goods for you? A. Yes; if application was made to him, but he was not to make application. My price at that time was 2 s. 8 d. per lb. I had no other clerk nor porter.
Prisoner's Defence. On the return of Mr. Barlow from Halifax, he accused me of stealing worsted; I distinctly denied it, and said "The quantity of worsted is the same as you left it," which it certainly was - many words arose out of it, and he said, "I am desired by my employers to discharge you instantly - however painful to my feelings I must instantly discharge you;" "I am sorry, Sir," said I "but I dare say I can find other employment." I was taken five days afterwards, in the Edgware-road, where my wife was about to open a little shop; I took the key over to him myself; it was not a clandestine departure. - A good deal has been said about my boxes; when I was taken the prosecutor said, "Do you know any thing of any cutlery" - I said, "My boxes are all there, search my papers, my pocket-books, and every thing for proofs (if you can find them) of my infidelity" - there was not a lock on any box. A good deal has been said about a bit of worsted being found in my desk - these samples were about the warehouse, and I was in the habit of shewing them - it might have been in my pocket; because five individuals had applied for samples, which I had shewn them - it might have been in some of my apartments, but it was not in my boxes.
WILLIAM BARTON re-examined. Q. How long was it before you left that you saw these fifty bundles of worsted? A. On the morning I left (the 12th of August) - on my return I discharged the prisoner, on my own authority - I never said that any persons had directed me to discharge him. On the Thursday after he left he sent a note to me, with a cart, and desired the goods that were left might be given up to him; and the carter told me, with a great deal of reluctance, that he was not to go to a house, but to meet the prisoner in the Edgware-road, opposite the New-road, Paddington; I went there, and met him. I did not know of any house being taken.
One witness gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
1591. HENRY GREENACRE WHALL was again indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, twelve knives, value 3 s., and twelve forks, value 3 s., the goods of William Barlow, his master.
WILLIAM BARLOW. The prisoner was my servant. I have here a duplicate, dated the 15th of July, for some knives and forks, pawned at Mr. Reeve's; they were kept in my warehouse.
CHARLES PAYNE. I am shopman to Mr. Reeves, pawnbroker, of Snow-hill. I have some knives and forks, pawned by the prisoner, on the 15th of July.
Prisoner's Defence. The articles may be had in any cutler's-shop - I have been in the commission trade myself eighteen months, and they were my own, and what marks can the prosecutor identify them by?
WILLIAM BARLOW. By the manufacturer's name upon them, and by having the desserts and the carvers to match them - they are a common sort. I did not know of the prisoner dealing in them for himself.
JURY. Q. Have you the carvers and desserts here? - A. I have not. I have compared them - they are the same, and have the same maker's name on them.
NOT GUILTY.
1592. HENRY GREENACRE WHALL was again indicted for stealing, on the 21st of June, one hundred razors, value 30 s.; one hundred and twenty knives, value 30 s.; and one hundred and twenty forks, value 30 s.; the goods of William Barlow, his master.
WILLIAM BARLOW. The prisoner was my servant. I found a duplicate in his box, of some razors pawned at Mr. Reeves', Snow-hill. I got them from him, they were missing from my stock, and they have my writing on them.
CHARLES PAYNE. I did not take in these razors, but I have a parcel of knives, pawned on the 12th of August for 7 s. I have known the prisoner some time, and have taken in various articles of him.
WILLIAM BROOK. I live with Mr. Essex, the pawnbroker. I have two dozen of knives and forks pawned by the prisoner on the 5th and 6th of August.
WILLIAM BARLOW. Those knives and forks are missing from my stock - and are numbered with the same numbers as I have my goods marked; one of the dozens are not marked, but they are of the same description as what I have; they are dessert knives, and have the same maker's name. I have the remainder of the set at home.
WILLIAM BRADFORD. I live with Mr. Baily, a pawnbroker. I took in these knives and forks from the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. With regard to what the prosecutor says, of his having an exclusive mark, it is a mere burlesque; it is the mark by which London houses are regularly supplied. The pawnbroker's say, they cannot be sure I pawned them. I am well assured they cannot, because I did not.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-362]
1593. JAMES FEATLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, a handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of Paul Mullett, from his person.
PAUL MULLETT. On the 14th of August I was on Snow-hill. A young man accosted me, and said I had lost my handkerchief. I then missed it from my right hand coat pocket. I had had it in my hand about five minutes before. The person who told me of it said he had seen the prisoner take it from my pocket, and he had him in his hand.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did not the boy deny having taken it? A. Yes; I have a mark on it.
WILLIAM MORGAN. On the 14th of August I was on Snow-hill. I saw the prisoner there, but did not see him do any thing; there were four or five boys together, and I saw one of them take the handkerchief from Mr. Mullett's pocket, but I cannot swear that it was the prisoner; about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour afterwards, a gentleman came and gave a handkerchief into my hands; I did not know that it was the handkerchief taken from Mr. Mullett; I seized the prisoner, because I thought he was theone who took the handkerchief; I saw him near him; I seized the prisoner at the time it was drawn; I signed this deposition at the Mansion House (looking at it); I did swear that the prisoner was the person, but I was sick, and had not been in such a situation before; I did not know what I was about; but, my Lord, and Gentlemen of the Jury, I did wrong, and I am very sorry; I have seen the person since who did take it - it was not the prisoner; the reason I swore at the Mansion House that it was him, was I thought it was him, but now I cannot say, (from there being a crowd at the time of the robbery,) that it was him; I have since seen the person who I can swear committed the robbery, and I cannot now take upon me to swear against the prisoner.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-363]
1594. SARAH WATSON and JANE COLLIER were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of July, a shawl, value 18 s., the goods of Henry Keymer.
WILLIAM BILES. I am in the employ of Mr. Henry Keymer, linen-draper, of Bishopsgate-street. On the 19th of July, about six o'clock in the evening, the prisoners and another person came into the shop, and wished to look at a silk shawl from 20 s. to 25 s.; I shewed them several, and after they had sat some time the other person went out, saying the shop was so hot, she must go out; I then shewed the prisoners some more, till my attention was called to the door to a person who inquired the price of a print; while there I looked round, and saw Jane Collier standing to hide the other, and the shawls moving on the counter; I then went back to the prisoners, and Collier said I need not give myself any farther trouble; what they saw were very pretty, and she persuaded Watson to have one; she then asked what was the lowest price; I replied 25 s.; she said she had not come out with an intention of purchasing, and had not money enough, but left 2 s., and said she would call on Saturday; I put the shawl into a bit of paper, and while they were going from the back of the shop to the door, I missed the shawl now produced; I told my employer to detain Collier, and I pursued Watson; I took her, and under her petticoats I found the shawl.
Prisoner COLLIER. Q. Did I attempt to move out of the shop while you went after Watson? A. No.
JOHN GREEN. I am the officer, and took them up.
Prisoner WATSON. My fellow-prisoner is innocent - she did not know what I was doing.
Prisoner COLLIER. I did not know what was taking place.
COLLIER - NOT GUILTY.
WATSON - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-364]
1595. CHARLES PREFERMENT was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of July, a pair of cloth trousers, value 2 l., the goods of James Adams and Thomas Wight.
THOMAS WIGHT. I am a tailor, and live in Fenchurch-street. I am in partnership with James Adams. These trousers are our property. On Saturday, the 2d of July, they were in the window, hanging on the rail, before five o'clock; they were brought back to me, with the prisoner, some time after.
AMOS DEACON. I am a carpenter. On the 2d of July I was working in Fenchurch-street; I saw the prisoner and two others walking along, and one of his companions leaned into the prosecutor's shop; the prisoner stood close to him with a bag, which was doubled up, and they all stood looking about; I went over to the prisoner, and asked him what he had got in the bag; he said Nothing; I said "If you have got nothing the better for you, for you must go back with me to that gentleman's shop," and then I believe he said, some person gave them to him; I saw the bag opened, and the trousers taken from it.
WILLIAM MOORE. I have heard Deacon's evidence, and know it to be correct.
WILLIAM YOUNG. I am an officer - I took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. The witness says he saw me take and put the trousers into the bag, which is quite a falsity; the person who took them gave them to me, and asked me to carry them.
One witness gave the prisoner a good character, and engaged to employ him.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Fined 1 s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18250915-365]
1596. JOSEPH PATTERSON was indicted for embezzlement.
Robert Govan. I am a baker, and live in Redcross-street. The prisoner was in my employ for three years and two weeks - he had authority from me to receive money for the bread he delivered - I have a customer of the name of Robinson - on the 26th of July, he accounted to me, about five o'clock, for money to the amount of 4 s. 8 d., but did not account for any received of Miss Robinson - if he had received any, he ought to have accounted for it.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. From whom did he say he had received this 4 s. 8 d.? A. From four customers, whose names I do not recollect, but I can positively swear Miss Robinson was not one.
MARY ANN FISHER. I am an apprentice to Miss Robinson. I paid the prisoner 3 s. 11 d. for Mr. Govan, on the 26th of July; I gave him 4 s. and he gave me 1 d.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it was the 26th? A. I recollect it perfectly well - I went before the Magistrate on the 2d of August.
WILLIAM PAYNE. I went on the morning of the 28th of July, to Mr. Robert Govan's house. I took the prisoner, and found five sovereigns, and one shilling in his fob, and some copper in his pocket behind.
ROBERT GOVAN re-examined. Q. Had you ever asked the prisoner about this 3 s. 11 d.? A. Yes, on the 26th of July, about five o'clock, when I booked the bread; he said, No, she had not paid.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not part with him for a short time? A. Yes, and took him back again, at an advance of wages - when he went away, he did not leave 12 s. in my hand in case there should be a shilling or two deficient.
Prisoner's Defence. When I first left my master, I left money in his hands. I left him on a Wednesday - I called on the Friday, I went with the new man to the customers, and when I came back, my master asked me to come back, and he would allow me 3 s. a week more.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-366]
1597. JOSEPH PATTERSON was again indicted for the like offence.
ROBERT GOVAN. The prisoner was in my employ, and authorised to receive money - he never accounted to me for five sovereigns received of Miss Rivet, for Mr. Dallimore, of the Globe, in Fleet-street - I was present when the officer searched him on the 28th of July, and found five sovereigns in his coat, the officer asked him where he got the money, and he confessed, it was part of Mr. Dallimore's bill; he ought to have accounted to me every day, at five o'clock, for what money he received.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you make this charge before the Magistrates? A. No, because the person could not appear - I had found it out that day, but the Magistrates committed him for the 3 s. 11 d. - I stated this case, but the Magistrate said he could not hear it in the absence of the witness. I told the Magistrate we had found part of Mr. Dallimore's bill, but the whole of it was 6 l. 12 s. - the prisoner did not say he had lost part of this money - he said, he had lost a part of a bill of 13 l. 5 s., which he had received about two months before - he stated he had lost ten guineas, and paid me 4 s. a week.
JANE RIVET. I am in the employ of Mr. Dallimore. On the 27th of July, I paid the prisoner, on account of Mr. Govan, 6 l. 12 s. - I gave him six sovereigns and a guinea, he went out and got change, he signed the book and the bills.
WILLIAM PAYNE. I went to Mr. Govan's on the 28th of July; I searched the prisoner, and found five sovereigns in the button-hole of his jacket - he began to cry and said, it was part of Mr. Dallimore's bill - there was no guinea found on him.
ROBERT GOVAN re-examined. Q. Had you asked the prisoner on the 27th of July, if he had received Mr. Dallimore's bill? A. Yes, and he said No, he paid me 6 s. 8 d. that day, which was all - I went on the 28th to inquire and found it had been paid.
JANE RIVET. I am certain I paid him on the 27th.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18250915-367]
1598. JOHN SCHMIDT was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of August, a watch, value 30 s.; a seal, value 8 s.; a key, value 4 s., and a ribbon, value 1 d., the goods of John Clark, from his person.
JOHN CLARK. I am the mate of a vessel called The Times, which lay opposite the Custom-house, in the same tier with the Sarah and Mary - it is called the Yarmouth tier. When I went to bed on Saturday night, the 27th of July, I put my watch, seal, and key by the side of my bed. I awoke about six o'clock next morning, they were gone.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
DANIEL BLIGH. I am a Thames Police Surveyor. I was on duty about two o'clock in the morning of the 28th of August, and heard a call for the police from the Sarah and Mary. I went on board, and found the prisoner in custody of the crew. I took him, but only found a small piece of money on him - he had no hat on; I asked him where it was, one of the ship's company took up a hat from the deck, and said "Here is a hat;" the prisoner at first said it was his; but I looked into it, and found this watch - then he said it was not his. I put it on his head, and said "It is your's no doubt." I took him in the police-boat, and asked where he got the hat; he said he bought it on shore for 1 s. 6 d. I then said "Where did you get the watch?" he said I should hear about that next morning. I then called to the people on board the vessels in that tier, and said, if any person had lost a watch, they might apply to the office - the Times was one of the vessels. The prisoner then said, he had taken it from one of the vessels - he had only come out of prison on the Thursday, and he must go there again. I asked him how he found it in the dark; he said he heard the b - y thing tick, and took it through distress.
Prisoner's Defence. I was three months in the House of Correction. I applied to the Prussian Consul, and he said "You have been in confinement, and shall go there again;" So I was obliged to go on board the ship, and take this watch to support me.
GUILTY . Aged 24.
Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18250915-368]

THOMAS LEWIS. I am a jeweller. On the morning of the 5th of August, I lost these seals - they had no stones in them, and these in Court have.

1600. ISAAC MURRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, two hats, value 2 l. 2 s., the goods of John Okey, his master, in his dwelling-house.
JOHN OAKEY. I live in London-wall and am a hatter. The prisoner was in my employ on the 20th of August. These hats are my property - I know them by the stamp inside, and the mark on the paper.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Did you miss them yourself? A. No, I did not.
COURT. Q. After these hats were found I did you look to see if any were missing? A. Yes, and I missed one which was made for a particular customer; I did not see it after it was finished.
WILLIAM SHARP. I am an apprentice to Mr. Reeve, the pawnbroker. On the 22d of August, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, the prisoner came to our shop, and offered these two hats, which I have had in my care ever since. I saw the name of Oakey and Co. inside and asked how he got them; he said his father worked for Mr. Oakey, and desired him to pawn them - I kept the hats and sent him to fetch his father - but no person came.
JOHN ELLIS. I was sent for to take the prisoner.
NOT GUILTY.

1601. SAMUEL WRIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of September, a book, value 3 d., the goods of John Hand, from the person of Samuel Dare .
SAMUEL DARE. I am shopman to Mr. John Hand, a hatter, who lives in Cheapside. On the 3d of September, I was looking at a show, at Bartholomew Fair, and had this book in my pocket, and soon after I got home it was brought by the officer.
WILLIAM MARSON. I am a constable. I saw the prisoner go behind a person in a blue coat, about the height of the prosecutor, and stay there about a minute and a half,or two minutes - he then moved off quietly, and I saw him concealing something under his jacket - I took it from him, and found it was this book. I afterwards saw Hand's name in it.
Prisoner's Defence. The book happened to drop at my feet. I took it up and was stopped by the officer.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Whipped and Discharged.

1603. SARAH SANDERS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of September, a sheet, value 18 d.; two pillows, value 5 s.; two pillow-cases, value 6 d.; a candlestick, value 2 s., and a flat iron, value 1 s., the goods of Lewis Lloyd, in a lodging room.
ELIZABETH LLOYD. I am the wife of Lewis Lloyd; he lives in Christopher's-court, St. Martin's-le-grand. The prisoner took a room on the second floor in my house, a month ago last Friday; the articles stated were let to her; she left without notice, but was not indebted to me - her husband paid the rent. I let her the room for her husband, but he did not come till the evening - he came home every night. I received information, went into the room, and missed the articles.
WILLIAM STAPLES. I am a pawnbroker. I have two pillows pawned, I believe by the prisoner, on the 9th of September, in the name of Saunders. I cannot swear to her person.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-373]
1604. ELIZABETH GAINSFORD, MARY THOMPSON, HANNAH BROOKS, and MARY ANN PORTER, were indicted for that they, on the 15th of September, in and upon Thomas Henry Ingham, feloniously did make an assault, and put him in bodily fear and danger of his life, and from his person and against his will, a watch, value 2 l.; a seal, value 1 d.; a key, value 1 d.; a ribbon, value 1 d., and two shillings, and a penny, from his person, feloniously did steal.
THOMAS HENRY INGHAM. I am a scale-board cutter, and live in Ewer-street, Union-street, Borough. On the 15th of September, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, I was in Butler's-alley, Little Moorfields. I made a bit of a stop to ease myself, and three women went before me - they were Gainsford, Porter, and one who is not taken. I went on, and the one who is not here said "Come old fellow, are you not going to have sixpennyworth to night." I said "It is a pity but you had sixpennyworth at the tread-mill." I turned my head, and saw Thompson and Brooks coming after me; they immediately struck me, knocked my hat over my eyes, and knocked me down, and began to ill-use me in a most cruel manner; one of them said "Cut the b - 's ear off, and frisk him;" they took my watch from my pocket, and 2 s. 1 d. from my right-hand waistcoat pocket. I knocked down Porter, and the one who is not here, and got the watch from them; they ran away into Little Moorfields - I followed, and said, I should detain them till I got assistance to take them. The whole five then immediately beset me, and ill-used me again. After I had been knocked about in a most horrid manner, and a great many persons collected, an officer came named Herbert. I asked him to take them into custody; he said he had nothing to do with it, I must go about my business, or he would take me - they all burst out laughing. In about half an hour, I got the assistance of some friends, and found an officer named Payne; he took the girl who is not here, searched her, and let her go. I then went to Cripplegate watch-house, and the constable sent an officer with me. I found Gainsford and Thompson in Little Moorfields; we took them to the watch-house, went out again, and found Porter, who ran away, and got in between the two horses of a hackney coach; we took her, and the next evening, I took Brooks in Little Moorfields. I asked her where she was going - she said, "To take a walk." I said, if she would go with me to the corner of Moor-lane, I would talk about that. I then gave charge of her.
JURY. Q. Were you sober? A. Perfectly so.
JOSEPH HORTON. I am a constable. On the 15th of September I was at the watch-house. The prosecutor came and stated he had been robbed of his watch, and he had spoken to some officer, who would not assist him. I went with him to Little Moorfields, and we saw Gainsford. I told her I had a charge of felony against her; I told her to walk before me. I went down Little Moorfields, and there I took Thompson; I took them to the watch-house. When we were walking to Guildhall from the Compter, Gainsford and Brooks said they knew something about it. They said the prosecutor had asked a woman named Place to go down the alley for sixpence, which he gave her, but before she would go down she would spend it in gin, that as soon as she had drank the gin she would not comply with his wishes, and he struck her a violent blow, which caused her to cry Murder! and the others went up and assisted her; they admitted having struck him, but as to robbing him they denied it.
ANN WOOLMAN. I live in the Horse Ride, No. 2. I was coming down Moorfields, about half-past eight o'clock that evening, and saw Gainsford and the prosecutor quarrelling; he accused her of robbing him; she struck him; and a young woman not here pulled off her cap, and some of them held him against the wall, while the others beat him.
JOHN TURNER. I am an officer. The prosecutor came to the watch-house, and stated that he had been ill used; an officer was sent out with him, and the prisoners were taken and sent to the Compter.
GAINSFORD's Defence. I was going down Little Moorfields, and I met a young woman; she said she had been down a court with a young man, and would I have any thing to drink, and we went into a house; as we came out the prosecutor met us, and he struck her; they fought for some time, till fifty or sixty people were collected; she cried out Murder! and I went up to take her part.
THOMPSON's Defence. I was going along Little Moorfields and heard a noise, and saw the prosecutor running about like a madman; I think he was intoxicated; I went away; and as I was going along White-street, about an hour and a quarter afterwards, the officer tapped me on the shoulder.
BROOK's Defence. I went down Moorfields; he met me, and asked me to go with him, which I did down Tenter-street; he wanted me to go farther, but I refused to go; he then asked if I would have any thing to drink, I said Yes, and he went with me to the corner of Moor; lane; we staid there some time; he then said to the landlady, "Do you know where I can get an officer?" I said "For what?" and he said "You are one - your own words has done it." I staid at the corner of Moor-lane with him, and he gave a boy some halfpence to go for his brother and his wife. I stood there some time, and some persons said "Why don't you go, you are no prisoner." I then went to Mr. Barnes', and the officer came and took me.
PORTER'S Defence. I was coming down from Finsbury-square, and the prosecutor knocked me down, and cut my arm; I was two or three minutes there; I was not in Little Moorfields at all.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-374]

1605. JAMES TAYLOR was indicted for a libel.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18250915-375]
1606. FREDERICK WILSON was indicted for a misdemeanour.
(See Page 522.)
MR. LAWS conducted the prosecution.
ROBERT COLES. I live at Southampton, and have been a cabinet-maker. I saw in the newspaper an advertisement respecting advancing money, in consequence of which I sent a letter to the person from whom it came; in reply to that I received a printed letter, on the 16th of May, about three weeks after I had written. I then wrote another letter to John Cooper, which was the name subscribed to the letter I received. I did not send any thing in that letter; but I sent a third letter, with my note of hand for 350 l.
WILLIAM HENDERSON. I am clerk to the solicitor for this prosecution. I served a notice on the prisoner, and on his solicitor's clerk, to produce these letters.
MR. ADOLPHUS to Mr. COLES. Q. Were you present when all the prisoner's papers were seized at his lodgings? A. I was; I do not know whether they were all taken away; my original letters are in Court.
Re-examined. Q. Did you make any list of his papers? A. No; I do not know whether Mr. Cardale has many letters in his possession. I wrote a first letter, and had an answer to that which is here - I afterwards received these two, in consequence of which I sent something on the 29th of May: here is the second note I sent - this signature is my writing.
WILLIAM WATTS. I have two letters here which I brought from Mr. Cardale.
ROBERT COLES re-examined. Q. Is this letter your hand-writing? A. Yes; all these three letters are my hand-writing; I enclosed this note in one of these letters; I came to town and gave notice for the apprehension of the prisoner on the 15th of June.
SARAH HANDLEY. I live at No. 35, New Union-street, Moorfields, in the parish of St. Giles, Cripplegate, in the City. I knew the prisoner by the name of J. Cooper; he lodged with us in May last - he paid 5 s. down; I asked him for a reference, but he said if he paid the money down it would be sufficient, as he came from the country. A great many letters came to him in the name of "John Cooper, Esq.," and he opened them. I have frequently seen him write; these letters are his writing and this note, except the signature, "Robert Coles" - he was occupying our front room, on the first floor, when he was apprehended, and had been there about four months.
Cross-examined. Q. Who do you live with? A. My grandmother. He occupied our room as a counting-house - he did not sleep there; he had a clerk there; I cannot swear to any particular letter which I saw him open; he has frequently shown me the difference of the clerk's hand-writing and his. I do not know of any letter he received so as to specify them; these two letters are written by the prisoner - he differs in his hand-writing; some of his papers were taken at our house - whether these were or not I cannot tell. I often took in letters of the postman for him; they were taken to his room; and he has opened them before me.
JOHN BROWN. I am watch-house-keeper, and know the prisoner - I saw him write this letter while he was in custody at the watch-house; he asked me to get it delivered according to the address "Mr. Williams, No. 8, Lloyd's-row, Sadler's Wells." I sent for Bee an officer, and gave it to him.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you always take this manly course with letters? A. No; it is very rare that we have letters, but I generally see them. I made no mark on this letter, but it appears to be the same.
ROBERT COLES re-examined. Q. Was this letter produced to you when the prisoner was arrested? A. The watch-house keeper read it at Bunhill-row - I went to the place described; the landlady shewed us which was Mr. Williams's room. I found my own letters and bills there; the letters had been addressed to "No. 35, New Union-street."
JAMES HANCE. Q. Whose hand-writing are these papers? A. The prisoner's. I know his hand-writing well; I dare say I have seen him write a hundred times.
RNBERT COLES re-examined. Q. Did you, in consequence of this letter of the 11th of May, send any bill? A. Yes; a bill for 350 l., which I have not been able to find. I sent it in consequence of my belief that what was stated in this letter was correct. I received another letter, stating the form of that note was not correct, and I sent a second note on the 29th of May, for 350 l.
COURT. Q. Did you ever get it into your possession again till the prisoner was taken up? A. No.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Was not that letter sent back to you by the post? A. No; it never came to my hands.
MR. LAW. Q. How many letters of your's did you find? A. Two. I sent three or four. I sent the last letter to the same direction as the other. I sent it by post, and paid the postage. It was never transmitted to me again; in consequence of the first letter, I sent the first note, and in consequence of another letter, I sent a second note on the 29th of May; I never received one shilling for either of them, nor had a word of explanation until the prisoner was taken up. I had him apprehended, and saw at Lloyd's-row the list of bills, and my letters,which had been addressed to New Union-street, and a vast number of papers beside. I sent my last bill on the 29th of May - came to town the 14th of June - and took the prisoner on the 15th.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I have not worked at any trade for some years. I have some houses, and live upon my property. I made a tea-chest last year. I did keep a shop, but am in no way of business now. I employ myself in working sometimes. I have nothing to do with farming, but like many others, I wanted accommodation for a short time. I have eight or nine houses, some of them are freehold - they let for about 150 l. a year. I did apply for money for this note - but if I had applied to my friends I might have borrowed it. I was in London the latter end of April, but had not negotiated a loan then. I never applied to a person named Coster to raise money - nor ever had any thing to do with the raising of money till I received the letter from the prisoner - nor ever saw him until he was in custody. I received an annonymous letter, in consequence of which I came to town, and called on Mr. Whitty, in the Temple, and on Messrs. Spinks and Brundett. I went to Abchurch-lane, and called at Mr. Cardale's office, by Mr. Creed's direction. I never saw Mr. Creed until I came to town. I had seen a prospectus about a society for the protection of trade. I went to Mr. Creed, on Tuesday the 14th of June. I had received the letter on Tuesday the 12th. I took the prisoner in Finsbury-square. I sent my first note in a letter to him. I have never had any money demanded from me, by virtue of that note. I transmitted a letter containing my second, on the 20th of May, and came up to town on the 13th of June. I found the note at the prisoner's lodgings.
MR. LAW. Q. You received your anonymous letter and came to town, and saw Mr. Creed at Mr. Cardale's office, and went and discovered these things? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Did you go to Mr. Spinks, in consequence of the letter you received from London? A. Yes; I applied to a solicitor in Southampton, and he gave me a letter to Brundett and Spinks.
MR. LAW to JAMES HANCE. Q. Do you know the prisoner? A. I have known him more than two years - I know Mr. Coster - he and the prisoner were connected in bill transactions - I have known the prisoner by the name of Wilson and Oglivie - I was acquainted with his circumstances - he was not, during any part of my acquaintance with him, in a condition to discount bills for 300 l.; he was chiefly supported by what he received from Coster - I do not know what he paid him - I knew him residing in Love-lane, and carrying on business in Rood-lane, Fenchurch-street, in conjuction with Coster - there were bill transactions carried on there - I did not know of his residing in Union-street or Lloyd's-row.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you discontinued your acquaintance with him? A. Yes, since the month of February last; from that time I have known nothing more of him than his being here; when I speak of him as in connexion with Coster, I mean he was employed by Coster; since then he may have gone into business, which I have not known; he arrested me in the month of May, and he had been bail for me on some occasions before.
MR. LAW. Q. You had not parted upon bad terms? A. No, I had no quarrel with him.
(The letters sent to the prosecutor were here read.)
35, New Union-street, Moorfields, May 11, 1825.
SIR:
I have been informed, you require an advance of 500 l.; if this is correct, as I have an unlimited discount at a Scotch Bank, I will cash your acceptances, or promissory notes for the above, or any other sum; at three or six months, charging five per cent, interest, and 2 s. 6 d. for commission; and I will also undertake to renew the same as they become due, upon receiving ten days' notice, that I may have time by receiving others to provide for the first - you may rely upon honour and secrecy.
JOHN COOPER.
MR. ROBERT COLES.
May 24, 1825.
SIR;
Unless I am in possession of your promissory notes, at least ten days, I cannot hand you the proceeds, as I before stated. I discount with a country bank; you will not be running the slightest risk in sending it to me direct, as I must indorse it myself, before I cash it - I will enclose you a Bank of England post bill for the amount, after deducting interest and commission.
JOHN COOPER.
May 28, 1825.
SIR;
I received your letter, but the bill is of no use, in consequence of a mistake in the drawing of it; I now send you a copy, and be careful to make it correct; you must make it payable in London, where your banker's, at Southampton, make their country notes payable, and when it is due, by paying the money into the Southampton bank, they will send it to the London bankers to pay it.
JOHN COOPER.
Southampton, May 28, 1825.
L. 350
Three Months after date, I promise to pay to Mr. John Cooper or order, Three hundred and Fifty Pounds for value received.
Your name.
At the bankers in London whoever they are.
The letter written by the prisoner at the watch-house, also a list of bills found at his lodgings, were here read, for which see his former trial, page 522.
HARRIET PADDOCK. I have known the prisoner by the name of Frederick Wilson. He lodged with me at No. 25, John's-row, St. Luke's. He first rented a front room furnished, at five shillings and sixpence per week, and afterwards took the floor at eight shillings. He always slept there, and kept very good hours. I saw a gentleman twice who came to visit him, and gave his name Coster. I did not think he was in circumstances of affluence - when he first came, I took him to be a lawyer's clerk - he had no servant - he had a wife and two children - his circumstances seemed to improve while he was there - I have lent him a few shillings at times.
Cross-examined. Q. How long was he with you? A. He came a month before Christmas, and left on the 18th of April.
ROBERT COLES re-examined. Q. Will you look at this printed letter, and tell me whether you had writter a letter to which this was reply? A. Yes I had, which I addressed to "Stewart and Co.;" I think I did address a letter to Coster, in Staining-lane. I did not receive any letter from Coster, signed "Coster;" it was some considerable time afterwards before I received a letter from the prisoner - I think it was in April or March that I wrote to Stewart.
MR. ADOLPHUS addressed the Court and Jury on behalf of the defendant.
GUILTY.
Transported for Seven Years.
